<meta>This page continues the introductory arguments of Agostino Steuco (Augustinus Eugubinus), a 16th-century Vatican librarian and scholar. He is developing his thesis of "Perennial Philosophy"—the idea that a single, divine truth has existed since the beginning of time, though it became obscured over the centuries.</meta>

# AUGUSTINUS EUGUBINUS, BOOK I.

<margin>C</margin> ...began to exist among men, either by the indication of nature or by the aid of revelation. Anyone who wishes will understand that this truth was once obscure and held by only a few, but later became most radiant, burning throughout the whole world. Seeing these traces, these remnants of wisdom, we have believed them to be like rays of a greater light from ancient ages, and later of a supreme light; and thus, all things look toward one single truth.

---

### *On the three ways in which Wisdom has been known, but the later way is the most excellent.*

## CHAPTER II.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial letter "I" featuring a figure in a classical landscape with buildings and trees.</image-desc>

THEREFORE, to begin with Wisdom, and repeating the previous reasoning more strictly, we say that Wisdom—or Truth—coming to men or offering itself from heaven, has progressed through three stages. Among the first men, it was more abundant, generous, and made perfect by God’s own delivery. Later, as the years increased, it was scattered, cut apart, and overturned; affected by the great injuries of time and men, it reached posterity like a fable or a dream—vile and torn. From this source came the origin of the world, God as the Author, the first principles, chaos, water, living creatures, the <term>Craftsman Mind</term> <note>original: "Mens opifex"; a reference to the Platonic Demiurge or the Divine Intellect that shapes the universe</note>, the flood, the giants, the tower, and a more certain affinity of divine things with human things. These were very well known in ancient ages, but were either entirely ignored by posterity or were so obscure and uncertain that they were regarded as little more than fables. The Philosophers who arose partly rejected this wisdom as fabulous, and partly passed it down in their writings as they had received it—obscure and contaminated. They preferred the Refutation <note>The skeptical rejection of ancient traditions</note> which they persuaded themselves they had discovered by their own judgments. Thus, truth began to be held as fables, and the obscure or false began to be held as true and certain.

<margin>D</margin> There happened to be, therefore, three parts and kinds of Philosophy. The first was that whole which, from the origin of the world, spread itself among many nations as if by hand and by rumor succeeding rumor; if this had not been violated later, that light would have stood out as a great thing for human affairs. The second kind had its origin from the Philosophers who speculated on natures and causes; this departed very far from the first, as human judgments frequently slip and do not find the truth they seek. Finally, a third Philosophy shone forth, dispelling the darkness of the former by its own clarity, not confining itself to one place, but filling all things with its rays. This third way healed the errors of Philosophy and the blows and wounds that had been inflicted by time; it unwove the distorted judgments of the middle way. Sought by all, and in the judgment of all men—even the Philosophers themselves—it is necessary for the world and alone worthy of the name of Wisdom. Just as in every language and doctrine there are many frauds and fables, so here alone is the sweet and desired rest for souls, who previously wandered through the uncertain conjectures of men.

This, which was the true science of human things, has always shone with some light in the world, always demonstrating the same Prince of the world, the same origin, the same immortality of souls, the same punishments for faults and the same rewards, and the same expiation for sins—which will later be clear from these testimonies compared among themselves. Therefore, since we have said it is probable that there was a certain tradition of Wisdom from the founding of the world, having its beginning from God—who revealed His greatness, divinity, and sublime secrets to the first man, and taught him that there is a Son and a Father in God—it is clear from this that almost all nations have had in their writings some records of the Father, the Son, and the Spirit, of good and evil Angels, and of the creation of these and the world. Once the errors are wiped away, these things must be restored to their proper place. Likewise, many things which are in harmony with the truth, which men have discovered with reason as their guide, must be demonstrated. I shall not bring new things to your ears, but things that are by far the most ancient, so that it may appear that there is necessarily one single religion for the entire human race.

He argues that all ancient cultures preserve fragmented memories of Christian concepts like the Trinity and Creation.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introductory discussion on the "Perennial Philosophy," moving from general principles to the specific historical lineage of the doctrine of the Father and the Son.</meta>

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<margin>A</margin>
### *That the divinity and power of the Father and the Son were celebrated among the ancients.*

#### CHAPTER III.

Theology possesses no mystery more profound or greater—and there is no other secret that precedes it—than the divinity and understanding of the Father and the Son. This is the primary mystery in all knowledge of divine matters, which, due to its magnitude and necessity, was never obscure in any age. However, since the grace of contemplation and of knowing the truth has been granted not to all, but to very few in every age, and since the truth committed to books is subject to human accidents just like all other things, often collapsing or perishing for this reason, we must be content here with the testimonies of a few, and from these be led into a consideration of the rest. For every age and era, a few testimonies in each proof will suffice; since it is clear that truth does not dwell or linger with the multitude, but with the very few, being rejected and even scorned by the rest. And because the monuments of the most ancient ages neither exist, nor could have survived the accidents of time, it is fitting for us to judge that the knowledge of the earlier ages devolved upon subsequent ages like an inherited estate. Thus, later thinkers received from their ancestors whatever they spoke concerning great matters, until the entire succession is traced back to the first man. This reality will seem necessary to every prudent person; and Plotinus <note>A major Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 204–270 AD) who argued that his teachings were not new but interpretations of ancient wisdom.</note> also testified to this. Furthermore, as even Aristotle confesses, posterity received certain remnants of truth descending from antiquity, which we must believe came like torn limbs from a single vast body. We shall therefore use those testimonies which the passage of time allows us to use. But concerning the succession of doctrine, it shall be discussed wherever necessary.

<margin>B</margin>
The first of all, therefore, are the Chaldeans <note>Ancient inhabitants of Mesopotamia, often associated by Renaissance scholars with the earliest astronomical and magical wisdom.</note>, near whom was also Paradise; and the first men, who are said to have inhabited the eastern part of Paradise, held this region especially. This at least is certain: that the first men lived here after the flood. Rightly, therefore, did they boast of being the most ancient, though as often happens, they rather insolently added a most deceitful heap of years to their age. First, then, are the Chaldeans; from these came the Hebrews, the Egyptians, the Phoenicians; the Greeks were born most recently, and after them the Romans. It is established that all these—among whom certain more illustrious figures emerged—knew that God, an immense and immutable nature, had begotten another Mind from eternity. They knew this both through that Theology descending from the first man and through the oracles echoing throughout the whole world. They even called this Mind by the same names which the later and greater Theology <note>Referring to Christian Revelation/Theology.</note> used: sometimes the "Son of God," sometimes the "Voice of God," sometimes the "Word" <note>original: *Verbum* / *Logos*</note>, sometimes "Mind" and "Wisdom." They asserted that this Mind was the creator of all things. In reciting their sayings, I shall perform the duty of both a reviewer and an interpreter. All of this was known to the most ancient Theology of the Chaldeans, then of the Egyptians, and finally of the Greeks. The leaders of Chaldean Theology were those whom they called "Magi" in their native tongue—meaning wise men, or Philosophers and Priests. The undisputed leader and prince of Egyptian Theology was Hermes Trismegistus <note>A legendary Hellenistic figure associated with a syncretism of the Greek god Hermes and the Egyptian god Thoth, credited with ancient sacred writings.</note>. Among the ancients whose names reached posterity, Orpheus primarily governed and celebrated Greek Theology, followed by Homer and Hesiod, whom they called "Theologians"; among the philosophers were Thales, Pythagoras, Anaxagoras, Empedocles, Parmenides, and Plato. The most recent, though somewhat different from those above, were Aristotle and Theophrastus. It was therefore stated by the equal voice and consensus of the predecessors, handed down to posterity, and committed to writing, that there is one Source of things, the parent of a second Mind, the creator of the world. That the Parent himself begat a certain Mind from eternity, and that this Mind at one time created the world and all things individually...

<summary>This page argues that the Christian doctrine of the Trinity (specifically the Father and Son) was known to ancient civilizations through a "perennial" chain of wisdom originating with the first man. It lists the succession of this knowledge from the Chaldeans and Egyptians through the Greek philosophers like Plato and Orpheus.</summary>
<keywords>Perennial Philosophy, Trinity, Logos, Chaldeans, Hermes Trismegistus, Ancient Theology, succession of doctrine, Plato, Aristotle</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument that ancient pagan wisdom—specifically from the Chaldeans and Persians—contains hidden truths about the Christian Trinity. It follows the previous discussion on the antiquity of the Chaldean nation.</meta>

# AUGUSTINUS EUGUBINUS, BOOK I.

->Testimonies of the Sages will be present for every nation.<- <margin>C</margin> For it is certain that the testimony of the Sages stands for the entire nation; whatever the better and more learned men attest to is considered the consensus and sense of the whole people. Indeed, everyone defers to those more skilled. The Magi of the Persians and the Chaldeans (for the Chaldeans are prior to the Persians in both kingdom and origin, and therefore the learning of the Chaldeans was earlier) were the Sages and Priests of that nation, as was shown above, who wrote the deeds of kings and possessed certain secret sciences known only to themselves. It is well established that these men not only held great mastery over divine matters, but also understood the natures of demons and the methods of summoning them to such an extent that they forced them, once summoned, to perform wondrous portents in their sight. Hence Balaam <note>original: "Balaham"; the biblical prophet from Numbers 22-24</note>, the most celebrated of these Magi in his time, was believed to be able to harm the Hebrew people through sorceries and incantations, having been summoned by the King of the Arabs for this reason. Moreover, as Moses testifies, he lived by the river of the sons of Ammon—that is, the Euphrates—which makes it clear that he was an Aramean, or a Chaldean, namely one of these Magi. This was the reason why the Magic art (or rather, its vanity and mockery) was so powerful in those places in those ancient times: because the greatest knowledge of divine and natural things, and of the demons themselves, had been handed down by the first men who inhabited those regions. It is fitting that the ruin of these demons, and their prior and subsequent natures, were better known to those ancients. However, both this and all other greater knowledge has vanished with the antiquity of the ages.

Hence the Erythraean Sibyl <note>A legendary prophetess of the ancient world</note>, who is said to have been a Chaldean and the daughter of Berosus the Chaldean, and also Sambethe, published clear and open oracles—almost innumerable ones—concerning all divine matters, and truly concerning the Son of the Most High God. Similar and far more numerous truths lay hidden in the secrets of her nation and the mysteries of the Sages. There are twofold testimonies of Chaldean Theology concerning the Son of God and the Mind begotten from eternity: those of the Magi and those of the Sibyls, <margin>D</margin> most faithfully preserved in the writings of the Greeks; so that they may not only be seen in their original source, but are also brought forward by the most serious and ancient writers. Therefore, in the sayings of the Magi descending from Zoroaster (even if, as usually happens, they sometimes turned aside toward superstitions and the most vain worship of demons and elements), these words are found among others: *The Father perfected all things, and delivered them to the second Mind, whom the entire race of men calls the first.* <note>original Greek: "πάντα ἐξετέλεσε πατὴρ, καὶ νῷ παρέδωκε δευτέρῳ, ὃν πρῶτον κλήζεται πᾶν γένος ἀνθρώπων."</note> This is the Theology of the Magi.

Moreover, the most recent Theology has declared that the Creator of all things seen and those that escape the gaze of the eyes is the Father, creating these things through that intermediary Intelligence and Mind. This Mind is indeed "second" if you look toward the Father, but "first" if you look at the created things, visible and invisible, of which this Mind itself was the principle. For this reason, he says it is called "first" by all men—that is, the principle and source of all things. How great a mystery lies beneath this! For as Numenius <note>A Neopythagorean philosopher who saw parallels between Plato and Moses</note> says, and as we shall see later, the Father was not known to men, who only knew the one who had created the world. "But that one is the Son," he says, "and the second Mind, whom men did not know had a Father, and therefore they said that he was the first." Indeed, he himself who created the world, who was carried over the waters, stretching out the heavenly mass from above, who fashioned man in his own image, called him to the reward of immortality, and cast him out of Paradise after his transgression—he was the very same who later redeemed the world he had once created, and filled it with piety, justice, and the worship of God. Therefore, men, knowing only him, called him "the First," being entirely ignorant of the other Parent who preceded him in this rank of Begetter. Not even the Hebrews knew this, except for the wiser among them. Only this Son of God, therefore, do the Hebrews hold as known; and in their blindness they do not realize they are blaspheming the Redeemer of men, who had previously given the Law to Moses on the mountain. And that most celebrated and most holy name, **Jehovah**, almost always belongs to the Son of God.

<summary>The author argues that ancient Chaldean Magi and Sibyls possessed secret knowledge of the "Second Mind" or Son of God, whom they often mistook for the primary Creator. He identifies the Old Testament figure Jehovah specifically with the Son/Redeemer rather than the Father.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Perennial Philosophy," specifically focusing on how ancient civilizations—the Magi, Chaldeans, and Egyptians—understood the relationship between the Father (the Creator) and the Son (the Mind or Intellect).</meta>

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<margin>A</margin> This one was known, held by all to be the first: and the greatest and first Theology is entirely contained within these words of the Magi <note>The "Magi" refers to the ancient Zoroastrian priests of Persia, whom Renaissance thinkers believed possessed a primal, divine wisdom.</note>: for it encompasses the divinity and the procession by which the Father and Begetter precedes the Begotten. Indeed, they called him the "Prior Mind," and said that he gave created things to the "Second Mind" as if to a lord and prince. The generation is apparent, for he calls him Father: the creation of things is also encompassed through the Father, as the supreme Dictator, and through the Son as the one sent and destined for the creation of things. Then he says that the Father handed over created things to him: that is, to be ruled by the same authority with which he had created them. Finally, he shows that the Son is and is called the first of all minds. Therefore, the whole world knew, preached, and adored the Son of God: and he who in the most recent ages visibly declared himself to all was that very same one whom all once venerated obscurely, calling him Jove <note>Jupiter/Zeus</note>, the Creator and the Power ruling all things: known to philosophers, religions, and all ages, as will be better declared. And so that no expression of ineffable love might be left undone, in order to make man God, he himself made himself man. Moreover, the Theology of these Magi was held in great value among the ancients, and even Aristotle mentions them in his books on First Philosophy <note>Aristotle's *Metaphysics*.</note>.

### Proof of those things which have been brought forward, from the Theology of the Greeks and Hebrews.

### CHAPTER IV.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'V' depicting a seated figure within an architectural or landscape setting.</image-desc>

<margin>B</margin> Moreover, it becomes most clear that the Magi knew, believed, and preached these things concerning God the Father and Begetter, and the Intelligence that creates all things, because the later figures who practiced philosophy among the Greeks—following the Chaldeans and Egyptians as leaders and authors, to whom the Greeks betook themselves for the sake of learning—arranged the principles of things in much the same order, establishing the Good in the first place, and thereafter the Mind. For these same Chaldeans call God the Begetter not only Father, but also the Good, as appears in the same passage: a term which both Plato and all the others adopted. They also placed the Mind—in Greek, <term>nous</term> <note>original: "νοῦν"</note>—in the second place. I mean that Mind born from the Father from eternity as the first-born and only-begotten, and established by him as the principle of things. Anaxagoras <note>A Pre-Socratic Greek philosopher (c. 500–428 BC) famous for introducing the concept of *Nous* (Mind) as the ordering force of the universe.</note>, therefore, imitating the same from the more ancient ones, pronounced the principle of all things to be <term>nous</term>, that is, Mind. Lest you believe he spoke of any other than that of the Chaldeans, it appears most especially in that he judged this very mind to be the wisest, and called it—as Aristotle witnesses—<term>unmixed and impassive</term> <note>original: "ἀμιγῆ καὶ ἀπαθῆ". This means the Mind is pure and not affected by external physical changes.</note>, without entanglement, without the commission of any other thing: which is proper to God alone. Anaxagoras attributed all things to this same Mind, as did Moses among the Hebrews and Hermes <note>original: "Mercurius". Referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Egyptian sage.</note> among the Egyptians, preaching that it approached and modified Chaos, arranging and creating things from it, and when all things were motionless and at rest, it gave them motion and order. Nor did Aristotle interpret his mind kindly or benevolently when he claimed that Anaxagoras established an eternal Chaos that preceded the creation of the Mind, such that an infinite time intervened from Chaos to creation. It is not to be believed that Anaxagoras, whose sentiments you find breathing ancient Theology, felt this: nor if he says that Chaos preceded, did he thereby assert the eternity of that confusion: since both Moses and Hesiod <note>An early Greek poet.</note> preach that previously the earth was empty and invisible, and thereafter the earth, sky, and light appeared: yet no one will think they placed an eternal time between those beginnings and created things. Anaxagoras therefore learned this creation from the ancients. Indeed, even Euripides, whom they say was his disciple, agrees with his teacher, as will be demonstrated later. Thus he learned not only that the world was created, but also the method of creating: how the Mind formed the shapeless and immobile Chaos, and gave it motion. <insert>He shall instill the movement of the leadership of all things.</insert> <note>original: "τῶν πάντων ἡγεμονίαν κίνησιν ἐμποιήσει"</note>

<summary>This page argues that ancient Greek philosophers like Anaxagoras derived their concept of a "Universal Mind" (Nous) from older Chaldean, Egyptian, and Hebrew sources, aligning with the Christian concept of the Son of God. It asserts that the "Perennial Philosophy" has always recognized a secondary divine intellect that brings order to primordial chaos.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's project of reconciling ancient philosophy with Christian theology, specifically focusing on the "Mind" as the creative force of the universe.</meta>

# AUGUSTINUS EUGUBINUS, BOOK I.

<margin>C</margin> Aristotle says that Anaxagoras <note>A Pre-Socratic Greek philosopher (c. 500–428 BC) famous for introducing the concept of "Mind" into philosophy.</note> claimed that while all things were once at rest, **Mind** <note>original: "τὸν νοῦν καὶ διακρῖναι" — to distinguish and set the mind in motion</note> gave them motion and separated them from one another. Moses sings these same truths concerning God, asserting that at first there was darkness, and water was mingled with the earth; but God, having scattered the darkness, brought in light and separated the waters from the earth. If you wish to learn who this was, learn from Anaxagoras: it is namely <term>Nous</term> <note>Greek: νοῦς</note>, the **Mind**, **Intelligence**, **Wisdom**, or **Spirit**. To understand what this Mind is, consult the Chaldeans <note>Ancient Babylonian sages often associated with mystical oracles.</note> and Hermes Trismegistus <note>original: "Mercurium" — the legendary Hellenistic figure credited with the Hermetic writings.</note>, who call Him by the specific name "Son of God" and "Creator of the World," and identify Him as the Mind and Intelligence itself. Above Him they placed <term>The Good</term> <note>Greek: τὸ ἀγαθὸν</note>, which is Goodness itself; and in this way, they explained the principles of all things. The Platonists also—whose books are filled with the most ancient oracles and sayings of the Magi and the Egyptians—place "The Good" first and the "Mind" second in the same order. This is the very Mind which the Chaldean said was held as the first principle by all mortals. Therefore, Aristotle, hearing that the ancients attributed the sovereignty and creation of things to the Mind, pronounced with a spirited voice that it is <term>the most divine of all things that appear</term> <note>Greek: τῶν φαινομένων θειότατον</note>, the most divine of all that can be thought; thus he subscribed to the ancient theology which calls the Mind the beginning. Discussing the divinity of this Mind elsewhere, he asserted its infinite pleasure consists in understanding itself and in the love of its own divinity. For although he did not know that this Mind had a Father, he spoke the truest things. For the Mind, by understanding the Father, also understands itself. Aristotle grants it this eternal, pure, and incorruptible pleasure because it contemplates and recognizes itself (for it has nothing greater than itself). Indeed, he seems to have uttered this voice by heavenly rather than human instinct. But hear also Plato: when he heard that the creation of things was attributed to the Mind by Anaxagoras, he said he was greatly satisfied with it, rejecting the opinions of all others. He believes that no probable reason can be given for any thing unless you summon the Mind to all things; with Mind established as the ruler of things, you can affirm that nothing is not beautifully placed and discovered. <margin>D</margin> For if the highest Wisdom, Intelligence, and Mind made it, then surely it made things better and more choice. If the Mind created them, certainly they were created such that nothing better could be made. Therefore, all things are excellent. And because Moses said something similar—*And they were very good*—Plato says in the *Phaedo*: <quote>Since, then, the Mind is the one that puts things in order and is the cause of all, I was delighted with this cause, and it seemed to me in a certain way to be right that the Mind is the cause of all.</quote> <note>original Greek: "ὡς ἄρα νοῦς ἐστὶ ὁ διακοσμῶν τε καὶ πάντων αἴτιος..."</note> Hearing from the book of Anaxagoras, he says, that the Mind is the builder of the world and the cause of all things, I was wonderfully gladdened by this; and for some reason it seemed to me to be best that the Mind is the cause of all, giving Intelligence the sovereignty over all things. They asserted these things not so much because they were subscribing to Anaxagoras's opinion (though it pleased them wonderfully), but because they were confirming what they found among the Chaldeans and Egyptians, incorporating those findings into their own sayings. Indeed, Macrobius <note>A 5th-century Roman provincial and Neoplatonist writer.</note>, setting forth the opinion of the Platonists regarding principles, established these two first principles: <term>The Good</term> and <term>The Mind</term> <note>Greek: τὸ ἀγαθὸν, τὸν νοῦν</note>, or Goodness and Intelligence. Of these, the first is the name of the Father, the second of the Son. For the Son said of the Father: *No one is good, except God alone.* And of himself: *I am the beginning, who also speaks to you.* From these things it is clear that the earlier oracle of the Magi is a true testimony of that ancient nation; there should be no suspicion of falsehood here, as if these things were fabricated by later men. Furthermore, there remains the testimony of the ancient Theology of the Hebrews, which, when I reflect upon it, I am accustomed to marvel at greatly. For from that testimony of Chaldean Theology, it is proved that the ancient Theology of the Greeks perceived that the world was created through the Son of God. For the Chaldean calls the Son of God "Mind"; the Greek confesses the "Mind" as the creator and adorner of things; and the Hebrew Theology—calling that which the Chaldean and Greek call "Mind" and the Egyptian calls <term>Logos</term> <note>Greek: λόγον — Word, Reason</note>, "Word," or "Reason," by the names "Spirit" and "Intelligence"—likewise declares it to be the creator of the world. And he whom Anaxagoras calls "Mind" and says ordered the Chaos, the Hebrew calls "Spirit" and says worked upon the waters and the Chaos. Now that was the

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<margin>A</margin> operation, because it established the world—that is, the heavens—as the principle of all other things. The reasoning behind these names is the same among all peoples. It is called "Intelligence" by the Chaldean and the Greek, and "Reason" by the Egyptian (as I shall demonstrate). If you compare them, these are the same as "Spirit": for Spirit is Wisdom, it is Intelligence; to this, the creation of all things is attributed by everyone. I also show by another line of reasoning that the Hebrews are in agreement with the Greeks regarding this Principle. Indeed, that very name <term>νους</term> <note>original: "nous" - Greek for Mind or Intellect</note>, Nun, is Hebrew. It often happens that names transferred from one language to another are increased or diminished in their syllables, or certain letters are altered. For example, what was *Rapa* in Hebrew—meaning "to heal"—the Greeks (who received the ability to be born and to speak from the ancestors of the Hebrews) added a syllable and called it *therapeno* <note>original: "therapeuo" - to treat or heal</note>. By the same example, but in reverse, from the Hebrew *Ba nun*—meaning "to understand" or "to be wise"—the Greeks derived *Nun*, meaning "Mind" or "Intelligence." With this explained, it is clear that Hebrew Theology confesses the world was created through the same thing as the Theology of the Greeks. The Hebrew Prophet, singing of divine works, says: "Who made the heavens in intellect" <note>Psalm 136:5</note>. In Hebrew this is *Thebuna*, in which the first syllable, according to the Hebrew idiom, is an addition; and it is from that word that the Greek language derived its <term>νους</term>, Nun. The Hebrew Prophet confesses that God created the heavens through *Nun*, through Intelligence, through Mind. It appears from both the meaning and the sound that this is the same thing celebrated by the Greek Theologian and called the "Principle of things," and that which Hebrew Theology has confessed. And the Hebrew specifically named the Heavens as the most excellent thing in all that creation. Thus it is evident that the Chaldeans, Greeks, and Hebrews knew and preached the Son of God, and praised Him as the Principle of all things. This is proven by repeating these arguments: Chaldean Theology confesses a Father and a fertile, creative Mind.
<margin>B</margin> This Mind is praised, looked up to, and venerated by Anaxagoras, Plato, Aristotle, and the entire family of Platonists. All these, therefore, celebrated the offspring of God, though they were unaware of it. For they all lean upon that Chaldean sentiment. Since, however, Hebrew, Chaldean, and Greek Theology speak the same thing, and these latter speak of and preach the Son of God, it follows necessarily that Hebrew Theology also speaks of the Son of God. Behold, those things which those ancient ages spoke of in secret and as if with a veiled face, the most recent ages have preached, known, adored, and venerated with a clear and illustrious voice.

->*Concerning the same Mind, Offspring, and Divine Wisdom from Plato: the queen and moderator of all things, by the consensus of all the ancestors. And that Aristotle praised it, looked up to it, and wondered at those who had spoken of it.*<-

### CHAPTER V.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'M' featuring floral and foliage scrollwork within a square frame.</image-desc>MANY things regarding the "Mind" in Plato are also worthy of admiration concerning the highest mysteries of our Theology. For this Mind—which others established as the second Principle and following the supreme Good—Plato extolled with wonderful praises, not only in the *Phaedo* but in many other places. He gives it primacy, the invention of all things, and dominion over heaven and earth, calling it the creatress, the empress, the queen, and wisdom. You will find nothing regarding it in Plato that is not also held in the Sacred Scriptures. From every description in his work, as well as from other theologians, it is clear that this is the eternal offspring of God, the almighty wisdom of His Father. In the *Cratylus*, he says: *“And if Justice is what Anaxagoras says it is, namely Mind; for he says that Mind, being self-ruling and mixed with nothing, sets all things in order, passing through all things.”* <note>Translated from the Greek quote provided in the text</note> Cratylus asserts that Justice is what Anaxagoras called Mind; since it is the empress, queen, and prince, mixed with nothing, he relates that it creates all things, passing through all things—so says Plato. You hear of that Mind which the Chaldean mentioned, now called the empress,

# AUGUSTINUS EUGUBINUS, BOOK I.
<margin>C</margin>
...the directress, the creator of all things, Law itself, and Justice itself. This is also what Laërtius <note>Diogenes Laërtius, biographer of the Greek philosophers</note> claims Plato felt: *he understood justice to be under the law* <note>original Greek: τὴν δικαιοσύνην ὑπὸ νόμον ὑπελάμβανεν</note>; that Plato suspected Justice to be the Law of God. And as our Marcus <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero</note> says even more clearly: "It arose at the same time," he says, "with the Divine Mind; wherefore the true law, the primary power fit for commanding and forbidding, is the right reason of the highest Jove." What could Plato have said more divinely concerning the Divine Mind? That justice, moderation, and the tempering of all things belong to it; that it is the disposer, the adorner, the creator, present everywhere like a law, like an everlasting order, arranging all things. For all power has been given to it in heaven and on earth, and it shall be his to judge all flesh—he is our God, the Just One. In the same book, Socrates asks Hermogenes: *What then of the nature of all other things? Do you not attribute to Mind or Soul the power of ordering and possessing them?* <note>original Greek: τί δὲ καὶ τὴν τῶν ἄλλων ἁπάντων φύσιν, ἢ πίστινας ἀναφέρεις νοῦν ἢ ψυχὴν τὴν διακοσμοῦσαν καὶ ἔχουσαν</note> "What," he says, "do you not believe Anaxagoras, that Mind and Soul are what adorn and contain the nature of all things?" Here Plato again confesses that the Mind adorns and sustains all things, as you see; it is the inventor of all order and all nature, which is order and equity; the directress; and that the fixed orders of things and their fixed positions are the work of the Mind, which organized Chaos, brought forth forms, and applied order and temperament everywhere. And, as Aristotle reported, Anaxagoras confessed it to be *mixed with nothing* <note>original Greek: ἐδενὶ μεμιγμένην</note>, mingled with no thing—namely, not corporeal, but purely divine, free from all contagion of the body. The Holy Scriptures speak of nothing else regarding that creator of the world. Among them, this Mind is the *self-father* <note>original Greek: αὐτοπάτωρ</note>, the Female Ruler, the omnipotent Queen. It is *going through all things* <note>original Greek: διὰ πάντων ἰὼν</note>, stretched through all, present everywhere, fitting the whole of nature, sustaining it, moving the heavens, and applying perpetual orders to the lands. In the *Philebus*, however, he says far greater things: *All the wise agree, extolling themselves, that Mind is King for us of heaven and earth, and perhaps they speak well* <note>original Greek: πάντες συμφωνοῦσιν οἱ σοφοὶ ἑαυτοὺς μὲν σεμνύνοντες, ὡς νοῦς ἐστὶ Βασιλεὺς ἡμῖν, οὐρανοῦ καὶ γῆς καὶ ἴσως εὖ λέγουσι</note>. "All the wise agree," he says, "boasting in this, that Mind is the queen for us of heaven and earth; and perhaps they speak rightly."
<margin>D</margin>
Plato could have spoken nothing more magnificent, nor more venerable to our high Theology. He is the King of heaven and earth in his writings, who in Saint Paul is the "King of ages, immortal, invisible." In this, Plato confesses that all the wise agree: that this is the king of heaven and earth. Who then are these wise men? Not Anaxagoras alone, evidently, but also the Chaldeans, as it appeared, Hermes Trismegistus, Orpheus, and many others whom Plato had read. But before all was Moses, who called this—which the Greeks in their tongue called Intelligence or Mind—by his own name: the Spirit, who was moved over the waters; that is, he was fashioning the heaven and establishing the celestial adornment, which is properly called the *cosmos* <note>original Greek: κόσμος, meaning order or world</note>. Therefore, the Mind is called *the adorner* <note>original Greek: διακοσμοῦσα</note>. Listen then and look up to what Plato says concerning the consensus of the wise, and weigh who those wise men are whom he says glory in this, as if they speak of a great and divine matter. For you shall see even Aristotle later confessing the same Mind to be the prince of the world. Plato continues in the *Philebus*: "Shall we say, O Protarchus, that a certain irrational and reckless force governs the universe and this which we call the totality, or on the contrary, *as those before us used to say, that a certain Mind and wonderful Wisdom, arranging them, governs and moderates?*" <note>original Greek: καθάπερ οἱ πρόσθεν ἡμῶν ἔλεγον, νοῦν καὶ φρόνησίν τινα θαυμαστὴν συντάττουσαν κυβερνᾷν</note> To this Protarchus responds that the former position does not even seem holy: "*But to say that Mind orders all these things is worthy of the sight of the world, the sun, the moon, the stars, and the whole revolution; and I would never say or think otherwise concerning them*" <note>original Greek: τὸ δὲ νοῦν πάντα διακοσμεῖν αὐτὰ φάναι, καὶ τῆς ὄψεως τοῦ κόσμου καὶ ἡλίου, καὶ σελήνης, καὶ ἄστρων, καὶ πάσης τῆς περιφορᾶς ἄξιον, καὶ οὐκ ἄλλως, ἐγὼ γ' ἄν ποτε περὶ αὐτῶν εἴποιμι ἢ δόξῃ δοξάζοιμι</note>. You see here what the ancestors had handed down; you see the ancient Theology concerning the Mind, which not only created and fitted things, as Anaxagoras said, but orders, composes, and rules all things for all time. To this Mind he attributes the created celestial order of the sun, moon, and stars.

<margin>A</margin> of the stars. For indeed, both Job and Moses <note>The author links the Hebrew Bible with Greek thought to show they share the same divine inspiration.</note> revealed this: that this very Mind and Spirit adorned the heavens, created light, kindled the moon and stars, and set them in their turnings and revolutions to create day and night and to shine upon the earth. Note also that the ancestors asserted these things—namely the Chaldeans, the Egyptians, the most ancient of the Greeks, Orpheus, Hesiod, and Pythagoras, Timaeus, and many others whom Plato himself read. From this, the antiquity of this ancient Philosophy becomes clear. Continuing, Plato says: "We know that in the universe there is something infinite, and a certain limit, over which presides a cause <note>original Greek: *κοσμῶσά τε καὶ συντάττουσα ἐνιαυτούς τε, καὶ ὥρας, καὶ μῆνας, σοφία καὶ νοῦς λεγομένη δικαιότατα ἄν.*</note> adorning and coordinating the years, the seasons, and the months, which may most justly be called Wisdom and Mind." O most holy mysteries sent from heaven! You perceive that Wisdom, which the Hebrews also claim was the creator of the world, and of which Solomon writes: *The Lord by wisdom founded the earth; by understanding he established the heavens.* <note>Proverbs 3:19</note> And concerning which the same Solomon says, in a manner entirely similar to Plato: <note>original Greek: *ἡ σοφία πλησιέστατα τῆς τὰ πάντα ἐργαζομένης, τίς αὐτῆς τῶν ὄντων μᾶλλον ἐστὶ τεχνίτης, ἢ ποιήσας τὰ πάντα τῷ λόγω σὺ καὶ σοφία σου κατασκευάσας τὸν ἄνθρωπον:*</note> "What is richer than wisdom, which works all things? Who is a greater artisan of reality than she? You who created all things by your word (or by your reason) and by your wisdom fashioned mankind?" This is also that Wisdom which, among the <term>Cabalists</term> <note>Jewish mystical theologians</note> of the Hebrews, holds the second place after the "Crown of the Most High," obtaining the second degree of divinity, to whom they give sovereignty over all things. But hear Plato himself narrating and asserting these same things. For according to the testimony of Laërtius, who writes that Plato established two principles of the created world, Matter and God, <note>original Greek: *ὃν καὶ νοῦν προσαγορεύει καὶ αἴτιον*</note> whom he calls both Mind and the Author or Cause. Plato declares him the Creator and Commander of the world, and hails that same Mind, which he calls both God and <margin>B</margin> Cause. It has become clear, therefore, what this Mind is: namely, a Divine offspring, that is, the Daughter of God (as the Chaldean used to say), which in our Theology is called Wisdom, just as it is in Plato. Likewise, learn who this <term>Cause</term> <note>original Greek: *αἴτιος* (aitios)</note> is, to whom Plato gives authority over the world (according to Laërtius), from the second epistle of Plato. In it, you will see that this "Cause" of the world has a Father. For he commands three friends that, in their friendship, they should swear by the lord, prince, and cause of all things, and by the father of that cause. If, therefore, for Plato himself the Mind is the Cause (according to Laërtius), and the Cause has a father, then the Mind has a Father. Concerning which, more later. Thus, in Plato, the Mind, Wisdom, and Cause is the Son of God, because this cause has a father—a wonderful assertion of that Chaldean oracle. This same Mind, which was the cause of the world, Aristotle also recognized and embraced in many places, thinking of it honorably. Among these is a wonderful testimony in his first book on Philosophy. For seeking the principles of things which he found among his predecessors, and having encountered many whom he did not approve of in their assignment of principles, he finally said: <note>original Greek: *νοῦν δέ τις εἰπὼν εἶναι καθάπερ ἐν τοῖς ζῴοις καὶ ἐν τῆ φύσει τὴν αἰτίαν καὶ τοῦ κόσμου καὶ τῆς τάξεως ἁπάσης, οἷον νήφων ἐφάνη παρ' εἰκῆ λέγοντας τοὺς πρότερον...*</note> "The man who said that Mind exists in nature—just as it does in animals—as the Cause of the world and of all order, certainly appeared as a sober man compared to those who spoke recklessly before him. Therefore, those who held this view not only established this cause well and fittingly as the principle of things, but they also brought forward such a Cause from which motion existed or exists in things." In these matters, although Aristotle speaks obscurely, he feels those things which Plato had taught before: that just as the Mind had discovered all the orders of things and the great beauty we behold with our eyes, so it gives guidance to things in the present, being the bond by which the universe...

...that all things are sustained, the law of all matters, the pilot of the world: and that there is a moderator of things, and a most wise craftsman for all time, who existed thus in the beginning of things: just as he now rules living creatures and nature, and brings them into the light, so in the past he discovered these orders by his wisdom. This very Mind he calls "Most Divine" in his books *After the Natural Things* <note>The *Metaphysics*.</note>, saying that it contemplates itself. In his *Ethics*, seeking after its contemplation, he calls it God. Thus, according to Plato, the Chaldean, and Aristotle, the Mind is God, is the Offspring of God, and is the Queen of the world.

->*That the Mind, according to Macrobius, is the Daughter of the Supreme Good, and the Supreme Good itself is the Highest Parent.*<-

### CHAPTER VI.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial U containing floral ornamentation and a central landscape or architectural motif.</image-desc> BUT SO THAT these things may stand as most true, and that the Mind is second from the Father himself (as the Chaldean used to say), and is the Daughter and divine Progeny (as Plato says in his epistle, for which reason the Chaldean called him Father), we must hear and admire the things spoken by our Macrobius <note>A 5th-century Roman philosopher famous for his commentary on Cicero's *Dream of Scipio*.</note>, as I mentioned a little earlier, gathered from the Platonists themselves. I shall bring forth the whole passage from the *Dream of Scipio*: "Furthermore," he says, "when a treatise dares to raise itself to the highest and first God of all, who among the Greeks is called <term>τὸ ἀγαθὸν πρώτων ἁπάντων</term> <gloss>the Good of all first things</gloss>, or to the Mind, which the Greeks call <term>νοῦν</term> <gloss>Nous/Mind</gloss>, which contains the original species of things, which are <term>εἴδη</term> <gloss>Ideas/Forms</gloss>, born and proceeding from the highest God: when they speak of these—the highest God and the Mind—they touch upon nothing at all fabulous. But if they attempt to assign anything to these things, which surpass not only speech but also human thought, they flee to likenesses and examples. Thus Plato, when he was inspired to speak <term>περὶ ἀγαθοῦ</term> <gloss>concerning the Good</gloss>, did not dare to say what it is, knowing only this about it: that what kind of thing it is cannot be known by man. He found the Sun to be the only thing most like it among visible things, and through its likeness, he opened a path for his discourse to raise itself to the things that can be understood. Therefore, while antiquity established no image for Him as it did for other gods, it did so because the highest God, and the Mind born from Him, are as much beyond nature as they are beyond the soul: where no myths can reach." Behold, you have heard and seen in Macrobius—who was not a Christian—two most divine and greatest things: the Supreme Good itself, the sovereignty of all things (as will also be clear in Plato), and the Mind born and proceeding from it. Born, I say, from the highest God, who is called by the Philosophers "the Good itself," "Goodness itself" by excellence: concerning which that Mind itself, his son, testified in this world: *No one is good except God alone* <note>A reference to Mark 10:18, here attributed to the "Mind" or Logos.</note>: namely, by that fountain-like, chief, and supereminent goodness. You have also seen that the Mind contains the species of things: by which creation is signified, and (as you shall see Philo also testifying)

<margin>A</margin> The creator of the world, the inventor of all things, which are contained within their own origins.

For as *Philo* <note>Philo of Alexandria, a Jewish philosopher who blended Greek philosophy with Biblical exegesis</note> himself says, if one wishes to speak more plainly and directly, one should call the Archetypal World—or the original world—nothing other than the Word <note>original: "Verbum"; referring to the Logos</note> itself, the Creator of the world. You will also see *Hermes Trismegistus* <note>A legendary Hellenistic figure associated with a body of wisdom texts</note> attributing the species, or ideas and origins of things, to the Word of God. By this attribution of species, therefore, it should be clear that the Wisdom of creating and inventing out of nothing is being designated, as all the aforementioned authors have proclaimed. It is designated that this Mind is the creator of the world, the adorner of things, the mother of forms, and a vast, inestimable wisdom. Behold, *Plato* called it, for the same reason, <term>the Cause</term> <note>original: "αἴτιον"</note>, the Author: in whose works, according to the testimony of *Laërtius*, Mind and Author are the same thing—that is, the Author of the world, the Founder, through whom all things were made. Concerning this, the prince of our Theology, the divine *Paul*, says things that are entirely similar, so that I may compare the two philosophies:

> Who is the image of the invisible God, the firstborn of every creature: for in him were all things created, that are in heaven, and that are in earth, visible and invisible, whether they be thrones, or dominions, or principalities, or powers: all things were created by him, and for him: and he is before all things, and by him all things consist. <note>Colossians 1:15-17. The author provides both the original Greek and a Latin translation, here rendered into English.</note>

This is the most divine trumpet of our Theology.

Compare all these things, therefore, and give thanks to the eternal Mind: whose mercy was so great that it always declared itself to the world, and did not hide itself even from the gentiles. Him whom Saint *Paul* calls the invisible God, *Macrobius* <note>A 5th-century Roman philosopher and grammarian</note>—following the Philosophers—calls the Supreme Good, the Primal of all things, the Prince of all things, calling him "God" by way of excellence, just as our great Theologian does. Him whom *Macrobius* calls the Mind, or Intel-
<margin>B</margin> ligence, or Wisdom, born from the highest God, which contains the species, *Paul* calls the Image of God (since the Son is the image of the Father) and the Firstborn of every creature—that is, either begotten before all created things, or the principal among all things, to whose greatness others cannot attain: above all created things. Him whom *Macrobius* said surpassed thought and speech, and who could not be known, being both incomprehensible and above nature, *Paul* calls both invisible (namely the Father himself) and the Firstborn (that is, the Mind preceding all creatures). And he to whom *Macrobius* said antiquity attributed the species, or the ideas and origins of things, *Paul* preached as the one through whom visible and invisible things were created—whether the celestial Gods themselves, whose names and natures men do not even know, or men and all earthly things. And he preached that he is before all things (that is, above nature, as *Macrobius* says) and that through him all things subsist. The truth, therefore, is clear, open, and known even to the gentiles. *Macrobius* likewise reported that when the Philosophers set out to speak of these greatest matters that surpass speech and thought, they sent away and commanded fables to be far removed; yet they use fables when they speak either of the soul, or of aerial or ethereal powers, or of the other Gods. He also said that the other Gods (namely those whom Saint *Paul* cited above) have no likeness to the Supreme Good and the Mind itself, because these are before all things and above all things. We have also seen *Macrobius* testify that all this Wisdom and stupendous Theology which we have narrated was known to antiquity. Therefore, it was known to the *Chaldean*, to *Zoroaster*, and to the wise men of the Persians.

Thus, from every side, by true conjectures, it shines forth that this Wisdom, which the whole world now worships, has radiated through all ages and nations—though more hidden than it is now, after the Mind itself, moved by its own goodness and the mercy of its Father (who is the Supreme Good), descended from heaven and clothed itself in human form and solid nature...

# AUGUSTINUS STEUCHUS OF GUBBIO, BOOK I.

...earth, dwelled among us, and spoke Wisdom with its own proper voice.
<margin>C</margin>

## From the same Oracles of the Chaldeans concerning the Mind, the divine Progeny, in whose image the human soul is made; and from Philo.

### CHAPTER VII.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap initial 'C' featuring a seated figure, possibly a scholar or philosopher, framed by ornate floral and architectural motifs.</image-desc>Why do you wonder, then, that the Greeks and Latins proclaimed the divinity of the Eternal Mind, and attributed to it the reason, invention, and order of all things? Why wonder that they perceived and asserted that it has a Father, since all this Wisdom is most ancient, handed down by the most primitive nations? The Greeks, approaching these nations for the sake of learning, drew streams into Greece from their most abundant fountains. Behold, therefore, how the same Chaldean Theology, as held in the sentences of the Zoroastrians <note>The "Chaldean Oracles" were often attributed to Zoroaster in the Renaissance</note>, teaches that this very divine and eternal Mind created [the soul] in its own image according to Moses: *the paternal mind sowed symbols into the souls* <note>original Greek: σύμβολα πατρικὸς νόος ἔσπειρε ταῖς ψυχαῖς</note>. By this oracle, it is declared that by the paternal Mind—that is, by that which is the Mind of the Father, the Wisdom and Intellect of the Father—there has been sewn into our souls a symbol of its own divinity. This is just as Philo the Jew confesses in so many places, asserting that the Word <term>Verbum/Logos: the divine reason or speech of God</term> itself is the archetype and model of our soul: that we are its likeness, just as it is the image of its father. The oracle says no differently, calling "symbol" what Philo calls "likeness" or "image," and what Moses calls "image and likeness"—in Hebrew, *Zalmenu, Demusthenu* <note>transliterated Hebrew: tzalmenu, demutenu (Gen 1:26)</note>. Psellus, the Christian Philosopher, felt and explained these same things when interpreting the aforementioned passage: *Just as the Mosaic book says God fashioned man according to his image, so also the Chaldean says the Creator and Father of the world sowed symbols of his own property into the souls. For from the paternal seeds, not only souls, but all the super-worldly orders sprouted.*
<margin>D</margin>
*And some signs exist incorporeally in the incorporeal substances themselves, being both incorporeal and intellectual; but other signs and symbols are in the worlds: the unspoken vision of the property of God, which even surpasses the virtues.* <note>Steuchus provides a Latin translation of the Greek text by Michael Psellus, an 11th-century Byzantine scholar</note> Just as, he says, the Mosaic book fashions man to the image of God, so clearly the Chaldean says the Creator and Father of the world inserted symbols of his own property into souls; since from the paternal seeds, not only souls, but all the heavenly orders sprouted. And indeed, some signs, which are in the incorporeal substances themselves, exist incorporeally and unitively; but other signs and symbols in the world are the secret properties of God, which even transcend the virtues. Thus says Psellus. In his writing, you see that from the paternal seeds—that is, from the Creator of the world, whom he previously called Father—not only our souls were born, but all the heavenly orders. Saint Paul confessed this earlier: that that Image and divine progeny created thrones, powers, dominions, and virtues. Therefore, you can perceive "Symbols" as the very origins and beginnings of things. Here it is also evident that Psellus speaks in the same manner as Solomon concerning the creation of man, saying that God inserted symbols, or the likeness of his own property; for Solomon said: *Because God created man and made him an image of his own property* <note>original Greek: ὅτι ὁ θεὸς ἔκτισεν τὸν ἄνθρωπον καὶ εἰκόνα τῆς ἰδιότητος ἐποίησεν αὐτόν (Wisdom of Solomon 2:23)</note>. Psellus also used the word which Empedocles used: From the One—that is, the truth—all things *sprouted* <note>original Greek: ἐβλάστη</note>: men, beasts, fish, birds, trees. Finally, the *long-lived gods* <note>original Greek: δολιχαίωνες θεοὶ</note>. Just as Paul speaks of thrones, dominions, and powers, Psellus speaks of heavenly orders. He likewise relates that these symbols exist in the eternal, intelligible substances incorporeally, by a certain divine reason of excellence, and through unity or simple aggregation. He says these same symbols in this world are the secret virtues of God—namely, the heavenly creation, both the lower and the higher, the visible and the invisible. It is clear, therefore, as stated above, that from the Mind—that is, the Wisdom of the Father—visible and invisible things were born, with Theo-

He claims that all ancient wisdom points to the human soul and celestial hierarchies being created by the Divine Mind (the Word).</summary>

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<margin>A</margin> Since the Word <note>original: "logia," referring to the divine utterances or the Logos</note> casts its rays everywhere: Behold, every tongue confesses the Lord. Similar ideas are seen in Philo regarding the Divine Word and in the Chaldean <note>likely referring to the Zoroastrian Oracles or the "Chaldean Oracles" attributed to Julian the Theurgist</note> regarding the Mind, of which he says: *The invisible, seminal, and artistic divine Word* <note>original: "ἀόρατος καὶ ἀσῃματικός τεχνικὸς θεῖος λόγος"</note>. Therefore, the seeds of things exist in the Divine Word according to Philo, just as according to the Chaldean, the Mind has sown its own symbols in souls. Philo explains this Chaldean Theology further: *Every human being, according to their intellect, is allied to the divine Word, having become an impression, or a fragment, or a radiance of that blessed nature* <note>original: "πᾶς ἄνθρωπος κατὰ μὲν τὴν διάνοιαν ᾠκείωται θείῳ λόγῳ τῆς μακαρίας φύσεως ἐκμαγεῖον ἢ ἀπόσπασμα, ἢ ἀπαύγασμα γεγονώς"</note>. This means every person, by virtue of their reason, shares a fellowship with the Divine Word, being made as an image, a cutting, or a ray of the blessed nature. With these words, Philo explains those symbols proposed by the Chaldean, for the symbols are many in number: Philo declared this through three names—Image or Likeness, Cutting, or Out-shining—whereas Moses would call it the "Breath of Life." And while the Chaldean uses a great number and speaks of a "symbol" in the singular, all these are the same: the three terms in Philo, the "symbol" in the Chaldean, the "property" in Solomon, the "image and likeness" in Moses, and in Marcus Tullius <note>Cicero</note> the "sampling" or "draught." Cicero says our souls are "drawn and sampled from divinity"; and in his *Tusculan Disputations*, he speaks in exactly the same way as the Chaldean, which is truly marvelous: "The human soul," he says, "is derived from the divine mind." He makes the Divine Mind the origin of our soul, just as the noble Chaldean—honored by all—describes it filling everyone, but especially the minds of the wise with its clarity. Recognize here the Mind as the Creator of the world (as discussed above) and now as the Creator of man; both of which were handed down by Moses. Whoever sees the other teachings among these same Chaldeans and their most ancient Theology will be all the more struck with wonder, which I confess has happened to me many times. Regarding this same paternal Mind, that Magus <note>Zoroaster</note>, or the whole choir of Magi, spoke thus in another noble and divine oracle: <margin>B</margin> *But the paternal Mind does not admit the will of that soul until it departs from oblivion and speaks a word, having placed in memory the paternal sign* <note>original: "ἀλλ' οὐκ εἰσδέχεται, κείνης τὸ θέλον πατρικὸς νοῦς μέχρις ἂν ἐξέλθῃ λήθῃς. καὶ ῥῆμα λαλήσῃ μνήμῳ ἐνθεμένη πατρικῷ συνθήματι"</note>. This is the same passage mentioned by Proclus. The royal meaning and divine pronouncement is this: nothing that the soul attempts or strives for pleases our Creator unless the soul first casts down, rejects, and shakes off "oblivion" <note>forgetfulness of its divine origin</note>, and breaks out into prayer and praise, exercising its memory, and always remembering that it was made in the image of God and received a paternal symbol. Explaining this sentiment, Psellus says word for word: The paternal Mind does not support the desires and impulses of the soul's will as long as it remains in forgetfulness of those things with which it was most richly endowed by the *Father of all good things* <note>original: "παναγαθοῦ πατρός"</note>, and until it reaches the remembrance of the sacred signs it received from Him, and utters a voice of pleasing thanks, employing the remembrance of the symbols of the Father who created it. For the soul consists of sacred reasons and divine symbols which come from the "intelligible forms," while the symbols come from the "divine unities." We are images of the intelligible substances, and likenesses of the unknown *tokens* <note>original: "συνθημάτων"</note>, signs, or orders. It is also necessary to know this: that every soul differs from every other soul according to its species, and that there are as many species of souls as there are souls. These are the words of Psellus; the rest is clear. As for what he called "intelligible substances" and "divine unities," I believe he feels—along with other Platonists and Dionysius the Areopagite—that these are the Gods or Angels, whom they call the first *intelligible* <note>original: "νοητοὺς"</note> and the second *intellectual* <note>original: "νοερούς"</note>. The Platonists also call the first ones *divine unities* <note>original: "θείας ἑνάδας"</note>, thinking these are the Ideas to which all things are reduced as simple units. Whether this is true will be investigated later. He teaches that our soul is an image of these. For Angel and man are similar; both are the image of God.

<margin>C</margin>
I pass over the other things that Psellus <note>Michael Psellus, an 11th-century Byzantine scholar who wrote extensively on the Chaldean Oracles.</note> philosophizes regarding the soul, since they do not belong to this place. I only show now that the oracle speaks concerning the Mind of God, or of the Father; which Mind itself often spoke while it was with us, exhorting us to the memory and love of its Father, and that it is necessary to love him, to remember him, and to praise him together. In such a way the prophet exhorted his own soul: *Bless the Lord, O my soul, and forget not all his benefits.* <note>Psalm 103:2</note> The oracle now speaks to the soul itself, that it should be mindful of its Father and Creator; otherwise, whatever it has studied will be pleasing in no way. Indeed, these same things found in Hermes Trismegistus <note>original: "Mercurium Trismegistum," the legendary Hellenistic figure associated with the Hermetic corpus.</note> will strike one with greater amazement and compel one to break forth into praises of the most holy, most merciful, and eternal Mind: which, just as it revealed itself to the Chaldeans, so it did not hide itself from the Egyptians.

# Concerning the Mind, the Divine Offspring, from Hermes Trismegistus: And the same as the Creator of the human spirit, as from Philo the Chaldean.

### CHAPTER VIII.

SO THAT YOU MAY RECOGNIZE that all ages and all nations from every side make a grand assembly to preach the truth, one must hear what Hermes Trismegistus says concerning this Mind. Where he had said that since the Father is Mind, he begat another Mind as an artisan <note>term: "opificem" - a craftsman or demiurge who shapes the world.</note>, as will be declared later: he introduces this same artisan mind speaking to Hermes himself (to whom it had appeared, just as it appeared to Adam, Abraham, Moses, and many other divine fathers) in the *Poimandres*, which is a most well-known book, and no sensible person doubts it belongs to Hermes: <note>original Greek: "παραγίνομαι ἐγὼ ὁ νοῦς τοῖς ὁσίοις..."</note> *I, the Mind, come to those who are holy, and good, and pure, and merciful, and who live piously; and my presence becomes a help, and immediately they know all things, and they invoke the Father propitiously with love, and they give thanks, blessing and singing hymns, being ranked toward him by affection.* These are the words of Hermes, and many others which would be long to relate. You have the Mind speaking of the Father and Begetter, to whom it says it leads the good and pious, and that it is the leader to the Father, to the paternal love. This is nothing other than what the same Mind speaks, having assumed a human figure, in our Theologian John: *And whatsoever ye shall ask the Father in my name, that will I do, that the Father may be glorified in the Son.* <note>John 14:13</note> And, *If ye keep my commandments, ye shall abide in my love; even as I have kept my Father's commandments, and abide in his love.* <note>John 15:10</note> And, *And this is life eternal, that they might know thee the only true God, and Jesus Christ, whom thou hast sent.* <note>John 17:3</note> Behold, in both there is love toward the Father, praise, and blessing, to which the Mind itself once invited us, and later did so after assuming our image; and of which the Chaldean said, that no action is pleasing to it which is done without the remembrance of God: and that the soul is polluted and impure which has no memory of God. We are therefore invited by three greatest Theologians—two most ancient, the other more recent—to the worship, love, and memory of God. In the second and third, the Mind itself speaks; in the first, another speaks concerning it. Among all of them is the name of the Father, and the Mind itself speaks of the Father. The Chaldean calls him the Paternal Mind. Most recently, moreover, when many things had been said in Hermes, Hermes replied: <note>original Greek: "εὖ μοί πάντα..."</note> *Well have you taught me all things, as I desired, O Mind.* Therefore, no doubt or wonder remains as to where the Platonists, and many others before Plato—where Anaxagoras and those whom Plato mentioned—extolled the Mind with praises, celebrated it, and named it. Likewise, all the Theologians before Plato, and later Plato himself and Aristotle with their whole school, [regarded] the creation...

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<margin>A</margin> They attributed the [creation] and order of things to the Mind; however, the Chaldean <note>Refers to the "Chaldean Oracles," a collection of spiritual texts from the 2nd century AD often attributed to Zoroaster.</note>, approaching more closely, confessed that man was created by the Mind, as was shown before. Philo <note>Philo of Alexandria, a Jewish philosopher who blended Hellenistic philosophy with Torah study.</note> likewise declared this Mind to be the creator of our soul. You will hear similar things from Hermes <note>original: "Mercurium," referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary author of the Hermetic corpus.</note>, so clear, open, set forth, and bright, that for joy I cannot even contain my tears: *The Mind, the Father of all, being life and nature, gave birth to a man equal to himself, whom he loved as his own offspring.* <note>original Greek: "ὁ δὲ πάντων πατὴρ ὁ νοῦς... ὃν ἠράσθη ὡς ἰδίου τόκου."</note> A little later: *And I, having arisen, became the guide of the race, teaching the words—how and in what manner they shall be saved; and I sowed in them the words of Wisdom, and they were nourished by the immortal water.* <note>original Greek: "ἐγὼ δὲ ἀναστὰς... ἐτράφησαν ἐκ τοῦ ἀμβροσίου ὕδατος."</note> The Mind, the Father of all, since it is life and nature, bore man similar to itself, whom it loved as its own child. I, says that same Mind, having raised them up, am the leader of the race, teaching the words in what way and by what reason they are preserved, and I planted in them the reasons of wisdom, and nourished them from the immortal water. How great is the similarity of these things which Hermes now says with those which the Chaldean said a little before: he said that the Mind inserted Symbols into souls: *the paternal mind sowed symbols in the souls* <note>original Greek: "σύμβολα πατρικὸς νόος ἔσπειρε ταῖς ψυχαῖς."</note>; Hermes, moreover, uses the same word: *I sowed in them the words of Wisdom.* The Chaldean and all others attributed the creation and the arrangement of all things to the Mind. In no different way, Hermes, openly and clearly, is similar to Moses in all things: *The air brought forth the winged creatures, and the water the swimming ones. And the earth and the water were separated from one another, just as the Mind willed.* <note>original Greek: "ἀὴρ δὲ πτηνὰ ἤνεγκε... καθὼς ἠθέλησεν ὁ νοῦς."</note> The air, he says, brought out the birds, the water the swimmers. And a separation of the earth and water from each other was made, just as the Mind willed. Therefore, he whom Moses said separated the dry land from the waters, commanding and saying that the waters should be gathered into one place and the dry land appear, and he who ordered the waters to produce every kind of living creature—behold, it was the Mind, the creator of things: which Anaxagoras and Plato constantly asserted; for the Greeks read these things among Hermes and the other foreigners <note>original: "barbaros," used here in the classical sense of non-Greeks.</note>.

<margin>B</margin> Behold how divinely Hermes conforms himself to Philo; indeed, all antiquity responds to itself. Philo introduces two Minds: one above us, the other our own. That which is above us, he says, is our <term>archetype</term> <note>The original pattern or model from which copies are made.</note>, and that we were created in its likeness. And he derives this from the book of Moses: which Mind Hermes himself calls the <term>Logos</term> <note>The Word or Reason.</note>, the Word, which is the same thing, as we shall see later.

->Concerning the same Mind also from the Chaldean oracles, that it is the creator,<-

### CHAPTER IX.

CONCERNING THIS same Mind there exists among the Chaldeans a wonderful mystery and oracle, which is brought forward by Proclus <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote extensively on the Chaldean Oracles.</note>, although not so properly explained by him: *For alone, having plucked the flower of the Mind from the paternal strength, he possesses the power to understand the paternal mind, to instill it into all fountains and beginnings or principalities, and to understand, and to remain always in a tireless whirling.* <note>original Greek: "πολὺ γὰρ μόνος... ἀόκνῳ στροφάλιγγι."</note> Which are: Alone plucking from the paternal virtue the Flower of the Mind, he possesses understanding itself, and to instill the paternal Mind into all fountains and principles or principalities, and understanding itself, and to remain in himself by an unwearied conversion. This oracle, although somewhat obscure—which is the custom of oracles and mysteries—it must be known that it calls the "Flower of the Mind" the chief, excellent, distinguished, and pre-eminent Mind. Just as the Greeks and our own people call the most eminent person the "flower" and "glory," so the principal, most eminent, and queenly Mind is the "flower of the Mind." It is the same here as the "paternal Mind" above. This is said to have wisdom, sense, and understanding itself from the paternal virtue, for it is the wisdom of its Father. Philo asserts that the Father filled it with eternal ideas and the Origins of things, and that he begat it as the wisest. This same queenly and paternal Mind, he says, having received the paternal virtue, pours wisdom into the others, which he calls fountains...

<summary>This page argues for a universal theological truth (Perennial Philosophy) by showing how Hermes Trismegistus, the Chaldean Oracles, and Philo of Alexandria all describe the Divine Mind (Logos) as the creator of the world and humanity, mirroring the account in Genesis.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Mind, Hermes Trismegistus, Philo of Alexandria, Chaldean Oracles, Logos, Creation, Archetype, Perennial Philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Ancient Theology" (Prisca Theologia), specifically comparing the celestial hierarchies and the creative "Mind" found in Chaldean, Platonic, and Hermetic sources with Christian doctrine.</meta>

->AUGUSTINE EUGUBINUS, BOOK I.<-

...ties and principalities, or beginnings—namely, the divine orders of Angels, who are also called "Principalities" in our own scriptures <note>original: "nostris literis"</note>. They are never called "sources" <note>Latin: "fontes"</note>, as far as I know, unless you wish to call them sources in the same way as *archai* <note>Greek: ἀρχαὶ</note>—that is, beginnings, principalities, or heads—rather than the origins of things, as is wrongly believed by some Platonists, except insofar as they have received power from God. From that very paternal and queenly Mind, it is said (as the most wise Paul and Plato have testified) that the power to understand, to be, and to remain is given to the Angels, and that they are always turned toward God himself and his divine, eternal beauty: *The spirits stand around in crowds, on the right and on the left; nor is it enough to have seen Him once, it delights them to linger; they cannot be filled; they hasten in rivalry to gaze upon Him without end.* <note>The author is quoting or paraphrasing Latin poetry to describe the angelic host.</note> This the Chaldean calls the "untiring whirling" <note>Greek: ἄοκνον στροφάλιγγα</note>, a restless conversion, a conversion that is vehement, kindled, and burning. Hence they are also called Seraphim <note>Hebrew: "burning ones"</note>, glowing and flaming with love.

And by this oracle <note>The "Chaldean Oracles," a mystical text from late antiquity</note> are explained those things which the sublime Paul preached: that through Him all things visible and invisible were founded, every kind of God, Thrones, Dominations, and Principalities, whom the Chaldean also calls "sources" and "beginnings." Mercury <note>Hermes Trismegistus</note> asserts that living creatures were created by Him; that the earth was separated from the water as He had wished; that man was created in His likeness; that the principles of wisdom were inserted into our minds; and that we are called by Him and led back to the Father. The Chaldean, moreover, calls Him the fashioner of the world, the fashioner of souls, of Angels, and of Gods. Other Egyptians call Him the creator of the Sun or of light.

Truly, this Theology has been most luminous through all ages. Thus, anyone who thinks that this religion and wisdom did not exist in the world until now, and wonders at the doctrine of the Father and Son delivered to the earth as if it were a new religion, will cease to wonder once the most ancient Theology has been diligently studied and properly explored. In its wonderful and stupendous harmony among all nations, no one—provided they are skillful and not hardened by stubbornness or the harsh persuasion of opposing dogmas—will fail to marvel, to praise the eternal God, and to acknowledge the beneficent Parent. He, who was to show His whole self to us, and who had previously given various obscure visions of Himself to men, came in person and dwelt with us for a time, so that we might rejoice with Him for eternity. And for this reason, no excuse will remain on the Day of Judgment. For He was always known in some manner; and His will—that we live rightly—and His power—that all things were created through Him and that He was our origin—was always celebrated and known.

Regarding this same Mind, it is worthwhile to hear a wonderful and most profound mystery, of which all our subsequent Theology (which declared the ancient Theology better and more aptly) was an explanation. This oracle is cited by the Platonists: *For the paternal Mind, conceiving, being self-generated, sowed in all the fire-heavy bond of Love, so that all things might remain for an endless time, loving.* <note>Greek: νοήσας γὰρ πατρικὸς νόος αὐτογενέθλιος, πᾶσιν ἐνέσπειρεν δεσμὸν πυριβριθῆ ἔρωτος ὄφρα τὰ πάντα μένη χρόνον εἰς ἀπέραντον, ἐρῶντα.</note> By thinking and understanding, the paternal Mind, which is of itself the generator, inserted into all things a bond heavy with fire, a bond of love, so that all things might endure for an immense time as loving things. This truly divine voice, sent from heaven, first names (consistent with itself) the paternal Mind, the Mind of the Father, who has a Father. It calls Him "Self-Generating" <note>Latin: "per se Genitricem"</note>; because, as all philosophy harmoniously and tirelessly proclaims, the Mind was the principle of things, producing all things from nothing, only out of those eternal origins whose simple unity is itself. Hence it is called by others "Self-Wise" <note>Greek: αὐτόσοφος</note>, "Self-Perfect" <note>Greek: αὐτοτελὴς</note>, and "First-Born" <note>Greek: πρωτόγονος</note>—that is, both generated from eternity before all things, and itself the first generator of things, the first origin. It says that by thought alone (which is our dogma and that of the Platonists) it produced, procreated, and inserted into things the bond of love, a fiery bond, by which all things, loving and adhering to one another, might endure for all time. This bond is first seen in living creatures as the wonderful desire for joining bodies, as the Greek and Latin philosophers teach; from which perennial generation exists, never cooling at any time, in all living...

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<margin>A</margin> ...by the female and male desiring one another. Aristotle, in confessing that this was devised by God, certainly confesses it was by that same being whom the Chaldean <note>Refers to the "Chaldean Oracles," a collection of mystical texts often cited by Renaissance Neoplatonists.</note> calls the Mind, the daughter of God and the inventor of all things. For he himself said in the *Politics*: <note>original Greek: ἅμα γ’ ἡ φύσις ἀναπληροῖ... "At the same time, nature fulfills the eternal being through this cycle, since it cannot do so by number, but only by species. Thus has nature been ordained by God, for both the man and the woman, toward fellowship."</note> "At the same time, nature fulfills the eternal being through this cycle and return, so that it may be everlasting; since this could not happen according to individual number, but only according to species. Thus was the nature of the male and female provided by God for their union." He says these same things in the books *On Generation and Corruption*: "Since all things were corruptible, because they were furthest removed from that which is first," <note>original Greek: τῷ λοιπομένῳ τρόπῳ... "God filled up the whole in the remaining way, making generation continuous."</note> "God filled up the universe in the remaining way that was left, making generation perennial." This he did so that all things might generate through mutual loves, ardently loving one another in turn. The Chaldean proclaims that this was done by the Divine Mind, calling that bond "fiery" <note>original: *pyroeidē*, meaning of the nature of fire or glowing.</note>—heavy with fire and inflamed. And for that reason, as you will see in Philo’s book *On the World*, the Word of God is an "unbreakable bond" <note>original Greek: δεσμὸς ἄῤῥηκτος</note>, the indissoluble bond of the whole world; for it inserted an eternal love not only into living creatures, but even into inanimate things, by which they might long for one another. This love is as miraculous in trees and herbs, in the elements—water, fire, earth, and air—as it is in animals. Nor was it anything else that Moses recorded as being done by God: *Let each bring forth according to its kind.* Therefore our own Theologian <note>Likely referring to the Psalmist or Job.</note> says: *Your hands have made me*; and, *You have curdled me like milk*; and, *You have formed me before and behind*, and many other such things. For He both discovered the fitting forms and added love to both. And you see how wonderfully they agree, those who preceded the Christian religion by almost more than two thousand
<margin>B</margin> years.
