->*Concerning the same Mind, from Hermes and the Platonists, and all its divinity: and that it is the creator of the world, the Idea of the world, and the archetypal world itself.*<-

## CHAPTER X.

THERE ALSO exist two other divine testimonies concerning the Mind, to be admired by every wisest man: one in Hermes Trismegistus, the other among the Platonists. First, Trismegistus taught the Greeks that this Mind of Anaxagoras and Plato—which Anaxagoras called "impassible" <note>original: *apathē*, meaning unaffected by external agency.</note> according to Aristotle, and which Plato said was "unmixed with pleasure" <note>original: *edonē memigmenon*</note>, mingled with nothing—should have these names and properties attributed to it, in the work titled *Asclepius*: <note>original Greek: τὸ οὖν ἀσώματον τί ἐστι... "What then is the incorporeal? Mind and Word, providing for itself out of the whole, free from every body, unerring, impassible by body, and intangible: standing itself within itself, containing all things and the savior of beings; whose rays are goodness, truth, the archetypal light, and the archetype of the soul."</note> "What then is the incorporeal? It is Mind," <image-desc>Circular library stamp of the Biblioteca Nazionale Roma Vittorio Emanuele</image-desc> he says, "and the Word, which sustains and contains itself out of the universe, free from every body: Unerring, impassible by the body, and intangible: standing by itself in itself, capable of containing all things, and the preserver of things: whose rays are goodness, truth, exemplary light, and the exemplar of the soul." These are the words of Trismegistus; even if an Angel were speaking, he could not declare things clearer or more divine. He was, as it appears, the source of Greek Philosophy; from there they drew their Theology, and from here they adopted that famous and celebrated Mind, the origin and cause of things, to which they gave wisdom and the invention of all things, describing it just as it deserved, and proclaiming it unerring, as one that was full of knowledge, skill, and prudence, whose every invention, as in the *Phaedo*...

# AUGUSTINUS STEUCHUS OF GUBBIO, BOOK I.

...as Socrates says, it could not have been anything but good and most skillful. For, says <margin>C</margin> **Trismegistus** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Egyptian sage</note>, it is infallible; it cannot be deceived or mistaken. Furthermore, it is sustained by no bodily aids, lives by no bodily force, and drives the ages forward; it does not need a body, which is corruptible, but being free and unhindered by any body, it is sustained by itself, by its own wisdom, and by the incredible power of its own nature. Nor can it receive any harm from bodies, as bodies are unable to touch it; for it is intangible. And since it is of such a nature, it is capable of containing all things and is the preserver of all: for in it all things live and subsist. It is the secret and hidden bond and the preservation of all things. Nor can it grow weary in protecting things: because it is so vast—not indeed in bodily mass or size, but in wisdom and skill—that it contains all things and is present everywhere. From this **Mind**, he says, as if from an immense light, all the rays of goodness, wisdom, and truth have shone forth. From here flashed out whatever exists anywhere, whatever is good, and whatever is true. Therefore, it was the **archetypal light**: that very light which, like a queen and mother, created all things. From it the celestial lights were kindled. This is called by **Philo the Jew** *panaugeia* <note>original Greek: παναύγεια</note>, which you might call "all-shining," a vast region of all light, the seat and the very origin of light, from which all light, both bodily and incorporeal, has flowed. And for that reason, it is also the **Archetype of the soul**, as all the authorities mentioned above confessed. It created man himself, and was like an archetype for him, forming him in its own image. For this reason, according to Philo, there are two <term>logoi</term> <note>Greek: λόγοι; "Words" or "Reasons"</note>, two reasons, two Minds: one above us, the pattern of our reason; the other our reason itself. And there Philo openly confessed—imbued, I believe, by these books of Trismegistus—that the **Archetypal Word** is the creator of our word, that is, of our reason, mind, and soul. Therefore, **Mercury** <note>Hermes</note> described the Mind to us, calling it also from the deepest wisdom the <term>logos</term> <margin>a</margin> <note>Greek: λόγον; the Word</note>, a term with which the theology of Philo and the Platonists is filled, as you shall see, descending and arising from <margin>D</margin> ancient ages, revealed by the Word himself, who disclosed himself to Mercury and many others of the ancients. And it is certain that the ancient Mercury truly spoke these things: since they are also used by Plato and all the Platonists, and are celebrated in their books. The same Mercury, in the book titled *The Sacred Discourse* <note>Greek: ἱερὸς λόγος</note>, speaking of creation and beginning as if a "Genesis," says these things: *God is the beginning of all things, as are Mind, nature, and matter* <note>original Greek: Ἀρχὴ τῶν ὄντων ὁ θεὸς, καὶ ὁ νοῦς, καὶ φύσις καὶ ὕλη</note>. Behold what **Laërtius** reports Plato to have said: that the world was created by God, and that he called God **Mind and Cause** <note>Greek: νοῦν καὶ αἴτιον</note>, a concept taken from Trismegistus. God and Mind, therefore, are the same. This was the principle of all things, which so many others confessed above. All these with one voice declare this Mind to be the creator of man, now declaring and consulting it as the Mind of the world. The ancients therefore established two principles of things: one active, the Mind and God; the other receiving and passive, nature or matter. This is the Theology of the ancients, as you have seen everywhere. The Platonists also say, according to this ancient Theology, that *the universe consists of Mind and necessity, with nature suffering necessity, but necessity turning back toward the Mind* <note>original Greek: τὸ πᾶν ἐκ νοῦ καὶ ἀνάγκης ἐσὶ, φύσεως πάθοντος τὴν ἀνάγκην, τῆς δὲ ἀνάγκης πρὸς τὸν νοῦν ἐπισροφὴν</note>, the Universe is from Mind and necessity, with Mind persuading and compelling necessity to obey, while necessity is compliant, turning itself toward the Mind and following it. Plato first said these things in the *Timaeus*, speaking in this manner, with few exceptions: "But," he says, "one must also include the things that come from necessity. For the creation of this world was a mixture resulting from the combination of necessity and Mind: but with Mind ruling over necessity by persuading it to produce the best results for the things being made. Thus, with necessity conquered and compelled by Intelligence and the commands of God, this whole was founded." It must also be observed and keenly noticed what **Proclus** reports: that **Theodorus the Platonist** found an opinion in **Porphyry**, as if brought out of Persia: *that they placed two minds before the soul itself, one containing the ideas of the wholes, the other of the parts* <note>original Greek: δύο νόας πρὸ αὐτῆς θέντας, τὸν μὲν τῶν ὅλων τὰς ἰδέας ἔχοντα, τὸν δὲ τῶν μερικῶν</note>: they established two minds before the soul, one of which...

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<margin>A</margin>...one might contain the ideas of universal things, the other of particulars. For these are the things which Philo said more clearly were the <term>two words</term> <note>original: "δύο λόγους" (duo logous); referring to the Logos as both a transcendent principle and an immanent one</note>, two reasons, two accounts: one archetypal, the other our own. Moreover, they granted to the great Mind, as the creator, the universal ideas—as Plato does in the *Timaeus*, Hermes in the *Poimandres*, and Timaeus of Locri. However, they said the lesser Mind, which is our own, holds the ideas of particular things. This Theology had come from the barbarians <note>In this context, "barbarians" refers to non-Greek ancient civilizations like the Egyptians, Chaldeans, or Hebrews, often viewed by Renaissance scholars as the original sources of wisdom.</note>. Although it is possible that they perceived two Minds rather as those which we demonstrated in the first oracle. "The Father," he says, "perfected all things, and delivered them to the second Mind, which the whole race of men calls the first." Far more clearly, however, and with fuller words—truly golden and in a celestial oration—Hermes spoke about this Mind in the third discourse to Asclepius; you will see there the clear origin of Greek Theology and of all the Platonists. It is also brought forward by Saint Cyril: <note>The following is a translation of the Greek passage from Cyril of Alexandria quoting Hermes Trismegistus</note> "For it is not possible to provide the divine mysteries to the uninitiated, but listen with the mind. There was one sole intellectual light before the intellectual light. And there is always a luminous mind. And there was nothing other than the unity of this, always being in itself, always containing all things by its own mind, light, and spirit. Outside of this, there is no God, no angel, no demon, nor any other substance. For he is the Lord of all, and Father, and God, and source, and life, and power, and light, and mind, and spirit, and all things are in him and under him." These words mean: For it is not possible that you should hand over mysteries of this kind among the profane, who are not yet initiated. But hear with the soul: There was one sole mental light, before the mental light; and there is always a Mind, either illuminated or illuminating; and there was nothing else but the unity of that very thing, always existing in itself, always containing all things by its own mind, light, and spirit. After a few words Hermes adds: Outside of this, there is no God, no Angel, no Demon, no substance, none other. For he is the Lord of all, and Father, and God, and source, and life, and power, and light, and Mind, and spirit, and all things are in him, and are under him. In these words of Trismegistus, it is discerned from where Plato

<margin>B</margin>drank his own mysteries. Moreover, just as Plato, when about to speak of these same mysteries in his *Epistle*, prefaced it by saying he wished to speak through riddles <note>or "enigmas"</note>, lest what he writes should come into the hands of the unskilled and they should despise what they do not understand; Trismegistus used the same preface, and Orpheus likewise: "I shall speak to those for whom it is lawful; let the door be shut to the profane." And first, in Trismegistus, there are these paternal names, as also in all the rest of the Theologians: "One mental light, before the mental light." Therefore, just as the Chaldean taught two Minds, of which the higher and prior had begotten the second; so Hermes taught two mental lights, two luminous Minds, Minds full of light. The first is Unity itself. For we have already often seen, and shall see, that the "One" is called the eternal progenitor himself. This One is both light, and is not a corporeal light, but a mental light—a light of another kind than that which is discerned by corporeal eyes. Moreover, this mental light is above, or before, the other mental light. Two lights: one prior, the other second or posterior. This light was always, whether the first or the second. And both lights were nothing other than one conjunction, one association. Nor was it anything else from eternity than the conjunction and unity of this light, and the highest union. Before all ages, it was always in itself. But once the world was created, it contains all things—not with hands, but by the divinity of its mind, and by the spirit, or its wisdom. Besides this first and second, there was nothing else from eternity: no God, or Angel, no demons who hold the second place, no substance. These neither were from eternity, nor can they be said to "be," for whom it is not true "to be" <note>i.e., they do not possess necessary or eternal existence</note>. God himself is, and these are his names. Likewise, in other places Trismegistus spoke of the same thing, in the *Poimandres*, where he had set forth a vision such as Moses saw in the bush, and a voice sent forth, of the same kind as Moses heard; and just as with Moses that same one said, "I am the God of your fathers, I am who I am," so in Trismegistus, declaring himself, he said: <term>I am Poimandres, the Mind of sovereignty</term> <note>original: "ἐγὼ μὲν φησὶν εἰμὶ ποιμάνδρης ὁ τῆς αὐθεντίας νοῦς"</note>. A little later: <term>That light, he said, I am Mind, your God, who is before the moist nature that appeared out of the darkness</term> <note>original: "τὸ φῶς ἐκεῖνο ἔφη ἐγὼ νοῦς ὁ σὸς θεὸς, ὁ πρὸ φύσεως ὑγρᾶς τῆς ἐκ σκότους φανείσης"</note>. # AUGUSTINUS EUGUBINUS, BOOK I.

...he said, I am Poimandres <note>original: "Pœmander"; a figure representing the Mind of the Great Lord in Hermetic texts</note>, that is, the Shepherd of the human race. For that is what the name seems to designate:
<margin>C</margin>
The Mind of Authority. That light, he said, am I, the Mind. Your God, who existed before the moist nature which appeared out of the darkness. Trismegistus utters these grand and sublime mysteries. First, when the Mind calls itself the "Mind of Authority." For he called the Father "Authority," who is first and before all, even greater than the Mind itself insofar as He begot it and gave it power; yet He is not greater in essence, since He begot one equal to Himself and co-eternal. He Himself, therefore, is authority, primacy, the beginning, the head, the first being, the nature of goodness. To Him belongs the Mind, as the Chaldean <note>likely referring to the Chaldean Oracles</note> said, so often calling it the <term>paternal mind</term> <note>original: "πατρικὸν νόον"</note>; in our Theology, it is the Wisdom of the Father. This same Mind also proclaims itself to be God, just as in the writings of Moses. It even boasts of having existed before the "moist nature." Here is a sublime, wonderful mystery. For the "moist nature" is what the ancients called Chaos—from the fusion and moisture of earth and water mixed together—or *hyle* <note>matter</note>, which is, with the letters inverted, <term>ilys</term> <note>original: "ἰλύς"; meaning mud or slime</note>, the slime of the ground. It is the earth "void and empty," or <term>invisible and unformed</term> <note>original: "ἀόρατος καὶ ἀκατασκεύαστος"</note>. Before this state—which was the origin of things, the beginning, the embryo, the seed, the foundation, the commencement of the whole world from earth and water—the Mind boasts that it existed. Therefore, this is the same one whom Solomon, calling her Wisdom, introduces as speaking (just as she speaks in Trismegistus): *The Lord possessed me in the beginning of his ways, before he strengthened the heavens above, and balanced the fountains of the waters,* and the rest concerning creation. In Solomon, she proclaims herself possessed by the Lord; in Trismegistus, she proclaims herself the Mind of Authority; in the Chaldean, the Paternal Mind; among our own writers, the Wisdom of the Father. Of whom, therefore, did Moses speak when, beginning his description of things, he said: *In the beginning God created the heaven and the earth*; and, *He who was carried over the waters*; and, *He who commanded the light to emerge*? He hinted at that Mind, which existed before the heaven and earth were made, before the
<margin>D</margin>
moist matter, and the commencement and origin of the whole world. Likewise, in that which he calls a <term>hymnody</term> <note>original: "ὑμνωδίαν"</note>, a hymn or song of thanksgiving, he speaks verses toward his creator: <term>You are the father, you are the Lord, you are the Mind.</term> <note>original: "σὺ ὁ πατὴρ, σὺ ὁ κύριος, σὺ ὁ νοῦς."</note> All those, therefore, who used the name "Mind," were perceiving the act of creation; they showed that all things were produced by its wisdom. There is an oracle among the Platonists from ancient Theology: <term>For this whole universe has long been an imitation of the Mind.</term> <note>original: "νοῦ μὲν γὰρ μίμημα πάλαι τόδε πᾶν."</note> This universe, he says, is drawn from the Mind, formed by the Mind as if by a craftsman. Indeed, it is a wonderful proof and indication that Hermes <note>Mercurius</note> and the Egyptians brought forth these mysteries concerning the Mind and the Word of God as the founder of the world, as seen in Plutarch, in the work he inscribed *On Isis and Osiris*, or *On the Theology of the Egyptians*. For he says that in their secrets there was a fable (for it was the custom of ancient Theology to shroud truth in fables): <term>That the Mind and Word of God, existing by itself in the invisible and hidden realm, proceeded into generation moved by a motion</term> <note>original: "ὅτι καθ' ἑαυτὸν ὁ τοῦ θεοῦ νοῦς καὶ λόγος, ἐν τῷ ἀοράτῳ, καὶ ἀφανεῖ βεβηκὼς εἰς γένεσιν ὑπὸ κινήσεως προῆλθεν"</note>, or moved itself to create. Do you recognize the streams of truth scattered through the whole world from a single source? If you wish to know who made that which is called *Genesis*, which Moses wrote (though God truly made it), containing the generation of heaven and earth, of celestial and terrestrial things: according to the Egyptian authors, it was the Mind, it was the Word of God, invisible, reigning in the secrets of its divinity. It remained in the secret, inscrutable darkness of its divinity; and, as our great Theologian <note>likely referring to St. John the Evangelist</note> says, *It was with God*, most closely joined to Him, in His bosom. It proceeded to create, to generate. Therefore, this is the one of whom Moses says: *In the beginning God created the heaven and the earth.* This is the Genesis made, by the witness of the Egyptians, by the Word, by the Divine Mind. Which, just as it is called in Greek <term>nous</term> (Mind) and <term>logos</term> (Reason/Word), so in Hebrew it is called Spirit, the act of knowing itself, Wisdom itself, Life. From these sources...

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<margin>A</margin>
I believe it flows from these sources, those things which Philo <note>Philo of Alexandria, a Hellenistic Jewish philosopher</note> sings so remarkably concerning the Word of God and the Mind, calling it similarly the *invisible Word of God* <note>original: "ἀόρατον θεοῦ λόγον"</note>, the craftsman of the world: and as Pindarus says, the *best artisan* <note>original: "ἀριστοτέχνην"</note>: while the Italian poet calls him the eternal master. He is the Word of God, the Mind, the invisible, secret, and hidden wisdom, which afterwards was made as if visible, whose mysteries shone forth more clearly in the final age as he himself drew nearer to us. Concerning this very subject, the prince of Egyptian Theology <note>Referring to Hermes Trismegistus</note> spoke in the *Poimandres*—matters which the other priests perhaps looked back upon in those things reported by Plutarch: *The Word radiating from the Mind is the Son of God* <note>original: "ὁ δὲ ἐκ νοὸς φαεινὸς λόος υἱὸς θεοῦ"</note>.

->*Concerning the Mind also from the oracle of the Chaldeans, and Porphyry, and Proclus: and that it is from the Father, that it is the creator of the world: and that it is that which is called the ultra-mundane Mind.*<-

### CHAPTER XI.

<margin>B</margin>
It is also declared by the Greek Philosophers, and it is believed that the oracle of the Chaldeans spoke concerning the Father and the Son, which was brought forward in the first place: among whom is Psellus <note>Michael Psellus, a Byzantine monk and scholar</note>. The Chaldean said: The Father perfected all things, and delivered them to the second Mind, or Intelligence, which all the human race calls the first. But Psellus the Philosopher says: *The first Father of the trinity, having fashioned the whole creation, delivered it to the Mind, which Mind the entire race of men, being ignorant of the paternal excellence, call the first God; however, our own dogma holds the opposite, that the Mind itself, the Son of the great Father, fashioned the whole creation. For the Father is said in the Mosaic book to dictate to the Son the idea of the production of created things, but the Son becomes the actual worker of the deed.* <note>original Greek: "τὴν πᾶσαν κτίσιν... ὁ δὲ υἱὸς αὐτουργὸς τοῦ ποιήματος γίνεται"</note> That is to say, the Father in the trinity first labored upon the universal condition of things and delivered it to the Mind; which Mind all the human race, ignorant of the paternal excellence, call the first God. But our dogma holds the contrary: because that first Mind, the Son of the great Father, performed the entire labor. For the Father, in the book of Moses <note>The Book of Genesis</note>, dictates to the Son the idea of the production of things: but the Son himself works by himself. Thus says Psellus.

But let Psellus take note that this oracle does not oppose our dogma, but agrees with it in every way, if one understands and unravels it correctly. For we ourselves confess the Father almighty, maker of heaven and earth, and the Son, through whom all things were made. This Chaldean dogma perceives that the Father was the author of all things, the first, but the Son was the second, commanded by the Father to create, or who created by the will of the Father, and who had begotten him as almighty. The creation belongs to both, but to the Father first, who is first. And that is also what Psellus handed down: that the Father dictates and proposes the idea of creation to the Son, but the Son performs the work. Therefore, the Father made first, in whom first was the idea and the will and the fruitfulness of creation: but the Son created, because he did nothing except what the Father willed, having received the fruitfulness and the entire labor within the paternal idea and fruitfulness. And creation is the first invention of the world, which, since it had been pleasing and conceived, decreed, and designated in the Father first, was also in the Son by the Father's gift: who gave him power in heaven and on earth, and glorified his Son with the glory which he had before the world was made. This is what the Chaldean perceives. This same Mind is likewise called by the Platonists the *ultra-mundane Mind* <note>original: "ὁ ἐπέκεινα νοῦς"</note>, before all things, and what that divine man <note>St. Paul</note> called the *firstborn of all creation* <note>original: "πρωτότοκος πάσης κτίσεως"</note>. Concerning which Porphyry says in his *Propositions*: *Concerning the ultra-mundane or celestial Mind, many things are said according to intellection. But it is known and considered through an ignorance which is better than any knowledge, as if concerning a hypothesis. Through proclamation many things are said, but through height come knowledge and comprehension. For like is known by like: because all knowledge is a likening to the thing known.* <note>original Greek: "περὶ τοῦ ἐπέκεινα νοῦ... πᾶσα γνῶσις τοῦ γνωστοῦ ὁμοίωσις"</note> Regarding the ultra-mundane or celestial Mind, he says, many things are said according to intellection. But it is known and considered by an ignorance that is better than all cognition.

# AUGUSTINUS EUGUBINUS, BOOK I.

better. Just as with one who sleeps. For through wakefulness many things are said; but through sleep
<margin>C</margin>
there occurs a notion and an apprehension. For like is known by like: because every notion is a likeness of the thing known. Porphyry <note>A 3rd-century Neoplatonic philosopher</note> hints at these things, which our Dionysius <note>Dionysius the Areopagite, a Christian mystic who emphasized "negative theology"</note> often says concerning negations in Theology: that ignorance in the highest matters is often better than science, and negation better than affirmation. Likewise, there is the sacred silence by which the ineffable divinity is worshiped. And we explain Theology rightly when we say God is inscrutable, unsearchable, incomprehensible, and ineffable. This is called by Porphyry <term>anoanoia</term> <note>original: ἀνονοία; a state of "non-thinking" or supra-intellectual unknowing</note>, an ignorance better than any notion. Porphyry also said that through affirmation many things are predicated of it, such as that it is the omnipotent creator and the maker of all things. But again, he said that negation (which, if it occurs in the soul, is called <term>anoanoia</term>) is better than any affirmation. And he provides a most beautiful example: Those who are awake can discourse much about one who sleeps, and tell many stories about him; but the true notion of sleep, and what sleep finally is, or what it is to sleep, will then be clear if you, who have slept at some time, have learned what sleeping itself is. And thus sleep is better known through another sleep than through wakefulness, because like is perceived by like. So regarding the highest and otherworldly Intelligence, many things are predicated through notion and science, but there is a certain ignorance similar to sleep, which understands better and more worthily by being silent, and by silencing, than if it spoke and affirmed. Because that super-worldly Mind exceeds all minds and senses. By these teachings of Porphyry, therefore, the distinction between the eternal Mind and all others is declared. For often both in Hermes <note>original: Mercurium; referring to Hermes Trismegistus</note> and other philosophers, there is talk of our mind and the mind in common; but reason itself everywhere separates the principal Mind, the mother of others, from every other. This principal one is especially called <term>ho epekeina nous</term> <note>original: ὁ ἐπέκεινα νοῦς; the Mind that is "beyond" or transcendent</note>. Concerning this, it is likely the same Porphyry or Proclus spoke in the same book when he says: Every process is from that toward which a natural conversion <note>A turning back or return to the source</note> occurs. Afterward, since all things are born from the Mind as from a principle
<margin>D</margin>
and origin, he concludes: <term>From these things it is manifest that the Mind is desired by all, and all things proceed from the Mind, and the whole world-order holds its existence from the Mind, even if it be in eternal things; it is not because it is eternal that it does not proceed from the Mind. For it does not fail to turn back because it is always ordered. But it both always proceeds and is always Mind according to substance, and is always a turning back of itself and an order.</term> <note>original Greek: ἐκ δὲ τούτων φανερὸν... καὶ τάξιν</note> From these things, he says, it is manifest that the Mind is desired by all, and all things proceed from the Mind, and every adornment is from the Mind, even if it be eternal. Nor does it fail to proceed from the Mind because it is everlasting. For it does not fail to be converted and reflected because it is always governed and ordered. But the everlasting also always proceeds according to substance: and it is always converted, even being indissoluble according to substance. He says these things without doubt concerning the otherworldly Mind or Wisdom. Nor do I linger now on what he disputes concerning the eternal orders; these will be weighed in their proper places. Proclus also divinely explained and assigned the nature of this Mind and its power in his *Platonic Theology*, as will also be demonstrated in the second book; of which he speaks thus: <term>Those who produce the multitude of souls from another older principle, and set down the Mind as the leader of the whole, say the best end is the union of the soul with the Mind.</term> <note>original Greek: οἳ δὲ καὶ τὰ τῶν ψυχῶν... τῆς ψυχῆς ἕνωσιν</note> These are the words of Proclus, reporting the decrees of certain other Platonists. Than which nothing is more venerable, nothing more celestial. That the Mind is the fountain, the origin of souls, and the greater principle, and that it is the prince and commander of all, as is found in the *Epistle* and in the *Philebus* <note>Dialogues of Plato</note>. Moreover, that the best end is for the soul to be united and joined with the principal Mind. For this is also our Theology: that it is necessary for us to be associated and made one with Him, if we wish to be blessed. Proclus also continues, declaring these same things better concerning the three principles, and what Platonic Theology feels:

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<margin>A'</margin> Of bodies, as has been said, and of souls, he declares the Mind <note>original: "νοῦν" (nous)</note> to be the father and cause; and that all things exist around it, and because of it, and for its sake—even those things which possess certain transitions and unfoldings through life. The Theology of Plato, says Proclus, pronounces the Mind to be the father and cause of bodies and souls: and that all things exist and operate around it, which possess life in their processes and developments. So he says. Observe, therefore, what Proclus now says the Platonic Theology feels concerning the Mind. For he hints at the *Letter to Dionysius* concerning the three kings, and that the Mind is the second of those commanders and three principles, around which Plato confesses all things to be. And thus it is the Daughter of the Supreme Good: since the Supreme Good is the first king. And he is that first king and father of the cause, and of the prince in the Letter. Moreover, the cause and the prince is the Mind.

## That the Mind is God, and the maker of the world: and that the ancient philosophers of Greece recognized this: and concerning the Ideas.

### CHAPTER XII.

<margin>B</margin> That which Plato said in the *Philebus*—that not only Anaxagoras, but almost all the elders, especially the wiser ones, gave the Mind the sovereignty and the invention of things, and declared it to be God and the cause—is testified not only by so many things above, but also by those whom Plutarch records as having emerged as noble in Philosophy. And first concerning Anaxagoras. He says that bodies consisted from the beginning: *but the divine mind set them in order, and produced the generations of all things* <note>original: "νοῦς δὲ θεοῦ αὐτὰ διεκόσμησε, καὶ τὰς γενέσεις τῶν ὅλων ἐποίησε"</note>. That the divine Mind adorned these things, and made the seeds and natures of all. Therefore, what Anaxagoras felt had been handed down by the earlier Thales. Thales, he says, perceives *the Mind to be the God of the world* <note>original: "νοῦν τὸ κόσμου θεὸν"</note>: the Mind or Intelligence of the World is God. Pythagoras likewise recognized this intelligence, or most divine Mind, according to the same author, so that he called God the *Monad* <note>original: "Μονάδα"</note>, that is, Unity: and *the Good itself* <note>original: "τὸ ἀγαθὸν"</note>, which is the nature of unity. This was previously brought forth by Hermes. Indeed, Trismegistus <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Egyptian sage</note> in the work entitled *The Mixing Bowl* (which name and subject Plato imitated in the *Timaeus*) said: *The unity is the principle and root of all things: and it shall be the principle: but without a principle nothing exists; the unity being the principle contains every number, being contained by none: and it generates every number, being generated by no other number* <note>original: "ἡ μονὰς πάντων ὅσα ἀρχὴ καὶ ῥίζα..."</note>. Unity, he says, is the principle and root of all. And since it is the principle (for without a principle nothing is made), it contains every number, contained by none: and it begets every number, begotten by no other number. This Monad, or Unity, which Trismegistus—the Egyptian Theologian and far more ancient than the Greeks—taught to Pythagoras, the same Pythagoras called *the Mind itself* <note>original: "αὐτὸς ὁ νοῦς"</note>, because it is Mind itself. Even Democritus, who otherwise raved much, called *the Mind God: the fiery soul of the world* <note>original: "νοῦν τὸν θεὸν : ἐμπεπυροσθῆι τὴν τοῦ κόσμου ψυχὴν"</note>. In saying that the fiery soul of the world is God, Democritus had this from the Chaldeans, who earlier had called God "fire" in the sentences of the followers of Zoroaster: *All things are born from one fire* <note>original: "εἰσὶ πάντα πυρὸς ἑνὸς ἐκγεγαῶτα"</note>. Our own scriptures also call the invisible fire God. From it all things were thus born, according to the Chaldeans and Democritus, because it is the soul of the world. Plutarch says that Socrates and Plato felt similarly to Trismegistus: *The One of a singular nature, born of itself, the singular, the truly good; all these names hasten toward the Mind. God being Mind is a separate form: that is, unmixed with any matter, being in no way entangled with the passible* <note>original: "τὸ ἕν τὸ μονοφυές... τὸ ἀμιγὲς πάσης ὕλης"</note>. The One itself of a singular nature, born through itself, the singular, true good is God: and these names look toward the Mind itself. Therefore, Mind is God, a separate form: that is, contaminated and thickened by no matter, mixed with no perishable thing. Behold what Anaxagoras said above, and what Aristotle reported of him. The Stoics also, who might think the world and the stars to be Gods, just as Aristotle and Plato did, nevertheless called *the Mind in the ether the highest and supreme of all Gods* <note>original: "τὸν ἀνωτάτω πάντων νοῦν ἐν αἰθέρι"</note>. From this it

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the divine Mind and the nature of Ideas, linking Platonic thought with ancient Chaldean, Egyptian, and Hebrew theology.</meta>

# AUGUSTINUS STEUCHUS OF GUBBIO, BOOK I.

It is clear what was intended by those who attributed divinity to the stars or the world—not, of course, that ineffable, supreme, and primary divinity who is the inventor of all things, but rather a certain divine quality created by God himself. Plutarch also relates that Socrates and Plato perceived Ideas <gloss>existing in the thoughts and mental images of God, that is, subsisting in the Mind</gloss> <note>original Greek: ἐν τοῖς νοήμασι, καὶ ἐν ταῖς φαντασίαις τοῦ θεοῦ, τουτέσι τοῦ νοῦ ὑφεςῶσας</note>. Thus, God is the Mind, the creator of the world, who contains the Idea of the world within himself. This passage and declaration by Plutarch is admirable and should be noted, so that you may know both what Plato thought of Ideas and in what place it is proper to hold them. They are, as Dionysius <note>Likely Dionysius the Areopagite, a Christian Neoplatonist</note> rightly and wisely defined them, <gloss>the pre-definitions and wills of God</gloss> <note>original Greek: προεκθέματα καὶ θελήματα θεοῦ</note>. Psellus also relates that the ancient Chaldeans held this view; by his testimony, you will recognize that the things brought forward by earlier writers belong to the Chaldeans, just as they also belonged to Hermes Trismegistus. <gloss>They honor one principle of all things, and hymn it as both the One and the Good; they consider Ideas to be sometimes the thoughts of the Father, sometimes universal reasons—natural, psychic, and intellectual—and sometimes the hidden essences of beings</gloss> <note>original Greek: μίαν ἀρχὴν τῶν πάντων δοξάζουσιν... κ.τ.λ.</note>. The Chaldeans, he says, believe in one principle of all things, calling it the Unity and the Good in their praise. They perceive Ideas sometimes as the thoughts of the Father, sometimes as the universal reasons of nature, mind, and soul, and sometimes as existences separated from things.

I say, therefore, following the opinion of Plutarch and Dionysius: according to the former, Plato's opinion was that Ideas are that primary concept of the Chaldeans—the thoughts and meditations of the Father, that is, of the worldly craftsman; according to the latter, Ideas are the very wills of God. If you add the second meaning—that they are natural and universal reasons in nature, and in minds and the soul—it will be correct, and nearly the same as the first. However, just as Plato, Aristotle, and Dionysius rejected the "ultimate" Ideas <note>referring to Ideas as independent, self-subsisting entities outside the divine mind</note>, exercising choice in the Chaldean dogmas, it is clear to everyone that these should be utterly rejected. Therefore, the dogma of certain Platonists must be considered an error, a fable, and a fabrication: <margin>D</margin> <gloss>the Intelligibles</gloss> <note>original Greek: τὰς νοητὰς</note>, that is, the Ideas which Plato placed in the mind of the craftsman, being a certain most divine and primary race of Gods, like a primary Hierarchy of Angels. Likewise, the "unities of things" and "separate Ideas" as sources and origins. This is a fabrication alien not only to the truth but also to the mind of Plato, as evidenced by Plutarch’s testimony which leads toward the clarity of reason.

Likewise, it is worthwhile and most worthy of knowledge to observe from Plutarch that the divine Mind and the Ideas themselves are the same thing, as Philo and Plotinus taught: that His Wisdom is the nature and substance of all Ideas, and that the entire series of Ideas is the same as the immense wisdom of God itself. It should also be noted that this Mind was the creator for all philosophers because they attributed the origins of things to its Ideas. From this, it is clear that they perceived and spoke of things they had learned from the "barbarians" <note>In this context, 'barbarians' refers to non-Greek ancient civilizations like the Hebrews and Egyptians</note>. The earlier Barbarians—the Chaldeans, Egyptians, and those whom it is right to place in the first rank, the Hebrews—preached this Mind as the creator of things, placing before it a certain other parent of its own. Regarding this, the witness for the Chaldeans is the sect of Zoroastrians and Magi along with the Sibyls; for the Egyptians, Trismegistus; for the Hebrews, Philo (among many others); for the Greeks, especially Plato and the entire family of Platonists; and earlier still, Orpheus. Among all these, you will find with one voice the Father of that eternal, omnipotent, and all-creating Mind. This I have partly shown, and shall partly show further.

-> *From Proclus and Porphyry concerning the Mind, and the Parent preceding it.* <-

### CHAPTER XIII.

Concerning this same Mind, not only as the craftsman of things but also as the second principle having a prior one before it, the Platonists speak many other things in agreement, handing down this Theology from one to another. For Proclus, in the aforementioned book where he discusses the Theology of Plato as it is handed down in the *Parmenides*, expresses many doubts and says these things:

<summary>Steuchus argues that the Platonic "Ideas" are actually the thoughts and wills of God, a concept he traces back to ancient Chaldean, Egyptian, and Hebrew sources. He asserts that the divine Mind is the seat of these Ideas and serves as the secondary creative principle under a supreme Father.</summary>

--- Page 49 ---
ON PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<margin>A</margin> [Proclus] says: *but if the ancients, and those who share in the philosophy of Plotinius, should say that the intellectual nature is born here, subsisting from the super-substantial principle of all beings, and that all the conclusions encompassed through this nature strive to harmonize with the one and most perfect power of the Mind:* <note>original Greek: αλλ’ ει μεν παλαιοι... συναρμοζειν υποχειρουσι</note> But the ancients, he says, and those who have been initiated into the Philosophy of Plotinus, say in the *Parmenides* <note>Plato's dialogue on the nature of the One</note> that the intelligible nature subsists from the one super-substantial principle of things, and they strive to join all the conclusions which are set forth through this nature to the one and most perfect power of the Mind. Behold, Proclus testifies that the Philosophy of Plotinus studied this above all: to show that the intelligible nature, the invisible substances, and the choirs of Angels proceed from one substantial principle. Moreover, he teaches a little later that it is such a great principle: namely the Mind, to whose ineffable power they attempt to recall all the conclusions which are in Plato’s *Parmenides*, and about which everyone differs so much; so that whatever Plato spoke of there pertains to the Mind and the intelligible orders, being born from the super-substantial principle, the Mind. And this is what is in our Theology: through Him were created things visible and invisible, whether thrones, or powers, or virtues, or dominions. This is called by the Platonists the *intellectual nature*, <note>original Greek: νοερα φυσις</note> and it is said to be born from the super-substantial principle by the ineffable and omnipotent authority of the Mind. The same Proclus claims that among the older Platonists (so that you may recognize their divinity) *it was also accustomed to be rumored among them, that the first in theology is Unity, but the Mind is one-many, and the Soul is one and many:* <note>original Greek: και παρ’ αυτοις ειωθε θρυλλεισθαι... την δε ψυχην εν και πολλα</note> Among them it used to be commonly said that the primacy of all things is Unity, but the Mind itself is "one-many," while the Soul is "One and many." These names are therefore symbolic and mathematical, by which they signified more divine things, designating the Three deities—and those who in the [Platonic] Epistle are the three kings—by mathematical names. The first is Unity, the Father, who is before all things, and for that reason is Unity and the Prin-

<margin>B</margin> ciple. But the Mind, being born from Him, is "One-many": One because it retains the paternal nature; Many because it acts according to number. For it is the second. From it, number begins. In the third place is the third king, or the third [principle], called "One and many," with the conjunction added, so that they might designate him as being further from the first according to number: because he is in the second place [from the Mind]. Indeed, the conjunction would not signify truth in him because it would make a third, or a number. But when the two are separated, there is one on each side. The third is "one and many": because on one hand he proceeds from the first, and therefore retains his nature and name; on the other hand, existing in the third place, he is also "many": because if you separate the three, unity remains on one side, and a multitude—namely two—remains on the other. But let us leave behind these excessively mathematical names. This much is clear: they established the Mind in the second place, after its Father; and the older Platonists handed this down. And Proclus brings forward from Porphyry *in the Patriarchal Treatise, he demonstrates with many and noble arguments that the Mind is eternal, yet it nevertheless has within itself the pre-eternal Mind, joining it to God. That [first] one was beyond all age; but the eternal holds the second, or rather the third rank from that one. For it is necessary, I think, for eternity itself to be established in the middle of the pre-eternal and the eternal:* <note>original Greek: εν τη πατριαρχικω πραγματεια... μεσον ιδρυθαι</note> Porphyry, he says, in the book where he treats of the Patriarchs—that is, the first authors of things—proves by many and excellent reasons that the Mind is eternal; yet it has in itself that which is pre-eternal to the Mind (to translate the word thus) connecting it to Unity. That [Unity] was indeed beyond every age; but the eternal holds the second, or rather the third order from it. For it is necessary, I believe, to place eternity itself as the middle between the pre-eternal and the eternal. Then Proclus adds (but let us not speak of this): *nevertheless, let us take this much from what has been said, that even the Mind has something better in itself than eternity:* <note>original Greek: τοσονδε δε ομως... κρειττον εν εαυτω του αιωνος</note> Let us accept this much, he says, from what has been said: that even the Mind has something better within itself than eternity. From these things, although they are spoken obscurely and in the manner of the Platonists, this meaning is nonetheless produced: that the Mind is everlasting.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the relationship between the Father (Unity/the Good) and the Son (Mind/Being) within the framework of Platonic theology, specifically addressing the concept of eternity.</meta>

# AUGUSTINUS EUGUBINUS, BOOK I.

However, it must have something that precedes it—not in time or in eternity (for eternal things are equal), but in nature. Thus, while there are two eternal beings, one is the generator of the other; the one who begets is necessarily truly eternal, and in a sense more so than the other. For the First Himself <note>The Father or the One</note> is before all eternity, not by time, but by nature. The Son Himself, being eternal, holds the second place; or rather, since between the eternal and the pre-eternal there lies an age or eternity, the "eternal" is necessarily in the third place. But you should know these things are said concerning the supreme excellence of the Father: because since He begat the eternal Mind <note>The Logos or Son</note>, He cannot but be pre-eternal Himself—that is, preceding eternity itself. And Porphyry <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher, c. 234–305 AD</note> himself testified that the Mind possesses something better than eternity. Therefore, these things are said to show that by which the Begetter precedes the Begotten, just as He Himself taught when He proclaimed His Father was greater than He, and referred all received goods back to Him. The Platonists, unable to explain this better, used the names "Eternal" and "Pre-eternal," the first of which belongs to the Mind, and the second to Him who precedes the Mind by nature. Declaring their conformity and the supreme conjunction of their nature, they say the Eternal—namely the Mind itself—possesses something better than eternity, specifically that it reaches toward the nature of the First. They also say the Mind itself contains the Pre-eternal within it—that is, a nature before time and before the ages—by which it is coupled with Unity, which is the Father Himself: the first being, the primacy, the fountain of all things from which all numbers proceed, and before all things, the Son Himself. Finally, this is the whole of that Theology which we celebrate daily: that the Mind and offspring of God is co-eternal with its Father.

->From the same Proclus, concerning the Begetter, the Supreme Good, the divine offspring, the Mind; likewise concerning the third, the Soul or Spirit; and concerning the divinity of the Mind itself.<-

### CHAPTER XIV.

Proclus <note>A major Neoplatonist philosopher, 412–485 AD</note> also spoke divinely concerning this, explaining both the Theology of Plato and the decrees of other Platonists. By his testimony, one learns both how much Unity itself excels the Mind, and again how great a conjunction exists between that Mind and Unity itself. In that very book on Platonic Theology, he says: *Substance and the Mind are said to subsist primarily from the Good, and to have their existence around the Good, and to be filled with the light of truth proceeding from there, and by the appropriate participation to attain it from the union of this light; whence that which belongs to Mind and Substance is more divine, as it depends primarily upon the Good.* <note>original Greek: "ἡ οὐσία καὶ ὁ νοῦς, ἀπὸ τοῦ ἀγαθοῦ πρώτως ὑφεστάναι λέγεται, καὶ περὶ τὸ ἀγαθὸν ὑπάρ-χειν ἔχειν, καὶ πληροῦσθαι τοῦ τῆς ἀληθείας φωτὸς ἐκεῖθεν προϊόντος, καὶ τῇ ἐπιβαλλούσῃ αὐτῆς μεθέξει ἐκ τῆς ἑνώσεως τοῦ φωτὸς τούτου τυγχάνειν, ὅθεν τὸ νοῦ, καὶ ἡ οὐσία ἐστὶ θεότερον, ὡς τοῦ ἀγαθοῦ πρώ-τως ἐξηρτημένον."</note> These are his words: Substance and Mind are said to subsist from the Good both principally and primarily, and to have their existence around the Good, and to be filled with the light of truth flowing from there, and to receive participation or dependence from the conjunction and union of the light, which is more divine than even the Mind and substance, as that which depends primarily on the Good. Thus he spoke: than which nothing more profound, grand, or sublime can be uttered by any Theology. Moreover, among the Platonists, it is a most high dogma that Substance (or Being) and the Mind itself are the same; likewise, the Good and Unity are the same. Rightly, then, are Mind and Substance (or Being) the same; for even that Mind, speaking to Moses, the most ancient of Theologians, said: *I am who I am* <note>Exodus 3:14</note>; in Greek, *ho on* <note>The Being</note>; to the Philosophers, *to on* <note>That which is</note>. And Dionysius <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite</note> calls Jesus *to on* (Being). Now, Jesus is the Son of God, the Mind itself. He is the first and most excellent Being, born primarily and principally from the Father, the Supreme Good and Unity. Therefore, just as Substance and Mind are names of the second principle proceeding from the Father, so the Good itself, or the Supreme Good, and likewise Unity, are names of the first principle. Unity indeed, because from it all things are born, even the Mind itself, the head and fountain of all. Proclus says...

It identifies the biblical "I Am" and the person of Jesus with the philosophical concept of the "Mind" or "Being" that proceeds from the ultimate Unity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Perennial Philosophy," specifically focusing on the Neoplatonic understanding of the Divine Mind (Nous) and its relationship to the Supreme Good (The One), drawing parallels with Christian scripture.</meta>

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<margin>A</margin> Proclus <note>A major Neoplatonic philosopher (5th century AD) who sought to synthesize Greek philosophy into a single system.</note> states, and it is a most constant report among all Theologians, that from the Supreme Good itself, primarily, first, and before all things, the Mind <note>original: "Mentem"; referring to the Divine Intellect or *Nous*</note> subsists; and from Him, and around Him, it has its existence, revolving around Him: because unless He existed, the Mind itself would not be. From Him it is born and depends, as the Mind itself confessed while in the world. From Him also, it says, it is filled with the light of truth flowing from that source. For the divine offspring is eternal light, light from light, and is the wisdom of its father. And for this reason it cried out: *My doctrine is not mine, but His who sent me* <note>John 7:16</note>; and when it said that it was glorified by its Father with eternal glory before the world was made. The Platonists signify all this by many names: dependence, participation, existence from Him and around Him, and light from Him. And as for what he says—that this light which belongs to the Father is more divine than the Mind—you should understand him to mean the pre-eminence of the Father, and that He is the first being and the fountain-head of the eternal light (which is the very birth of His offspring, or rather the offspring itself). For the paternal excellence cannot be designated otherwise. But hear even more wonderful things, so that you might say the heavens were opened to them, and that they saw the glory of the everlasting God. For all this Theology of Proclus belongs to those who came before him, the Platonists, so that I am stunned, marveling at our own radiant theology found among them: <term>to declare that which is second in order after the transcendent excellence of the One. If, therefore, our mind is also an image of the primary Mind, and according to this alone the Good, and if through the mind a participation in self-sufficiency and our own good is present to us: yet our mind is separated from the Good, and is needy by nature, and for this reason requires both pain and pleasure. But the divine Mind always participates in the Good, and is united to it, and because of this it is God, being good-form according to its participation in the Good, but God as being joined to the primary Deity.</term> <note>original Greek: "τὴν τῇ τάξει δευτέραν..."</note> He says that according to the order of Platonic Theology, the Mind is placed after the separate excellence of Unity. If, therefore, our mind is also an image of the first Mind, then also according to this great good, and to us

<margin>B</margin> according to the participation of the Mind, sufficiency and our own good may be present. But our mind is disjoined from the good, and is lacking, and therefore even lacking in pleasure for human perfection. But the divine Mind always participates in the good, and is united with it, and because of this it is divine. It is indeed similar to the Supreme Good itself, according to the participation of the Good: but truly divine, as that which is connected with the primary Deity. These are the words of Proclus. Behold the second order and the place of the Mind after the highest and most eminent excellence and super-eminence of Unity. Behold also our mind, the image of the divine Mind. For He made us in His image and likeness. Behold, there is no access for us to the Father, no communication of the Good, except through the first-born Mind, since our mind is far disjoined from the Supreme Good itself. Behold also the divine Mind, the Mind of God, the paternal Mind, as the Chaldean <note>Likely referring to the *Chaldean Oracles*, a foundational text for later Neoplatonists.</note> used to say, <term>good-form</term> <note>original: "ἀγαθοειδής"; having the form or character of the Good</note>, similar to the Supreme Good, bearing the likeness and form of the Supreme Good, its Father. Therefore it said: *He who sees me, sees also my Father.* <note>John 14:9</note> Behold God the Father Himself, the primary Divinity itself. Finally, behold what Saint John says: *And the Word was with God.* <note>John 1:1</note> Which speech indeed sounds like what Proclus says: <term>He was united to Him</term> <note>original: "συνήνωται πρὸς αὐτὸ"</note>, He was joined with the Good itself. John says: <term>He was toward God</term> <note>original: "ἦν πρὸς τὸν θεὸν"</note>; Proclus says: <term>around the Good itself</term> <note>original: "περὶ αὐτὸ ἀγαθὸν"</note>. And John says: *He was with God*; Proclus says: *Joined, united with Him.* When John says, *He was with God*, he designates that He was joined, He clung to God, He was inseparable from God, co-eternal. Whom John calls God, Proclus calls the Good itself, and also God. What John likewise calls the <term>Word</term> <note>original: "λόγον"; Logos</note>, Reason, or Word: Proclus calls the <term>Mind</term> <note>original: "νοῦν"; Nous</note>. Moreover, Reason and Mind are the same. Reason is the wisdom of the highest God. Note, I pray, burn into your mind, fix there that word <term>united</term> <note>original: "συνήνωται"</note>; she is joined, "one-made" (if I may speak thus), a word breaking forth from the Philosophers against the Arians <note>Followers of Arius who argued that Jesus Christ was a created being, separate from and subordinate to God the Father.</note> and others who disjoined the Mind from the Supreme Good. The Mind, he says, is joined, united with the Supreme Good. This Proclus takes from Plato; this all of them say.

<summary>This page argues that Neoplatonic philosophy, specifically the work of Proclus, mirrors Christian doctrine regarding the Trinity. It equates the Platonic "Mind" (Nous) with the Christian "Word" (Logos), asserting that both traditions view this second principle as eternally united with the Supreme Good or God the Father.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Neoplatonic hierarchies, specifically focusing on the relationship between the One (the Good), the Mind (Intellect), and the Soul, as interpreted by Augustinus Steuchus (Eugubinus) through the works of Proclus and Plotinus.</meta>

# AUGUSTINUS EUGUBINUS, BOOK I.

But if Proclus should say that the excellence of the supreme Good is even "set apart" <gloss>original: ἐξῃρημένω, meaning removed or transcendent</gloss> from the Mind: <margin>C</margin> you should know that he intends by this that majesty and most high citadel of divinity, not a separation. For if he felt otherwise, behold, he is refuted by his own words: the Mind is "united" <gloss>original: συνώνωται, meaning made one, unified, joined, or connected</gloss> with the supreme Good; nor would he be correctly explaining Plato. Concerning the Mind, it must also be considered what the same Proclus pronounces according to the opinion of the other Platonists:

-> "The Mind, which has its life in eternity and its essence in eternity, is itself eternity in its activity, embracing all thought together as standing in the 'now'; it is deiform <note>literally 'rhythmic' or 'ordered' in the Greek, but Steuchus translates it as 'Deifica' or God-like</note> because of the Cause of the universe which is before it. For it has two activities, as Plotinus says: some as Mind, and others as if 'drunk with nectar'; and in other respects the Mind is also God. It is God by virtue of that which is around it, just as the soul is Mind by its own supra-soulful excellence, and the body becomes Soul by the power that is before the body. All things, however, as we have said, hang from the One through the Mind and the Soul. And the Mind is 'One-form' <note>having the form of the One</note>, while the Soul is 'Mind-form'. And God, who is before the Mind, first echoes upon the mental nature. The Mind is most divine, as it is deified before all others. The Soul is divine insofar as it requires the mediation of the Mind." <-

Which is to say: the Mind has its life in eternity, and its substance and operation of understanding is eternity itself, having all intellection fixed in the present at once. It is Deiform, that is, similar to God, divine because of the cause of the universe which exists before it. For it has two operations, as Plotinus testifies: the first, as Mind; the second, as if drunk with nectar. And in other matters it is indeed as Mind; but in that which is before itself, it is as God. Just as the soul, by its excellence above every soul, is Mind; so the body, by the power which is before the body, becomes Soul. All things therefore, as we have said, depend upon the One, through the Mind and the Soul. And the Mind is indeed Uniform <note>possessing the form of the One</note>, but the Soul is "Mind-form," if I may speak thus. And God, who is before the Mind, first presides over the mental nature. The Mind, however, is most divine, as that which is deified before all others. But the Soul is divine, insofar as it requires the middle-ground of the Mind. These things are found in Proclus—mysteries as admirable as those above—so that it is a wonder by what instinct they arrived at these truths.

They say there are three principles: <margin>D</margin> God (or the Good, or Unity), Mind, and Soul. Three emperors: the first is the Good, the second is the Mind, the third is the Soul. The Mind, he says, is everlasting: eternal wisdom, eternal understanding, everlasting knowing. This Mind is, or is begotten as, God by the preceding cause, its Begetter, who is properly and primarily called "God" among us and among them. *Blessed be the God and Father of our Lord Jesus Christ.* *God glorified his Son.* *God sent his Son in the likeness of sinful flesh.* *Whom God set forth as a propitiation through faith in his blood.* *Nothing shall separate us from the love of God, which is in Christ Jesus.* And innumerable other examples.

The Mind, therefore, holds that divinity and eternal wisdom from the preceding cause. It is indeed itself God, but from that Begetter of its own. Whatever it is, it is eternally. It has two operations: one as Mind, as the principle of things, as understanding, moderating, and producing all things which it created, looking "behind" itself; the other as looking "above" itself toward its Begetter, enjoying from Him eternal pleasure, drunk with divine nectar, blessed by love and sweet charity toward its Father, whom it looks up to and always admires. From Him it is deified; it becomes and is God, just as it is, because it was begotten by Him, as Macrobius used to say.

The Soul is indeed the third principle, acting as Mind because of the conjunction of substance, and therefore it is also God, because it is from the first through the second, and from the second. Bodies, however, are the effects of the three principles. Finally, all things are from Unity. For the Mind is begotten from Unity; from there is its pleasure and its divinity. Therefore it is "most divine," since it alone is begotten by God, and having been begotten, is God. Other things participate in divinity, and for that reason they are not "most divine." This name "Most Divine" is found in Plato and Aristotle. Plato calls the Word of God "most divine"; Aristotle calls the Mind "most divine," as you shall see. The Soul is also God, through both, but especially through the second. And the Father, as Dionysius says, since he is the Father of lights, radiates into every nature...

<summary>Steuchus aligns Neoplatonic philosophy with Christian theology, arguing that the "Mind" (Intellect) described by Proclus and Plotinus corresponds to the Son (the Word) begotten by God the Father. He explores the hierarchy of the One, Mind, and Soul, emphasizing their interconnectedness and divine participation.</summary>
<keywords>Neoplatonism, Holy Trinity, Proclus, Plotinus, Mind (Nous), Soul (Psyche), The One, Divine Nectar, Deification, Participation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Perennial Philosophy," specifically focusing on how ancient Neoplatonists like Proclus and Porphyry intuited the Christian doctrine of the Trinity (Father, Son/Mind, and Holy Spirit/Soul).</meta>

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<margin>A</margin>...the Angelic nature. These things they have said, drawn partly from Plato and partly from our own Theology. For these are the very things which the Greeks and Latins defined: that the Spirit—that is, the Soul—and the third principle, exist eternally from the first and the second. The first is called by our writers, and by theirs, the Father; though they specifically call him Unity and the Good. By our writers, he is likewise called the source and principle of the entire Deity <note>original: "Deitatis." In this context, it refers to the Godhead or the divine nature shared by the Trinity.</note>. I say that they claimed the Soul, or Spirit, has its essence and its subsisting being from the Father and the Son together, and proceeds eternally from both at once as if from a single principle. The Greeks say the Son is the "cause" of the Soul, while the Latins say the "principle," just as he is the prior one. For all things that belong to the Father, or the Good, the Father himself gave to his only-begotten Son in begetting him, except for the fact of being the Father. And because the Soul or Spirit proceeds from the Mind or the Son, it held that Mind from the Father eternally, by whom it was eternally begotten; and the Father is the source and principle of the entire Deity. For behold, they themselves say that all things depend on the One, but through the mediation of the Mind and the Soul. Indeed, since the Father is the source and principle of the entire Deity, he first effects that the Son and the Soul are God—the one by begetting, the other by producing. For, as Proclus <note>A 5th-century Neoplatonist philosopher who heavily influenced later Christian mysticism.</note> testifies, even the Soul is God—not as a "fountain-source" like the first, who is properly God, but as produced by him. Therefore, he calls it "divine" rather than "God," and notes that it proceeds from the first, or is given through the medium of the second. Let us therefore give thanks to the most high God, who compelled even those unskilled in His religion, drawn away by a rapid ecstasy of the mind, to speak for it and to offer such testimonies about it that there is no one who would not marvel, be struck senseless, or, if he is pious, shed tears for joy.

->*The wonderful testimony of Porphyry concerning the same Mind begotten from eternity.*<-
->CHAPTER XV.<-

<margin>B</margin><image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'E' featuring floral and architectural elements within a square frame.</image-desc>THE same tears will flow, and the same joys will seize anyone who reads what Porphyry <note>A 3rd-century scholar and student of Plotinus; though often a critic of Christianity, the author here finds Christian truths in his philosophy.</note> wrote. Indeed, what Proclus has now spoken and proposed—as if a celestial Angel had taught him from heaven—the earlier Platonists handed down even more divinely and majestically. Porphyry, therefore, in the fourth book of his *History of Philosophy* (a passage which Cyril <note>Cyril of Alexandria, who wrote "Against Julian" to defend Christianity.</note> brings forward in its entirety against the Emperor Julian <note>Known as "the Apostate," he attempted to revive paganism; the author argues Julian was blind to the fact that his own beloved philosophy supported Christian doctrine.</note>, whose blind madness and hatred of truth would not let him see the truth, nor perceive that the religion he despised was the very one the ages and the secrets of all philosophy had worshipped, and which he ignorantly persecuted), wrote stupendous miracles concerning the "Supreme Good" according to the opinion of Plato: <note>The following is a translation of the Greek passage provided in the text.</note> "From this [the Good], in a manner inconceivable to men, was generated the Mind <note>Greek: νοῦς (Nous), often translated as Intellect or the Second Person of the Trinity in this context.</note>, the whole, subsisting by itself; in which are the things that truly exist, and the entire substance of beings. This is the first Beauty, and Beauty-itself, having its beauty from itself as its form. It proceeded before the ages, or eternity, springing forth from God as its cause, being self-begotten and its own father. For its procession did not happen because God moved toward its generation, but because this Mind proceeded self-generatively from God; and it proceeded not from any temporal beginning, for time did not yet exist. Nor, when time did come into being, was time relevant to it. For the Mind is always timeless and alone eternal. Just as God is the First, and is always alone even if all things are created from him (because he is not numbered with them, nor can their dignity be ranked with his existence), so also the Mind is alone eternal, subsisting timelessly; and those things which are in time, he himself is their 'time' in the identity of his own subsistence." These words mean: Plato hands down that from this Supreme Good, in a way unknown to men, was begotten the Mind, which exists entirely through itself: in which clearly are the true things, and all the substance of things: which is both the primary beauty and beauty in itself, and possesses the form of beauty. It proceeded before the ages, or eternity, flowing forth from God the author, existing as self-begotten, and as its own Father. For there was no process of the Father moving himself toward the generation of this [Mind], but rather it progressed by its own generative power, or from its generation from God: and progressing not from any temporal beginning: for not yet...

<summary>The author argues that ancient Neoplatonists like Proclus and Porphyry recognized the Christian Trinity (Father, Son/Mind, and Holy Spirit/Soul) through reason and "ecstasy." He cites Porphyry to show that the "Mind" is eternally begotten from the "Good," mirroring the relationship between God the Father and the Son.</summary>
<keywords>Trinity, Neoplatonism, Proclus, Porphyry, Mind (Nous), Soul, Eternal Generation, Emperor Julian, Cyril of Alexandria, Perennial Philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological exploration of the "Divine Mind" (Mens) and its relationship to the Father (Genitor), drawing parallels between Neoplatonic philosophy and Christian doctrine regarding the eternal generation of the Son.</meta>

# AUGUSTINUS EUGUBINUS, BOOK I.

For there was no time; but even when time was created, it is nothing compared to that Mind. For the Mind is always
<margin>C</margin>
lacking time <note>original: "carens tempore"; meaning existing outside the constraints of linear time</note>, and is the only eternal Mind. Moreover, just as that first God is one and singular always—even though all things were made by Him—because these created things cannot be numbered with Him, nor placed in the same rank as His own substance: so also the Mind is alone everlasting and without time. And when these things which exist in time were created, time itself began, while the Mind remained in the unity of its own substance. Porphyry <note>A Neoplatonic philosopher (c. 234–305 AD) who, despite being a critic of Christianity, is often cited by Renaissance theologians for his insights into the divine hierarchy.</note> testifies that Plato sensed these oracles sent from the cave of divinity and these ineffable mysteries. I cannot be filled with enough wonder at what divine "North Winds" (so to speak) carried them into heaven to look into the highest sacraments: except for the fact that now, with the Gospel shining forth, they were better able to explain those things which Plato, Hermes Trismegistus, the Sibyls, the Magi, and so many oracles throughout the whole world had spoken.

Therefore, entering at the beginning into the vast sea of Theology, he reports that from this Supreme Good—which has now often been clear is the name of the Everlasting Father <note>original: "sempiterni Genitoris"</note>—the Mind is born in a way that is ineffable and inexplicable to all mortals. As our own Prophet similarly said: *Who shall declare his generation?* <note>A reference to Isaiah 53:8, often used to describe the mysterious birth of Christ from the Father.</note> For whose mind could ever grasp the ineffable birth of the Mind? It is a miracle that they even recognized this much: that it is born, and that there are two, the Begetter and the Begotten. This is the first glory of this testimony: that it confesses this birth to be ineffable. The second is that the born Mind subsists through itself—being itself God and itself almighty. The third is that it is the home, seat, and origin of all things; the fountain and principle; the universal essence of things; the kingdom of true things and of the Ideas, which are the true essences; the vast mother. Thus it is the first beauty, the origin of all beauty, the primordial beauty, the exemplar of all beauty, carrying the beautiful world in its mind, being itself most beautiful. And therefore all things which it made were beautiful and good. This beauty, form, and "idea of loveliness," it holds from itself as its principle and origin.

<margin>D</margin>
To these points, add that it was born from God Himself eternally, and before the ages, begotten through itself before time. It is its own Father, as it were, because having been born, it is like its own principle and a father to itself, because being begotten by the Father, it was itself willing to be produced and born. Hence, even if it is born from its Father, God, it is nevertheless born ineffably, willing itself to be born, as if it were also the principle of its own birth—not by generating itself, but by being born conjointly and eternally from Him. For there is designated a certain ineffable union of the Begetter and the Begotten, an unheard-of kind of birth, and an incomprehensible manner. The logic of this birth is different from ours; we do not exist before we are born, nor do we have anything from ourselves, being different in substance from our parents. There, because the birth is ineffable, admirable, and of an entirely different kind, even though it is born, it is nevertheless co-equal and co-eternal with the father. It is said to be born itself, willing to be generated by the Father, so that by willing to be born, and ineffably exulting out of that birth, and being born inseparably from the father, it is almost a begetter to itself.

This is indeed a most high mystery, and a most powerful outcry against those who detract from the divinity of the Son <note>A reference to the Arian heresy, which denied that Jesus was of the same eternal substance as God the Father.</note>. Here, not only is nothing taken away, but almost too much is attributed. He then repeats that this generation is everlasting, before times, before the ages, when there was no time: so that even time, created afterward, is nothing compared to the Mind itself, which alone is everlasting and before all times, having no partnership with or dependence upon time. And just as the Father is everlasting and the one creator and author of all—because He is mixed with nothing and is reckoned far beyond all things left behind—so also the Mind is the sole eternal one of all things. It is even time itself, when it created things under time, and came to time itself (the measure of the world), about to establish time itself by which all things would be measured. Nothing could be said or thought higher, grander, more sublime, or deeper.
