<meta>This page continues the argument that ancient philosophy and Christian doctrine share a single, eternal source of truth. It specifically addresses the Platonists and the Jewish people, leading into a detailed examination of Plotinus.</meta>

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<margin>A</margin>O you Platonists! You did not realize that the Christian religion is the true patron of this wondrous Wisdom <note>The "Perennial Philosophy" or universal truth</note>, and that this religion is nothing other than the worship and knowledge of the Supreme Good and the Eternal Mind, and the science of their everlasting union. This religion speaks, cries out, and proclaims nothing else. What reproach do you deserve, and by what blindness were you captured, you who attacked the very faith whose admirable and stupendous mysteries you yourselves explain with the highest admiration? You even believe and assert that your leader, Plato, held these truths and left them in his writings. One must also pity the most wretched Hebrews, who, due to an unheard-of blindness and a profound darkness of the soul, suffer the same calamity. This Wisdom—which has been known through all ages since the rising of the world, which all nations have worshipped as revealed from heaven, and which the most learned of their own race committed to literary monuments—was hidden among all the scholars of their nation under secret mysteries. Yet now, they do not see it adored, worshipped, and acknowledged by the whole world. Therefore, this religion shall never fail, for it is that true Wisdom, the most ancient of all ages, a Theology hidden in secret mysteries.

->*From Plotinus concerning that same Mind, and its Begetter, and its union with the Father: and that after the Father, the Mind is the greatest of all things.*<-

### CHAPTER XVI.

<margin>B</margin>PLOTINUS, likewise the greatest among the Platonists and everywhere most celebrated, wonderfully approved all those things which were handed down by the ancients above. He discussed them so copiously that he seems to have held these mysteries of true Wisdom most perfectly. Indeed, this meeting and consensus of so many men and ages seems like a miracle. Let the Philosophers therefore hear what has been revealed in these most recent ages, which was even born before and understood by the minds of many men, but declared later and so widely diffused that what was previously contained in narrow places has escaped into the whole world. Now that Divinity is adored, and everyone philosophizes about Him, concerning whom only the wisest once did, and those were very few. For this is the "knowledge of the Lord" with which the whole earth is filled, because everyone now philosophizes, knowing, adoring, and always speaking of the Father and the Son—a knowledge that was previously held by only a few. 

And Plotinus—who was not a Christian, and perhaps did not even know of Christians—if he had known that they adored this Wisdom which his ancestors so greatly worshipped, and that they spoke of it as if from the deepest mysteries (the writings of which he himself venerated and approved), he certainly would have joined the other Platonists in adoring and retaining our religion. He would have proclaimed it to be true wisdom, though some of them, through ignorance, despised it as superstition. Plotinus, therefore, among other divine and sublime things, speaks thus in his book *On the Three Principal Hypostases* <note>original: "hypostafibus," referring to the three primary realities in Neoplatonism: The One, the Intellect, and the Soul</note>, which was later imitated and followed by Porphyry, Proclus, and many other Platonists: 

"What then must be said concerning the most perfect? Does nothing come from him, or do the greatest things come after him? The greatest after him is the Mind <note>original Greek: νοῦς (Nous)</note>, and it is second. For the Mind beholds him, and needs him alone; but he has no need of the Mind. And that which is generated from a superior Mind is itself Mind; and the Mind is greater than all things, because all other things come after it." <note>original: "τί οὖν χρὴ περὶ τοῦ τελειοτάτου λέγειν; μηδὲν ἀπ’ αὐτοῦ, ἢ τὰ μέγιστα μετ’ αὐτό; μέγιστον δὲ μετ’ αὐτὸν νοῦς, καὶ δεύτερον..."</note>

These are the mysteries of the innermost Philosophy which Plotinus proclaims; if he were a Christian, he could not have spoken more clearly. In the beginning, he says the Father is the most perfect—namely the principle, the greatest, the most eminent, and the most excellent.

<summary>The author argues that Christian doctrine is the fulfillment of ancient Platonism and Jewish mysticism, citing the Neoplatonist Plotinus to prove that the concept of the Trinity (Father and Mind/Son) was recognized by the greatest pagan philosophers.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, Platonism, Trinity, Eternal Mind, Perennial Philosophy, Neoplatonism, Greek philosophy, religious continuity</keywords>

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>The author continues his exploration of how ancient Neoplatonic philosophy aligns with Christian doctrine regarding the Trinity. This page focuses on the relationship between the Father (the Good) and the Son (the Mind/Logos).</meta>

# AUGUSTINUS EUGUBINUS, BOOK I.

preceding even the Mind <note>Latin: *Mens*. In this context, it refers to the Divine Intellect or the second person of the Trinity</note>. For this is what the Son said so often: <margin>C</margin> *The Father is greater than I.* <note>John 14:28</note> This is not only because of the body he assumed, but because the Father is the principle and source of the entire Deity; whereas the Son is begotten, being born, and is the offspring. Plotinus says that from the greatest and most perfect being, nothing is generated except the Mind; and that the greatest thing after the most perfect (the Father) is the Mind (the Son). And what is most wonderful of all, he says that the Mind looks up to and gazes upon the Father, and needs him alone, while the Father has no need of the Mind at all. 

For he reveals a most hidden mystery: that the Father begets the Son as if he himself were the greater authority, just as much as exists between the Begotten and the Begetter. And since the Father is the begetter and the first origin of all things, he does not need or depend upon the Son (the Mind); conversely, the son needs him alone, because he holds his divinity and substance from him, and learns all things from him. Nothing else is said here by Plotinus than what that same Mind often spoke among us mortals, teaching that he was sent by the Father, that he was his unique and most beloved offspring, and that he had nothing except what the Father had given him—whatever he possessed was handed down to him by the Father. 

Behold, Plotinus says the Son gazes upon the Father as the one upon whom his entire blessedness depends, as his own good and his own happiness. And rightly so. For if he holds such great divinity and such greatness of nature from him who is the greatest and most perfect—so that he was able to create the visible and invisible world—it is necessary that his blessedness be as great as that which makes his greatest son blessed. That is, the blessedness is as great as the magnitude of his works, his divinity, and his power. The Son gazes into the Father and is contained within the paternal bosom; his happiness is nothing other than the goods and knowledge of the Father, a treasury of the greatest delights. Therefore, he desired to raise all people up to these paternal goods. 

In addition to these things, Plotinus adds: <margin>D</margin> <note>original Greek: "ποθεῖ δὲ πᾶν τὸ γεννησαν..." translated below</note> *Everything that begets desires that which is begotten and loves it, especially when the begetter and the begotten are alone; and when the begetter is the best, it is necessarily joined with the begotten, so that they are separated only by "otherness." And we say that the Mind is his image. For one must speak more clearly. And because of this, according to Plato, all things are threefold:* He says, *All things are around the King of all, and second things around the second, and third things around the third.* <note>A reference to the pseudo-Platonic Second Letter, often cited by Renaissance scholars to prove the Trinity was known to ancients.</note> He also says that the Father has a cause, calling the Mind itself the cause. For the Mind is the <term>Artisan</term> <note>Latin: *Opifex*; the Demiurge or Creator</note> for him. Moreover, he says this one creates the soul in that "mixing bowl." <note>A reference to Plato's *Timaeus*</note> 

Therefore, since the Mind itself is the cause, he calls its Father "the Good," and that which is beyond Mind and beyond substance. In many places, however, he calls him Being, and Mind, and Idea; so that Plato recognized that from the Good exists the Mind, and from the Mind the Soul. These words are not at all new, nor have they just begun to be spoken, but were spoken long ago, albeit obscurely. The words now held on this matter are like interpreters of those ancient ones, attesting to the sayings of Plato as being ancient words. 

Saying such things word-for-word, Plotinus implies that whatever is now preached concerning the Father and the Son, the Mind, and the Good—from which the ancients said the Mind flows or is begotten—is <term>Ancient Theology</term> <note>Latin: *prisca theologia*; the idea of a single true theology existing through all ages</note>. Furthermore, he shows that the Good is beyond substance and beyond all things; in this, one must recognize the excellence of the supreme God and Begetter. For there is nothing more excellent or greater. He calls the same Good the "Begetter" and the Mind the "Begotten," and confesses them to be most closely joined because the begetter is the "best," and because the Begetter and the Begotten are alone. From this, he intends to show that there is an indissoluble union between them. For the greatest union comes from the greatest perfection. Observe, therefore, the divine and most profound Theo-
logy in Plotinus...

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<note>The author is discussing the theology of Plotinus, a 3rd-century philosopher, arguing that his concepts of "The Good" and "Mind" are actually the Christian Father and Son.</note> ...theology, concerning the eternal generation of the Son from the Father, and his union with Him, and concerning the blessedness of the same, arising from his gaze upon the Father. Finally, he says they are separated by "otherness" alone. A thing unheard of and amazing! By "otherness" alone, he says, the Good and the Mind are separated—in Greek *heterotēti* <note>original: ἑτερότητι; meaning "difference" or "otherness"</note>—what Christians would call a distinction of persons alone. For the Father is one [person], the Son is another, yet they are not a different "thing" or "substance." For so great is the perfection and perfect union between those two, as befits their divinity. Then, I pray you, look more closely at what Plotinus hands down. He says these mysteries, these very discourses, these supramundane sacraments, are ancient; and what is said now is not new, but is explained by these things handed down by the ancients. Listen, I pray, and turn your mind to those things which we spoke of both in the preface and throughout our progress: that the Philosophy we now hold existed also among the ancients. You hear Plotinus himself confessing that this entire Philosophy descends from the ancients. By this testimony is proved and confirmed whatever the Chaldeans, Hermes Trismegistus, Orpheus, the Sibyls, and all others had handed down concerning the divinity of the Father and the Son. They themselves testify to this; these things cannot have been fabricated by Christians after the birth of Christ. There are many other wonderful testimonies in Plotinus concerning the Father and the Son. Those whom our Theology calls more expressly and familiarly "Father" and "Son," they call "the Good" and "the Mind," "the Begetter" and "the Begotten," "the Image of the Father and the Good" (the Mind itself), and "the Good and Beyond-Substance" (the Father himself). Who would have known these things unless the celestial Philosophy had shone forth? Blessed, therefore, be the first-born, the only-begotten Son of the highest Good, through whom we were both created and redeemed, and loved to such an extent that he never concealed his deepest divinity, whose humanity, affability, clemency, kindness, and familiarity every land has felt in every age.

<margin>B</margin> <note>Printer's signature mark</note>

->From the same Plotinus: that the Mind is after the highest Good; that through it one reaches the highest; that it is the place and Idea of all beauty; and that it created the world.<-

### CHAPTER XVII.

Plotinus, likewise the chief of the Platonists, whose admirable genius is proclaimed by all—even though he flourished before Porphyry and Proclus, so that his testimonies ought to come first—nevertheless, following a certain order regarding that which they called the "Mind" and the "Offspring" of the highest Good, makes us eager to set these things forth. Indeed, various and manifold theology exists in his works, beyond the aforementioned, concerning those two: the Begetter and the Offspring begotten from eternity. From which, in his book *On Beauty*, he speaks these things as if from the innermost shrine of Philosophy: <note>The following is a translation of the Greek passage from Plotinus, Ennead I.6.9</note> *Let everyone first become godlike and beautiful if he intends to behold God and beauty. For he will come first, ascending to the Mind, and there he will know all beautiful forms, and he will say that this beauty consists of the Ideas. For all these are beautiful by the offspring and substance of the Mind. But beyond and above this, we speak of the nature of the Good, which has beauty set before it. Wherefore, in a general sense, it is the primary beauty; but if one distinguishes the intelligible realms, he will call the intelligible beauty the "place of forms." But the Good is that which is beyond, the fountain and principle of beauty; or he will place the Good and the primary beauty in the same [rank], except that beauty is there.* Thus far he speaks. Although these things are brought forth in an abstruse manner customary to the Plotinists, they nevertheless hold this meaning: You see first those two most holy and...

<margin>c</margin> <meta>...most high</meta> <note>Catchword for the next page</note>

<meta>This page continues the author's synthesis of Neoplatonic philosophy and Christian theology, specifically focusing on the hierarchy of the divine—the Good and the Mind—and how the human soul ascends to them.</meta>

# AUGUSTINUS EUGUBINUS, BOOK I.

...and the highest deities, the first of which in all ancient Philosophy is called the **Good**, and the second the **Mind** <note>Latin: *Mens*; often referring to the Divine Intellect or the Logos</note>. And he speaks of him who desires to see true beauty: it is necessary for him first to become **God-like** <note>Latin: *Deiformem*</note>, to be made like unto God, because he is never to be admitted to the true intuition of loveliness unless he takes upon himself a divine likeness. Moreover, this likeness—as Plato himself bears witness—is holiness and justice. It is necessary that he who is held by the ardor of beholding true beauty possess these things. And so, he who affects this blessedness, ascending in spirit to the summit of things, will make his first step toward the **Mind**; for this exists after the supreme **Good**. 

And the Mind itself used to say to men that it was the leader and the gate to the supreme Good; and that no one can see the Father unless the Son has led him and revealed Him <note>A clear Christian parallel where the Neoplatonic "Mind" is identified with Christ, the Son</note>. In this Mind, therefore, which was the origin of the better world and the principle of all things—which were contained in their own origins and eternal reasons (which Philosophers call **Ideas**) —he will discern every most beautiful variety of Ideas. For all must confess, as we saw above, that the Ideas have their place and seat in the Mind itself. The Mind, and the Word <note>Latin: *Verbum*; Greek: *Logos*</note>, as Philo says, is the place of the Ideas. Having progressed, therefore, to the Mind, he will here discern all Ideas and their loveliness; which, since they are contained by the Mind (for the Mind holds the exemplar of all beauty), by right all things are beautiful through the creative and generating Mind, which is the substance or existence of things.

But when he has reached the Mind, he will also surpass it, rising higher to another more sublime, which is called the **Good**, or the **Nature of Goodness**, which itself embraces an extraordinary beauty and radiance just as the Mind does, but more principally. And so it happens that he who wishes to speak perfectly should first establish beauty itself as the Parent—namely, the beauty that gives all good things—as the Second necessity. Wherefore, separating each part, he will call the intellectual beauty itself (that is, the Mind) the place of Ideas or forms. But the highest and further Good he will call the fountain and principle of beauty. Since even the Mind, whatever beauty it possesses, it has from that principle. Or, he shall establish the Good and the Beautiful on the same level, so that he does not call one the fountain and the other Beauty itself, but one and the same. And there he shall first establish these things. 

Plotinus also shows, along with the elder Philosophers, and proclaims this Mind to be the principle, inventor, and cause of the world, so that from here the supreme majesty and divinity of the Mind may shine forth. He brings forth many things worthy of being known, like ineffable mysteries, concerning it in his book *On Providence*. These things must not only be proposed by me, but also declared and explained, so that if they have any obscurity, they may be opened up, so that the naked and clear truth may remain. 

<note>The following is a translation of the Greek passage from Plotinus, Ennead III.2.1</note>
*We would speak rightly and consistently of Providence if we said that for the Universe to exist is for it to be according to Mind, and that Mind exists before it—not as being prior in time, but because the Universe is from Mind, and Mind is prior by nature and the cause of this, like an archetype and exemplar of that which is its image. If, therefore, we say the universe depends on Mind, and that its power has reached into all things, it is demonstrated that each of these wholes is well-disposed.*

These are the words of Plotinus, citing the Mind as the cause of the world confessed by his ancestors, of which it is (as Plato handed down in the *Timaeus*) the exemplar. The world, however, like an image, was made according to that power and form which the Mind embraced in its spirit; which form and worldly concept, as Philo said, was the Mind itself. And so the Mind appears as the principle and cause of the world. It appears that this is that by which the totality is sustained, through which the divinity of the Mind reaches every part. Plotinus says this is the true reason for providence: since the Mind and its power, passing through all things, made, placed, established, and distinguished all things in their orders; so that by this one most beautiful cause, you might be able to see in every reason of providen— <note>The text cuts off mid-word: *providentiae*</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Perennial Philosophy," specifically addressing the relationship between the Divine Mind and the creation of the material world, reconciling Neoplatonic thought with the Mosaic account of Genesis.</meta>

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<margin>A</margin> [Providence] extends most widely, showing that every single thing has been well-consulted, because the Mind was, and always is, the directress. However, regarding the fact that Plotinus <note>A major Neoplatonic philosopher (c. 204–270 AD) who sought to describe the nature of reality.</note> asserts in this passage that the Mind precedes the world not in time, but as its cause, author, and that by which the world is always sustained—so that he seems (as Proclus <note>A later Greek Neoplatonist who argued for the eternity of the universe.</note> affirms) to believe in the eternity of the world: I say that he, as has happened to many philosophers, held diverse opinions, and as often occurs, was called back from that personal opinion of his by the truth itself. There is no need for long-winded detours; let him stand as a witness against himself. For in the same book he pursued these points: "Since, therefore, the whole world is that which has been made, you, looking upon it, might perhaps hear it confessing these things: <note>original Greek: ὡς ἐμὲ πεποίηκε θεὸς... "that God made me, and I came forth from there, full of all living things and sufficient unto myself, and self-adequate, needing nothing."</note> *That God created me. And I came forth from that source, a completion of all living beings, sufficient and content with myself, lacking nothing, since all things are in me: plants, and animals, and the whole nature of things that can be generated, and many Gods, and populations of Demons.*" Nor indeed <note>original Greek: γῆ μὲν κεκόσμηται... "is the earth indeed adorned..."</note> *is the earth indeed adorned with all plants, and every kind of animal, and the power of the soul reached even unto the sea: while all the air, and the ether, and the whole heaven is devoid of soul.* Finally he concludes: <note>original Greek: νοῦς γίνων θεός τις... "Mind, becoming a certain god to itself, as if matter, stable and quiet, worked all things."</note> *The Mind, therefore, when it had given something from itself into matter, being itself stable, quiet, and unmoved, brought all things to pass.* By these testimonies—whether pronounced voluntarily and of his own accord, or because nature does not admit any other way of speaking and wishes us to speak according to this reason—it is clear that he spoke in the manner that all speak who confess that the world had a beginning. And the very word Moses used, "In the beginning <note>original Greek: ἐποίησε</note> *he made*," or *God created the heaven and the earth*: Plotinus used that same word. So also, *he was working*, or *he worked*. So, *the earth was adorned*, and the power of the soul *pertained* and *extended* itself. So, <margin>B</margin> *thence I came forth*, or *was brought into being*: and the fact that the Mind, without labor, without any self-commotion, but resting and sending its power into matter, *brought things to pass*. These things certainly show and display a certain time in which all things were created. For one who intended to designate an eternal dependency would not use past-tense verbs, but present-tense ones; or if occasionally past, certainly not always. He would say not "God made" or "worked," but "he works" or "he produces." What of the fact that he defines Providence as: <note>original Greek: πρόφασις τὶς καὶ λογισμὸς θεοῦ... "A certain foresight and reasoning of God, as to how this universe might come to be, and how it might be as excellent as possible."</note> *A certain foresight and consideration of God, so that this universe might be made, and that it might be held in the best way, so far as was right.* Do these not demonstrate that he looked forward and thought about a thing to be made? Is it not Mosaic Theology <note>Referring to the Book of Genesis attributed to Moses.</note> that God made all things well which he made, and saw that they were well made? From these sayings of Plotinus it is also established that God and the Mind are the same: and that this is the reason for manifest Providence, that all things are made divinely, and constituted by the most wise counsels of the Mind and Intelligence. Thus this becomes that saying of Moses, *And God saw the light, that it was good*, and so for every single created thing. Finally, *And behold, all things were very good*, namely, created by the highest Wisdom and perfect Intelligence. Then there is that most beautiful point in his writing, that the Mind is another, and the first world, from which this world had its beginning.

->*That creation belongs to the Mind, at the command of the Father, with the Mind itself working; and concerning these two, from the same Plotinus.*<-

### CHAPTER XVIII.

**A**S AN AID TO UNDERSTANDING what Plotinus said—that the Mind, quiet, without motion or self-agitation, made all things—it is worthwhile to notice what Eusebius and Cyril <note>Eusebius of Caesarea and Cyril of Alexandria, Christian fathers who quoted pagan philosophers to support Christian doctrine.</note> report, in their writings against Julian the Emperor, that Numenius <note>A 2nd-century philosopher who influenced Neoplatonism and famously asked, "What is Plato but Moses speaking Attic Greek?"</note> said: that the First God (for so the Platonists spoke, calling the first God "Father," and the second his "Offspring") is quiet and im-

-> AUGUSTINUS EUGUBINUS, BOOK I. <-

is mobile; secondly, that He Himself gave the power of action and the authority of creating, which is indeed an ineffable mystery. <note>The author is quoting Numenius of Apamea, a 2nd-century philosopher who linked Plato with Jewish thought.</note> *These are always the lives: the first is the first God, the second is the second God. It is clear that the first God will be standing still, while the second, conversely, is in motion. The first is concerned with intelligible things <note>original: "noeta" — things perceived by the mind rather than the senses</note>, while the second is concerned with both intelligible and sensible things. Do not be surprised again if I said this, for you shall hear something much more wonderful: instead of the motion pre-existing in the second, I say that the "sending" pre-existing in the first is an innate motion, from which the order of the world and the eternal, saving stability is poured out into the whole.* <margin>C</margin> There are, he says, these two lives: one of the first God, the other of the second. Namely, that the first God will be standing, resting; but the second, in turn, will be the one who moves himself. Therefore, the First is concerned with intelligible or spiritual things; the Second with intelligible and sensible things. Do not wonder that I have said this, for you will hear something much more marvelous. For instead of the motion which pre-exists in the Second, I say there is a "sending" <note>original: "missionem"</note> pre-existing in the First, and a joined motion, from which both the order of the world and the everlasting, saving stability remains in all things. These are the words of Numenius, which must necessarily be brought forward later.

Here I am struck with wonder; I stand astonished, and I can neither grasp nor explain from where such a depth of Theology flooded these minds. This mystery is most high—the sacred secret of our Theology: that the Father, who is God in the first place, sends the Son to act, and that He is the Messenger <note>original: "Angelum"</note> of the Father. And in Philo <note>Philo of Alexandria, a Jewish philosopher</note> He is the <term>governor</term> <note>original: "hyparchos" — a subordinate ruler or viceroy</note>, the magistrate of God; the <term>interpreter</term> <note>original: "hypophetes" — one who speaks for another</note>, the declarer, whom the Father used for all things as the executor of His wills. Therefore, just as He came into the world sent by the Father, so had He created the world and man; He appeared to the Prophets, gave the law to Moses, and performed the duties of the paternal Legation. Thus the Father gave judgment to Him, and He Himself shall judge all flesh. Finally, it is never the first God, but the second (though I call the First and Second one and the same God) who is shown and preached to have come.

You will not, therefore, wonder at these things in Plotinus. They were drawn from the earlier Platonists, Philo, and Numenius, to whose reading he devoted constant effort; and according to Porphyry, he wrote a hundred books of commentaries upon him. Numenius had even produced these points earlier: *that the first God is idle from all works and is King, but the creator God rules as he travels through heaven.* <margin>D</margin> The first God indeed is free from every work, like a king; but the craftsman God commands, going through the heaven. Philo also, in his "Best Idea of Divinity," thinks it a sin that the First, in His flowering state, should descend to the labor of making man; instead, He sent a messenger and legate, His only-begotten offspring, who, following the ways and instructions of the Father (whose Wisdom He is), not only produced man but furnished the entire world with beautiful forms and orders.

Saint Cyril <note>Cyril of Alexandria, a Church Father</note> reports that this was also the opinion of his teacher Plato, as related and handed down by the Platonists: *Plato says that he sees the highest God as "The Good," from whom Mind shines forth, and this Mind is the immediate creator of the world, while the First remains in stillness. In the third place, he introduces the Soul, from which they say all things are moved.* Therefore Plato, he says, confesses the supreme and highest God to be the "Greatest Good" itself. But from Him flashed forth the Mind <note>original: "Nous"</note>, and this is the immediate craftsman of the world, while the First remains in rest and leisure. He introduces a third, the Soul, by which he believes all things are stirred and agitated.

It appears, therefore (to speak for myself), that by a wonderful and heavenly benefit—for they could not have reached this by human consideration alone—they heard and read in the most ancient oracles of two Gods, joined in one substance and divinity: of whom the First, of admirable, respected, and august majesty, would send the Second, begotten of Himself from eternity, for all things. And this is that Theology so often preached by the Divine Mind itself: *That His doctrine is not His own, but His who sent Him.* And: *That God so loved the world that He gave His only-begotten Son, so that everyone who believes in Him should not perish, but have eternal life.* And: *As the living Father sent me, so I send you.* And: *That the Father judges no one, but has given all judgment to the Son.* Finally, he always preached that He was his parent and greater than Him, his teacher, the one sending Him: to Him [he gave] all honors, as to the greater...

He suggests these pagans must have had access to divine revelation or "ancient oracles" to reach such profound theological depths.</summary>

<meta>Continuing the discussion on the "Perennial Philosophy," specifically focusing on the relationship between the Father and the Son as mirrored in the works of ancient philosophers.</meta>

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<margin>A</margin>
He deferred to the greater one, and to the Father. Nor was any other Theology revealed by him, except that he had been sent by the Father and would return to him after a certain period of time had passed, performing all things while having assumed a human form. These things the Father did not do himself, but willed and commanded the Son—the "Second God"—to do, just as he had sent him as the craftsman of the world. By that same reasoning, the Second God created the world and man, as is preached. It was most true that he came into the world executing those things which the First did not indeed do, but willed and commanded the Second to do, as a great parent. The clearest reason for this is that between two beings, where one is the Parent and the other the Begotten, the Begotten ought to obey and perform what would not have been fitting for the First to do in the presence of the Second. The entire human race was an image of this reality, where the majesty and excellence of parents (if one may compare small things to great) appears over their children. And where justice reigns, children obey their commanding parents piously and reverently. This, as I have said, was an image of that which preceded from eternity in God. Regarding this matter, it is wonderful and astounding that the Platonists philosophize so deeply; they say that Plato felt this, though he preceded the birth of Christ by more than two hundred and fifty years. And Plotinus <note>A major Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 204–270 AD) who saw his work as an expansion of Plato's thought.</note> testifies that these discourses are not new, but are a certain ancient Theology flowing to the Greeks from unknown places. And again, Plotinus relates, as Saint Cyril also witnesses: <note>original Greek: "τὸν δὲ αὐτῆς νοῦν πατέρα φησὶ τὸ ἀγαθὸν..."</note> *He says that the Father of the Mind is the Good, and that which is beyond Mind and substance; and in many places he calls Being and Mind the "Idea." Thus Plato knew that from the Good comes the Mind, and from the Mind the Soul; and these accounts are not new, nor of the present, but were spoken of old, though not openly; the present accounts have become interpreters of those, confirming by testimonies that these opinions are ancient, using the very writings of Plato.* And when, he says, Plato calls the Mind itself the author and cause, he also calls the Good itself its Father, being that which is beyond Mind and substance. In many places, he calls being and substance itself the "Idea." Therefore, Plato knew that from the Good exists the Mind, and from the Mind the Soul. These discourses were not born now, nor are they new, but while everything had been said obscurely in previous ages, they have been declared by these words and explained as if by interpreters; these are the ancient sentiments of Plato himself, using his books as testimony for us. Thus says Plotinus. Nothing is more wonderful than these things: that those who did not practice the Christian religion—and even rejected it as foolish and superstitious because they had not reached its deepest mysteries, following instead the opinion and contempt of the common people—nevertheless confessed that these mysteries were far more ancient. I have spoken of this above, but I cannot restrain myself from repeating and driving home these things which are so sweet and pleasing to me.

<margin>B</margin>
->*A wonderful Theology from Plotinus concerning the same Mind: how it is generated by the Father and enriched with all good things.*<-

### CHAPTER XIX.

The same author, in his book on Providence and Speculation, discusses many things worthy of veneration and worship, but they must be explained and declared by us. The Mind, he says, has need of the Good itself, but the Good has no need of the Mind. Whence the Mind becomes a possessor of the Good itself, and is made <term>god-like<gloss>original: ἀγαθοειδής (agathoeidēs), meaning "of the form of the Good"</gloss></term>, similar to the Good itself. It is perfected by the Supreme Good through the Form, or Idea, which is in the Mind itself, which comes from the Good itself, making it "Good-formed." And a trace of the Supreme Good is seen, as it were, in the Mind itself. It is fitting to contemplate such an archetype by considering what is true in it from the trace that reaches the Mind. Therefore, the trace of that which is in the Mind itself gave to the Mind, as it looked upon it, a desire; and by always desiring, it always attains. In that higher place, however, there is neither one who desires (for what would he desire?) nor one who attains, for he was not in want. Therefore, it is not that the Mind itself lacks, for the desire is within it, and the intellect is directed toward its form.

# AUGUSTINUS STEUCHUS OF GUBBIO, BOOK I.

<margin>C</margin>
...of him. Since, therefore, the Mind is beautiful—indeed, the most beautiful of all things in pure light and pure clarity: *and most beautiful of all in pure light, and itself pure, embracing the nature of all beings, of which even this beautiful world is a shadow and image, in which all splendor is situated, because nothing is without intellect, or dark, or immeasurable within it, living a blessed life* <note>original: "καὶ πάντων καλλίστη... ἑνά" — Steuco is quoting Plotinus, Enneads 5.8.10</note>; amazement would surely seize the one who beholds it, and this, if as is necessary, he enters into it and becomes one with it. And since it embraces the nature of all things: of which even this beautiful world is a shadow and image, and in all that clarity shining forth, because nothing is unknown, or obscure, or unmeasured within it, living a blessed life: a stupor would indeed come over the one looking upon it, and penetrating it as is proper, and there being united, and made one and the same. Moreover, he says, just as one who has looked up at the heaven and seen the brightness of the stars, thinks of and seeks the *Poietēn*, the creator: so it is necessary that he who has contemplated and gazed upon the intelligible world <note>The "intelligible world" refers to the realm of pure thought and divine ideas, as opposed to the physical world.</note>, and admired it, should inquire after its artisan, who established it—either where he is, or who he is, *who, having seen all things, generated the Mind as a beautiful world, if a son <note>original: "κόρον" (koron) — Steuco notes this can mean "son" or "satiety/abundance"</note> produced from himself; certainly, because that Mind is a son, but the son is also from the Mind, for after him is Mind and the son, having been generated and satisfied; if he is being generated, he is of one who is insatiable, and for the one being generated, he has no need of thinking, but possesses true fullness and intellection, because he possesses it first; but concerning them, that he gives because he has, or because he is from the Good* <note>original: "ὁ ἰδόντα... ἀπ᾽ ἀγαθοῦ ἂν" — further quoting Plotinus on the generation of the Divine Mind</note>, He generated such a son, both a beautiful boy, and a son generated from himself.

Altogether, however, neither is he the Mind, nor the *coros*—that is, the son, or abundance—but he is even before the Mind, and the son, or satiety. For after him is the Mind and the son, or satiety, who needed to be satisfied and to understand. These things are indeed closest to Him, Who does not lack, nor has need to understand. But he possesses true fullness and intellection, because he possesses it first. That which truly precedes these things neither lacks nor possesses; otherwise it would not be the highest, or the Good itself. In these words of Plotinus is all this Theology.

<margin>D</margin>
The Good, since it is primarily God, is the Begetter and Father of all substance, and the giver of his own goods to the Mind, not being in need of the Mind: on the contrary, however, since the Mind is the offspring and the generated one, light from light <note>A direct reference to the Nicene Creed: "Lumen de Lumine"</note>, it stands in need of the Good itself; for it is generated by its Father, nor can it not be generated, nor can it shine from anywhere else than from him. The Good gives all things to the Mind itself, its whole substance—namely, by begetting it, he gives it divinity: but the Father receives nothing from the Mind. And since all things which the Father, or the Good itself, possesses, the Mind also possesses, it follows that the Mind is *agathoiedēs* <note>original: "ἀγαθοειδὴς" — "formed like the Good" or "God-like"</note>, the image of the highest Good, the mirror of divinity, so that he who sees the Mind exactly, sees the Father himself, and the Good.

Moreover, there proceeds from the highest Good to the Mind the Idea and the vestige, whether of the created world, or the very form of goodness, and the whole substance and divinity: and just as from a primary Sun, a second Sun has shone forth. Whereby it happens that if you see this fruitful one, you think you have seen the primary Sun. And because the Father is most beautiful, he is beauty itself and grace; he is discerned by the Mind, and considered, and desired, and always longed for: nor is the Mind frustrated in its longing, but at the same moment it desires, it attains. Nor is there anyone, whether Angel or by whatever other name he may rejoice, who receives as much fruit from the highest Good as the Mind. For the son is in the bosom of the Father. Therefore, the gaze and consideration of the Mind is toward the First; of the First, there is not a gaze, but love. Nor does the Good have anything—since he is most eminent—that he might look upon as greater, just as the Mind looks upon him. But the Mind is most beautiful in the light of its own clarity, and embraces the whole nature of things; it is the principle and source of all, of which this beautiful world is a shadow and image, as was said by Plato.

And it appears that, as Philo also relates, Plotinus now interprets the *noēton* <note>intelligible</note> world, about which Plato writes in the *Timaeus*, as the Mind itself: so that that archetypal world is nothing other than the Mind itself. When, I say, the Mind itself is in the highest clarity and beauty (For there was, as our [author] says, whom Plotinus seems to have followed in this place, in him the true light, which illuminates the whole world, and in him there are no shadows at all <note>A paraphrase of the Gospel of John 1:9 and 1:5</note>). Then since it is (as...

<summary>Steuco uses the philosophy of Plotinus and Plato to explain the Christian Trinity, arguing that the "Divine Mind" (the Son) is the perfect image and "second sun" of the Supreme Good (the Father). He identifies the Platonic "world of ideas" with the Mind of God.</summary>

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<margin>A</margin> Our author also says the same) in that blessed life itself, if anyone were to see it joined with him and made the same as him, he would be struck dumb, hanging there astonished; and it would happen to him as it did to those who said, upon seeing this beauty and brightness: "It is good for us to be here." <note>A reference to the Transfiguration of Christ (Matthew 17:4).</note> Moreover, he says, just as one who looks up at the sky is raised to the consideration of the Creator, so he who sees this Mind <term>Mens: The 'Nous' or Divine Intellect in Neoplatonism</term> and the intelligible world must necessarily inquire who begat it—who begat this son, satisfied him, filled him with wisdom, and gave him the ocean of wisdom. For the First, and the Good itself, is most wise and needs no one to fill Him with the abundance of wisdom and understanding. For He is most eminent, and is rightly called <note>original: "$\alpha\nu\epsilon\nu\delta\epsilon\eta\varsigma$"</note> *self-sufficient*, needing nothing. However, the Mind has <note>original: "$\pi\lambda\eta\rho\omega\sigma\iota\nu$"</note> *fullness* and true wisdom, because it possesses and enjoys it from that prior source. But truly, that which precedes the Mind is not "without wisdom" (for the Chaldean <note>Likely referring to the Chaldean Oracles</note> and Hermes Trismegistus said both were Mind or Intelligence, and that the first Mind begat the second), but because it received it from no one, it is said "not to have it" <note>Meaning the First Principle is the source of wisdom, not a recipient of it</note>. For if it received it from anyone, it would not be the supreme Good. But the Mind is closest—indeed, most closely joined—as a satiety with the supreme Good, as he warned above when he said: <note>original: "καὶ μάλιστα ὅταν ὦσι μόνοι τὸ γεννῆσαν, καὶ τὸ γεγεννημένον"</note> *And especially when the Begetter and the Begotten are alone together.* This is the whole theology of Plotinus, deriving it not only from his ancestors, Plato and all the philosophers of his and the preceding age, but he seems to have expressed our John <note>The Apostle John</note> entirely. For what John said—that the Word was in the beginning, that is, from eternity with God—is what Plotinus says: "The Begetter loves the begotten, especially when both are one." It was "with God," namely, the Mind was joined, united with God, and cohered inseparably. Likewise, "That without him nothing was made" corresponds to the fact that he himself has the fullness and knowledge of all things, and the pattern from the Father. Behold also that blessed life, the brightness and glory of the Son, which he had before the world was made. <margin>B</margin> Behold the wonder and ecstasy of those beholding the glory of the Son of God, and the highest pleasure: the souls stand around, crowding on the right and left: *Nor is it enough to have seen him once; it is a joy to linger forever.* <note>A quote from Virgil's Aeneid, applied here to a divine vision.</note> Finally, behold the Begetter and the Son in Plotinus, who was not a Christian. Therefore Truth, since it is clearer than the brightness of the Sun, has never remained hidden.

### CHAPTER XX.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'S' with floral and scrollwork designs.</image-desc>
BUT since there is such great and ineffable Theology in Plotinus—whom profane philosophers so admire and rightly call divine, while they used to attack Christian wisdom as superstition—it appears that they fought against the truth in the manner of the *Andabatae* <note>Gladiators who fought blindfolded</note>, dealing blind wounds to that which they would otherwise venerate, adore, and look up to if it were handed down by their own people, proclaiming those who spoke of it as divine. A few things must be set forth from what he wrote more precisely in the book on the Three Hypostases, entitling it <note>original: "$\pi\epsilon\rho\grave{\iota}$ $\tau\tilde{\omega}\nu$ $\tau\rho\iota\tilde{\omega}\nu$ $\dot{\alpha}\rho\chi\iota\kappa\tilde{\omega}\nu$ $\dot{\upsilon}\pi\omicron\tau\alpha\acute{\alpha}\sigma\epsilon\omega\nu$"</note> *On the three principal hypostases*, or, the three sovereign substances—namely those three kings of whom Plato dealt in his Epistle. In that book he asserts that the Mind is generated by the supreme Good, and from the Mind the Soul of the world, which is the Spirit itself. And he calls the First the supreme, saying "the One," and that one must ascend <note>original: "$\mu\acute{\epsilon}\chi\rho\iota$ $\tau\omicron\tilde{\upsilon}$ $\dot{\alpha}\kappa\rho\omicron\tau\acute{\alpha}\tau\omicron\upsilon$, $\kappa\alpha\grave{\iota}$ $\dot{\epsilon}\nu\grave{\omicron}\varsigma$ $\kappa\alpha\grave{\iota}$ $\pi\rho\omega\acute{\tau}\omicron\upsilon$"</note> *unto the highest, and the One, and the First.* From the Soul he says all souls are born, as we shall show more extensively later. But speaking of the Mind itself, he urges you to look back at the whole world, distinguished by its magnitude, beauty, order, and perennial motion. Then the Gods visible within it, and others, the hidden Spirits...

# AUGUSTINUS EUGUBINUS, BOOK I.

<margin>C</margin> ...animals also and plants. Likewise, ascending to its archetype and the truer world, you may discern all things there as intelligible <note>In this context, "intelligible" refers to things that can only be perceived by the intellect or mind, rather than the physical senses.</note> and eternal within it, as if in its own wisdom and life. He is explaining, I believe, and making use of that passage from John:
> What was made, in him was life. <note>original: "Quod factum est, in ipso uita erat." A reference to the Prologue of the Gospel of John.</note>
Plotinus, however, says those things were "their own understanding and life" <note>original: "τὴν οἰκείαν σύνεσιν, καὶ ζωήν"</note> in his domestic wisdom and life. Then he continues: "And the purity of these things, such great wisdom is beyond reach" <note>original: "καὶ τούτων τὴν ἀκήρατον τὴν τοσαύτην σοφίαν ἀμήχανον"</note>: And the Mind <term>Mens: The divine Intellect or the second person of the Neoplatonic trinity</term> which is the prince and prefect of all these, and the incomprehensible wisdom, namely of that intelligible world and of the Ideas. This sounds the same as what he has often spoken of elsewhere: that the intelligible world and the Idea of the world is the Mind itself. You have heard of this ineffable wisdom. He continues that this God and Mind is great, and when the contemplator has drawn near to him, "one asks then who generated this form, the simple one, who is before this multitude, the cause both of being and of this being great, he who makes number" <note>original: "ζητεῖ τις οὖν ὁ τύπον γεννήσας, ὁ ἁπλὸς, καὶ ὁ πρὸ τούτου πλήθους, ὁ αἴτιος, καὶ τοῦ εἶναι, καὶ πολλῷ εἶναι τοῦτον ὁ τὸν ἀριθμὸν ποιῶν"</note>. He asks who generated this one, and answers: the Simple One, who is before such a multitude, the Author both of being and of the greatness of him who also creates number. This is, of course, the Father and the principle of the Son, as our writers and the Greeks say: the most simple, Unity itself <term>Unitas: The "One," the ultimate source of all existence</term>, and that with him the Father generated all things—namely the fertility of all forms and species—and that he embraces all things, containing everything in his embrace and in the sea of wisdom. This, he says, is the theology of the ancients, among whom Parmenides distinguished those three hypostases <term>Hypostases: Distinct persons or realities within the divine nature</term> more exquisitely from one another: calling the First the principal Unity itself; the Second, "One-many," as we shall say later; the Third, "One and many," the reasons for which will be given in the second book. Thus Parmenides agrees "by nature with the three" <note>original: "φύσει ταῖς τρισίν"</note> natures (for so they were accustomed to speak). Anaxagoras, moreover, establishing a pure and undefiled Mind, placed at the head of things the simple and separable, the One itself. Heraclitus also recognized the One itself as eternal and intellectual. For Empedocles, the matter was the four elements, but the One itself was "Friendship," incorporeal indeed, since the elements are corporeal. <margin>D</margin> Finally, the ancients especially, who succeeded Pythagoras and Pherecydes, applied themselves chiefly to this supreme and simplest nature; but some left their thoughts in writings, while others handed them down not by writing but in common discourse: namely, that beyond Being itself <term>Ens: Existence or "that which is"</term> is "the One" <note>original: "τὸ ἓν"</note>, Unity itself; then follows "Being and Mind" <note>original: "τὸ ὂν, καὶ νοῦς"</note>, that which is called Being and Mind. Third is the nature of the soul. And just as those three are found in nature, so it must be thought regarding us. Also in the book on the *Generation and Order of those things which are after the First*, he says that the One itself, namely the Father, is the generator "of Being" <note>original: "τοῦ ὄντος"</note>, of Being itself, which is to say the Mind, and that this is the first generation. For since the Generator himself is most perfect, who neither seeks, nor has, nor needs anything (as he warned above), the abundance of his supreme richness generated another; once Generated, it turns back toward the Generator and is filled, and the Generated is that which looks back toward him: "and it is Mind" <note>original: "καὶ νοῦς ἐστι"</note>. Plotinus, teaching these and many other noble things of this kind in that and the following books, says that the First itself is for itself a most copious and rich fountain of its own goodness: also simple, and the first of all, called "beyond substance" <note>original: "τὸ ἐπέκεινα οὐσίας"</note>, beyond substance, namely beyond the Mind, which is called Substance and Being. Because it is the unbegotten principle, and the most perfect of all, the first and most powerful virtue, which all other virtues imitate: and because "it is necessary that the one begetting subsequent things be most precious, and it is necessary that the begotten be most precious, and the second after that one be better than all other things" <note>original: "δι’ ἃ καὶ τιμιώτατον εἶναι τὸ γεννῶν τὰ ἐφεξῆς, διὰ δ’ ἃ καὶ τιμιώτατον εἶναι τὸ γεννώμενον, καὶ δεύτερον ἐκείνου τῶν ἄλλων ἄμεινον εἶναι"</note>. That is, the first offspring of all, and the better one, as the Chaldean used to say: that the Mind and Being itself are the same. And as he says in the book *On the Good*, intelligible things are not outside the Mind: that this one nature, namely the Mind, is all things: that it is "the Truth" <note>original: "τὴν ἀλήθειαν"</note>, just as he himself spoke in the world: that that nature is a great God, "and a second God" <note>original: "καὶ θεὸς δεύτερος"</note>, bringing forth...

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<margin>A</margin>
...and showing himself, before one even sees him: that He is a king, not of things external to himself, but by a most just and natural command, and a true kingdom. <note>original: "ἀν τὶ ἀληθείας βασιλεὺς"</note> *A king in place of truth*, as if he were the king of truth, as it stands in our own scriptures, and the Lord of the universe, according to the nature <note>original: "γεννήματος, καὶ θείου τάγματος"</note> *of the offspring and the divine order*, justly called the King of kings and Father of the Gods. In his book on *Intelligible Beauty*, he asks: who could perceive the magnitude and power of Wisdom? For she holds all things with her, and she made all beings, or things, and all things followed with her; she herself is that which is and has been with him, and substance in that realm is wisdom. There are many other most divine things in his writings, in which—though there may be points that require refinement (for one should not expect an absolute and inviolate Theology from them in the same way as from our own authors)—the aim nevertheless intends to show that the wisdom of all ages, which in these most recent days has flooded into the world after breaking all barriers, appears through their testimonies. For behold, Plotinus confesses that the greatest philosophers of Greece perceived the Unity itself, the first and highest principle, to be the Divinity, and next after it, the Mind <term>Mens: The "Nous" or Divine Intellect in Neoplatonic thought</term>. Thus it appears that the Christian religion is the most ancient of all, revealed later and exhibited everywhere to everyone, even to children and women, though it once lay hidden only among the Philosophers, and even then was not perfectly understood. Truly remarkable also are those things in his book *On Ideas*. Whether one should stop where the Mind is, or whether one must proceed to that which is beyond the Mind. The Mind, he says, presided at the first principle, as if in the vestibules of the Good itself, announcing all things in itself, like a <note>original: "τύπος"</note> *type*, form, or image of that Good, existing more in multiplicity than that which remains in Unity. And the nature of this Mind must be considered, which reason pronounces to be <note>original: "τὸ ὂν ὄντως, καὶ τὴν ἀληθῆ οὐσίαν"</note> *truly Being and true Substance*. It is the same as the <note>original: "χορηγὸν τῶν λόγων"</note> *provider of reasons*—as if you were to say, the fountain of rational principles—and it truly understands those things which exist, and by understanding them, it creates them. Therefore, it is <note>original: "τὰ ὄντα"</note> *the beings themselves*. And these things exist before the world, namely as Ideas, not as figures or

<margin>B</margin>
images born from other things, but as <note>original: "ἀρχέτυπα, καὶ πρῶτα, καὶ νοῦ οὐσία"</note> *Archetypes, and first principles, and the substance of the Mind*. From this he infers: <note>original: "νοῦς γὰρ δὴ ἐστὶ τὰ ὄντα"</note> *For the Mind is indeed the things that are*. He pronounces that this same Mind does not create something immediately as soon as it has thought or understood it; for it was not the case that when it thought of God, some star was produced, nor was motion created the instant it thought of it. By this it is declared that not every one of its acts of understanding is a creation, but only when it wills. Speaking of the same in his book *On the Genera of Being*, he says: Look into this great and incomprehensible Mind, which is not <note>original: "πολυλάλῳ"</note> *much-speaking* (creating with mere words), but <note>original: "πολυνοῷ"</note> *much-thinking* (that is, most wise), and see how all things come from it. It is <note>original: "ἓν καὶ πολλά"</note> *One and many*. And these many, he says, are its wonderful powers—not weak ones, certainly, but pure, greatest, gathered virtues, or true powers. Nor do these proceed only to a certain limit; <note>original: "ἄπειρον τοίνυν, καὶ ἀπεῖρα καὶ τὸ μέγα"</note> *therefore there is infinite wisdom, and infinities, and greatness*. This greatness exists with the beauty of the substance which is in it, and with splendor and light. From these words of Plotinus, more precious than gold itself, it becomes clear that the Mind is the universal substance of things, spiritually and formally: it is infinite wisdom, it is all things, but formally, virtually, originally, in a divine and ineffable manner. It is the image and mirror of Goodness (as is found in the Book of Wisdom), and it is true substance. Our things, compared to that, are like a phantom, just as our mind is an image of His. And what must be most observed and fixed in the heart, you will gather from Plotinus: that the Ideas are nothing other than the infinite, immense, and wonderful powers of that wisdom, by which its ineffable invention and labor reach into all things, so that by these powers, all things created and yet to be created are contained. This is the reason why, although there are many divine names among the Hebrews, Moses used that one in the account of creation which signifies strength and power: *Elohim*. Therefore, those "many" things which are in Him are the Ideas. Moreover, the Ideas are his ineffable, immense powers, the most fertile fountains, the forms...

### CHAPTER XXI.

From the theology of the Chaldeans there are also the testimonies of the Erythraean Sibyl, who was Chaldean—the daughter, as rumor has it, of Berosus the Chaldean. She was perhaps called "Erythraean" not from the Ionian city of Erythrae, but from the Persian Gulf and the Red Sea <note>original: "mari rubro"</note>, which, reaching as far as the Persians and India, is called the Erythraean or Red Sea. Or rather, she was named after King Erythras, as Alexander’s generals testified, whose tomb was seen on that shore as a memorial to the ancient king. She produced these outstanding oracles, surpassing all other Sibyls and Prophets in both clarity and the number of her verses. For no prophet wrote so clearly and openly about the Son and the Father <note>original: "Filio, & Genitore"</note>, about the creative Mind, about the things to be done by it, and about human nature joined to divinity. When her songs came into the hands of the Romans (and indeed not all of them arrived, but scarcely the smallest part), they were often perversely interpreted because they were not understood, as if they did not pertain to Roman affairs. Therefore, she sang the same things about the Mind as the Magi did (though many of her oracles have been lost), so that it may be clear from all sources that the "Mind" <term>Mind: The Greek 'Nous', here identified with the Second Person of the Trinity</term> used by the Greeks and adopted for the creation of things was none other than the same Creator whom later 
<margin>D</margin>
centuries have confessed. His praise, glory, divinity, and majesty were always celebrated and known on earth, but as God, not as the Son of God, except by a very few. The Oracle of the Magi used to say that the Father performed all things and handed them over to the Mind—who is the first to be celebrated by mortals—namely, that the Father is the creator through this offspring of his, handing over to him all power and authority of creating, one giving and the other receiving. Thus the Mind was established as the principle of things by the Father. It is a unique testimony of that oracle that the Son, the Mind, is the commander of the world: but with the command having been received from another, namely from the Father; for which reason it said he perfected all things. In this, therefore, is the divinity of the Son: that he was both begotten by the Father and received the command of all things to be created once, and later established. Our own scriptures cry this out, teaching that all things were created through him, and that he was established by the Father as the judge of the living and the dead. As he himself said of himself: *All power is given unto me in heaven and in earth.* All things are in harmony, a wonderful consensus of all ages. What the divine oracle of the Magi preached, and what Plato and all the Platonists taught, might perhaps not have been so well known before Christ, until his presence made it most clear. It is a joy, therefore, to hear the Chaldean Sibyl preaching the same things and expressing this entire Divinity in two verses. For when she had said many things about God the Father, from whom she had begun the opening of her song, adding his majesty toward the Son, she brought forth:

> The all-nourishing creator, who placed the sweet spirit in all,
> and made him the leader of all gods.
> <note>original Greek: παντοτρόφον κτίστην, ὅστις γλυκύ πνεῦμα ἅπασι / κάτθετο, κ' ἡγήτερα θεῶν πάντων ἐποίησε.</note>
> 
> ->That is,<-
> ->He established the leader of the gods, who both nourishes and creates all things,<-
> ->and who gave sweet life to every man.<-

There are two things in this poem which Christians—that is, the worshipers of the revealed and known Mind—recognize...

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<margin>A</margin> Those who come to know these things leap with wondrous joy and rejoice with unceasing gladness. The Sibyl <note>The Sibyls were ancient prophetesses believed by Renaissance scholars to have predicted Christian truths to the pagan world.</note> cries out that the Father has given a sweet life to all. The Oracle of the Magi <note>Referring to the Chaldean Oracles, a collection of spiritual texts from the 2nd century attributed to Zoroaster.</note> says that the Father has perfected all things. The Theology of the Hebrews says: *He who made the heavens through understanding* <note>original: "per intellectum"</note>. Christian Theology calls the Father the creator of heaven and earth. The Platonists say many things of this kind. The prophetess continues, establishing him as the <term>all-nourishing creator</term> <note>original Greek: παντότροφον κτίστην</note>, the Creator who cherishes, sustains, and nourishes all things. She says the same was given by the Father as the prince and commander of the Gods, and as the king of both corporeal and incorporeal things. Hebrew Theology, always looking toward this same point, calls him the *Lord of Hosts*, as if he were the king, prince, and leader of the Gods, whom it calls Angels and Gods. The Chaldean therefore says: *The Father perfected all things, and delivered them to the second Mind*, which all men believe and call the first. Greek Theology proclaimed the <term>Mind</term> or <term>Intelligence</term> <note>original: "Mentem siue Intelligentiam"</note> as the author and creator, the inventor of every order, decorating the formless earth with forms. Hebrew Theology, among other things, calls this the *Spirit* working upon the Chaos; then it says the same: *He who made the heavens in understanding*, which sounds like *through Intelligence*. For among the Hebrews, "In" is "Through." The Greek and the Hebrew say the same thing. The Greek <term>Mind</term> <note>original Greek: νοῦς</note> or Intelligence is the same as the Hebrew *Spirit upon the waters* and the *Intelligence creating the heavens*. The newest Theology <note>Christianity</note>, now spread throughout the whole world, proclaims these things whenever it preaches. Consider and compare this oracle with the saying of the great Paul: *For to which of the Angels,* he says, *did he ever say: You are my Son, sit at my right hand?* But the Sibyl established him as the Father's <term>leader</term> <note>original Greek: ἡγεμόνα</note>, the prince, king, and leader of the Angels, that is, of the Gods. For "Gods" is used for "Angels" among the Hebrews and our own people; as in that passage: *You have made him a little lower than the Angels*—in Hebrew *than the Elohim*, that is, the Gods. And that other: *In the sight of the Angels*—in Hebrew, *In the sight of the Gods*. Behold the divinity and majesty of the Son, not separated from the divinity of the Begetter. All these authors confess two, 
<margin>B</margin> yet they show them to be one. Unless there is a most profound mystery in their words, there would necessarily be a contradiction. The Chaldean says the Father perfected all things and gave them to the Son, who is the first Mind. But the prophetess says the Father infused a sweet spirit and a sweet soul into all things. Afterward, she calls that Second one the creator and foster-father of all. Therefore, they are two, and they are one. For both are given the creation of things. Clearly, therefore, they handed down that all the divinity, authority, and power of the Son come from the Father. And since they call both the "giver of lives," they openly join their divinity, and they cry out that it is joined—so joined, in fact, that all things had two principles as if they were one, two creators, whom they assert to be most closely joined and "most one" for this reason: because there cannot be two creators of the world. For if the Father himself gives the sweet spirit to all, according to the Sibyl, and if the Father perfected all things, according to the Magi, what remains for the second Mind? And yet these same people declare the Mind to be the female creator. They saw, therefore, or they proclaimed what they had heard: a most closely joined substance and divinity. They confessed the Son as King of the Gods and Prince of creation, but his substance and authority were begotten from the Father. All Theology is deduced from this oracle. The Magi use the name of the Father, calling that first dictating and generating Mind by that name. If he is "Father" by their testimony, it is certainly because of him whom he begot. Thus, there is an eternal generation. Then they show a certain greater authority and rank between them, while one is called Father, is called First, and is said to deliver creation to the other, and establishes him as the creator of all. But they join the divinity and substance because they join the creator. For if the substance were separate, the authority and power would also be separate; one thing would be made by one, and another by the other. But since the creation of things is the most eminent work, if it is done as much by the Father as by the Son, they are necessarily one substance, one authority, one power, one empire, one divinity. For whatever is done by one is likewise done by the other. This could not happen unless they were one. Thus in the most ancient Theology of the Chaldeans were the Father and the Son. And the light, which afterward grew into immense flames, was then sparkling as if in the twilight.

It suggests that ancient "pagan" oracles were early, "twilight" glimpses of the full Christian truth.</summary>

# On the Reliability of the Testimony of the Magi and the Sibyls.
### CHAPTER XXII.

<margin>C</margin>
If there were anyone to whom it might perhaps seem incredible that these things were spoken and known by the Magi and the Chaldean Sibyl, he will certainly no longer doubt if he reads the books of the Platonists—which are filled with the sayings and theology of these Magi—and considers those points which we have brought forward from them above. Furthermore, Apollo himself testified <note>The author refers to "The Oracles of Apollo," often cited by early Christian apologists to show that even pagan gods "confessed" the truth of the Hebrew faith.</note> that only the Chaldeans and Hebrews were wise: *Only the Chaldeans and the Hebrews have obtained wisdom.* <note>original Greek: μοῦνοι χαλδαῖοι σοφίην λάχον, ἠδ' ἄρα ἑβραῖοι</note> To the Chaldeans and Hebrews alone was it given to know. Nor should you think it happened by any other means that these people especially recognized the birth of that great Mind, the divine offspring, and the creative Intelligence, than from their ancient Theology. This theology openly proclaimed that the Son of God, the creator of all things, would one day come into the world, and that a sign from heaven would be shown to the Magi themselves regarding this matter. Therefore, in their Theology, which was filled with oracles concerning the Son of God, both the time of his arrival was declared and the sign which would indicate the place was to be shown from the heavens. For this reason, they kept watch most vigilantly and had filled the entire East with that rumor, as Suetonius testifies in his lives of Vespasian and Titus. <note>Suetonius, a Roman historian, recorded a "firm and ancient belief" throughout the East that a ruler from Judea would acquire a world empire.</note> And the Sibyl had commanded that, having sighted the sign in the sky, they should follow the star as a leader and adore the great king. Moreover, they were to adore him according to the custom of their own nation. Now, no one among the Persians greeted a King without bringing gifts. These gifts were of the things in which their region was fertile. And so, since the Chaldeans and Persians abound in gold (due to neighboring Arabia), and in frankincense and myrrh, they offered these and adored that King of Gods, the creator of the world, the son of God, the primary Mind.

<margin>D</margin>
But the testimony and theology of the Magi is very clear, both in our own literature and that of the Platonists. It was enough to have spoken of these in the previous section; however, I decided to repeat the reasoning regarding their Theology because I had to speak of the Sibyl, who is herself a prophetess of Chaldean Theology. It is most clear, therefore, that the testimonies of this prophetess are true and reliable. For the Romans—a people, mind you, not yet imbued with the religion of Christ—carried her books to Rome and placed them in the Capitol. The power to inspect these was later granted to Lactantius <note>Lactantius (c. 250 – c. 325) was an early Christian author and advisor to Constantine I.</note> because of his friendship with the Emperor Constantine, as he was the tutor of the Emperor's son, Crispus. It must be believed that these testimonies were most faithfully reported by that most learned and eloquent man, both because of his supreme integrity and his learning (both of which his books and his appointment as the Emperor's son's tutor testify to most richly). But there is a most brilliant testimony on this matter in Marcus Tullius <note>Cicero</note>, first regarding the order of the verses of this prophetess, and then regarding what was contained within them. Cicero will attest to the sayings and oracles of the Sibyl when writing about that kind of divination which occurs by divine inspiration, which they call "Frenzy." Wishing to destroy this idea (following the custom of the Academic school), he gave us many clues and arguments regarding these poems. First, he testifies that those poems were composed as an <term>acrostic</term> <note>original: ἀκροστιχίδα. A poem where the first letter of each line spells out a word or message.</note>; that is, when something is connected and stands out from the first letters of each successive verse. He asserts that in the Sibylline verses, the whole poem is prefaced by the first letters of each verse. Compare, then, this testimony of a most eloquent man with the poem of the Erythraean Sibyl, which she sang concerning the final judgment and the last things, which is now very well known and is cited by Augustine in his books *On the City of God*. There you will see that from the first letters of each verse of the entire poem, there is gathered in Greek: *Jesus Christ, Savior, Son of God, Cross.* <note>original Greek: ἰησοῦς χριστὸς, σωτὴρ, θεοῦ υἱὸς, σταυρὸς</note> But hear what the same man [Cicero] discovered in the sayings of the Sibyls. He says that an interpreter of those poems was recently thought to be about to say in the Senate (based on a rumor among men) that he whom we already had as King in reality should also be called "King" if we wished to be safe. Hear, I say, what the Sibyl had prophesied to the Romans: That he who was in reality, who was substantially, their King, should also be called...

<summary>This page argues for the validity of the Magi and Sibyls as pagan witnesses to Christ, citing historical figures like Suetonius, Cicero, and Lactantius to prove that ancient prophecies predicted a divine King and the Star of Bethlehem.</summary>

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<margin>A</margin> and also, that he was to be adored as a King, if they wished to be saved. And the Sibyl <note>The Sibyls were ancient prophetesses in the Greek and Roman world whose oracles were often interpreted by later Christian scholars as foretelling the Gospel.</note> had predicted that this would happen shortly after the time when Marcus Cicero <note>The famous Roman orator and statesman, also known as Tully.</note> was writing these things. Moreover, there was a public rumor regarding that oracle. For he says: "There is a rumor among men that the interpreter of the Sibylline verses will necessarily say false things in the Senate, namely concerning a King of the Romans, whom it would behoove the Romans to call 'King' if they wished to be saved." This was the rumor among the people, and the oracle was known to many, though it was considered false by those same people because they believed there would never be any King of the Romans—whose state was a Republic—by whom the Romans might be made safe. Now, Marcus was writing these things after the death of Gaius Caesar <note>Julius Caesar</note>, shortly before the Triumvirate. Writing at this time, he said: "Whose interpreter was recently thought to be about to say certain false things in the Senate concerning a King of the Romans." Observe the timing. For he said, "He was recently thought to be about to speak," indicating that the time was very near when, the Sibylline verses having been read in the Senate, a certain King of the Romans would be shown from them by the interpreter, whom it would be necessary for the Romans to address and acknowledge as King if they wished to be saved—that is, to be redeemed by the Savior. And although a specific definition of the dates had been removed, the Sibyl had nevertheless established the time of that King so that the Romans and Tully himself could have understood that he would arise around those times. For from where else would the rumor among men have flowed from the verses, that a King was recently to be brought forth from them? For they were read according to the dates and Olympiads <note>Four-year periods used by the Greeks for dating events.</note> which the Sibyl also described. And perhaps the Sibyl had declared such an Olympiad for that King that, not long after his own time, Marcus and the others might recognize that this King was to be brought forth and shown from those verses—not that he would exist much later, but was already almost present. Therefore, concluding the same and detesting the idea of a King (since he defended the side of the Republic), he says: 
<margin>B</margin> "Let us act with the Priests <note>original: "Antistitibus"</note> so that they may bring forth anything else from these books rather than a King, whom hereafter neither gods nor men will suffer to exist at Rome." Clearly, he did not understand which King the Sibyl had spoken of. For she had predicted a King, not one through whom the Republic was to be overturned—which Caesar and Augustus did, under whom the most cruel proscriptions and miserable slaughters of Roman citizens occurred—but one through whom salvation was to be provided to the Romans. And for that reason, he was called "Savior" and "Salvation" by the Sibyl: to be provided, however, only if they recognized and addressed him as King. The Magi also, recognizing such an oracle at almost the same time Marcus was writing these things, both addressed this very King as "King" and adored him. Now Marcus said, "Recently." From that time in which he wrote these things until the birth of Christ, there were twenty years, more or less. Therefore he said, "Hereafter." The Sibyl had thus predicted that a King of the Romans—who were themselves the kings of all others—would arise not long after those times: specifically after the civil wars, after the Republic was pacified, and peace was obtained everywhere, and all nations were subdued by the Romans. The rumor had increased at Rome that a certain King was to be shown from the Sibylline verses. And see how they were in agreement: both the Magi in recognizing him, and the Sibyls in predicting him. And perhaps the same oracle of the same Sibyl was among the Chaldeans. For many authors claim that the Erythraean Sibyl was Babylonian. And one of them, the first of the ten Sibyls, Sambethe, is said to have been from Persia. But it was held more clearly in its own source among the Chaldeans. Therefore, throughout the entire East, just as at Rome and in Judea (as Josephus is the authority), this rumor concerning a certain great King had been spread abroad. Moreover, the Sibyl had not only prophesied many things about the King, but had also added the same circumstances that Isaiah did: that the Golden Age would return, and peace would be everywhere. When Virgil had also read these things in the Sibylline verses, he ascribed to a man—with the same ignorance as Tully—those things which were being sung concerning God. Thus Tully also, suspecting that this King would be a man, was indignant against him and against the Sibylline verses which prophesied concerning him...

The author links these Roman rumors to the arrival of the Magi and the prophecies of Isaiah, suggesting a universal expectation of a "Savior King" around the time of Augustus.</summary>
