# AUGUSTINUS EUGUBINUS, BOOK I.

From these sources, therefore, both the Theology of the Magians and the oracles of the Sibyl are proven, as is the faith <margin>C</margin> <note>The margin marker 'C' likely refers to a specific section or citation in the author's source manuscript.</note>, and all things agree wonderfully. From the Sibyl also, the reasoning for the succession of doctrine from the most ancient fathers to their descendants is greatly confirmed. For one Sibyl, either Chaldean or from Persia, is said to have prophesied innumerable things concerning Christ and his coming; she was called Sambethe, from the line of Noah, as I have said. She is reported to have written twenty-four books, according to the author Suidas. And the Emperor Constantine, when he had discovered the Sibylline verses within the works of Virgil, was struck with amazement; by that testimony he proved that the things the Sibyls prophesied concerning Christ had not been fabricated by Christians. And he speaks in this manner: The Erythraean Sibyl, therefore, who says she existed in the sixth age after the flood (that is, in the six-hundredth year), was a priestess of Apollo, wearing a diadem just like that god whom she worshipped, and guarding the tripod around which a snake was coiled. She gave answers to those who inquired, having been devoted to this cult and madness by her parents, in which—aside from disordered frenzies—you would see nothing else to be venerated, much like the stories told of Daphne. She, therefore, having entered at some time into the inner shrines of that foolish superstition, expressed the mysteries of Christ through divine inspiration by an arrangement of letters <gloss>which is called an Acrostic</gloss> <note>original: "ἥτις ἀκρόστιχις λέγεται"</note>. Having produced those verses which I mentioned above, Constantine continues: "And this was given to her to prophesy, as if to a virgin by divine power. I deem her blessed, whom the Savior chose as a prophetess of his providence toward us." But many people reject this faith, admitting indeed that the Erythraean Sibyl existed, but claiming these verses were fabricated by someone of our own people who was not ignorant of the poetic art—that they are counterfeit, inscribed with the Sibyl's name because they contain sentiments useful for life, calling people away from pleasures toward modesty. But the truth is in the open: when the dates are diligently collected, it is clear this poem was not written by our people after the coming and death of Christ. It is falsely <margin>D</margin> disparaged, since it was published by the Sibyl so many years before. For it is well known that Cicero saw this poem, translated it into the Roman language, and included it among his writings. Now, Cicero was killed by Antony, and Augustus survived Antony, reigning for fifty-six years. He was succeeded by Tiberius, in whose time the coming of Christ shone forth—that is, the growth of his religion occurred, <gloss>so that a new succession of people might stand together</gloss> <note>original: "ἵνα νέα τὸ δῆμον διαδοχὴ συνέση"</note>. Concerning this, I believe the most eminent of Latin poets [Virgil] spoke: *Now a new progeny is sent down from high heaven.* The Emperor Constantine says all these things in his oration *On the Christian Religion*. He examines the whole Virgilian poem from the sixth Eclogue. And it is worth noting that in the last poem, which the Sibyl composed concerning Christ through the use of prepositions <note>Referring to the acrostic structure where the first letters of lines form a name or title.</note>, these two verses are the very last:
> This one, now written before us in acrostics, is our God,
> The immortal Savior, the King who suffered for our sake.
<note>original Greek: "οὗτος ὁ νῦν προγραφεὶς ἐν ἀκροστιχίοις θεὸς ἡμῶν, σωτὴρ ἀθάνατος, βασιλεὺς ὁ παθὼν ἕνεκα ἡμῶν."</note>
That is,
> He who is now prescribed through each verse as ours,
> Is God, is the saving King, having suffered for our sin.

### CHAPTER XXIII.
### Concerning the Divine Word, and in what way it is Light from light, from Hermes Trismegistus.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'T' featuring a stylized landscape with foliage.</image-desc>THE THEOLOGY of the Egyptians also contains many testimonies concerning the eternal Mind and the unbegotten Father himself. For Hermes <note>The text uses "Mercurius," the Roman name for Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Egyptian sage.</note> calls that divine progeny not only "Mind"—following the universal theology of the Platonists and the ancients—but even by that very name which is famous and common in our own Theology: the <term>Word</term> <note>original: "Verbo" (Logos). The author is arguing that ancient pagans knew of the Christian concept of the Second Person of the Trinity.</note>. And he wrote stupendous things about it before the Word itself had come into the world, so that you may find almost the entirety of religion within his works. He agrees wonderfully with the Chaldeans, explaining eternal matters with human words.

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<margin>A</margin> for, and incomprehensible: <note>original Greek: ὁ δὲ νοῦς ὁ θεὸς ἀρρενόθηλυς ὢν, ζωὴ καὶ φῶς, ἀπεκύησε λόγῳ ἕτερον νοῦν δημιουργὸν</note> *But the Mind, which is God, being both male and female, life and light, brought forth by the Word another creator Mind.* That is, that Intelligence which is God, since it possessed the power of both male and female, and was both life and light, begat the Word, namely, a second acting Mind. This is what the most ancient Theology of the Egyptians proclaims. From the many sayings of this most ancient Theologian <note>Referring to Hermes Trismegistus, often identified by the name "Mercurius" in this period.</note>, I now cite a few, from which one may judge the whole matter. You hear him mention, in a manner similar to the Chaldean, two Minds, as if they were two persons of the Christians. And so that he might teach the unlearned how the Father could generate, he testified that He was endowed with the same power for begetting which male and female possess separately. In doing this, he made use of human words that sound like bodily things, as was the custom of ancient Theology. For it cannot fall within human senses how a Mind begets a Mind, since in the procreation of all living creatures, both powers are necessary. However, for eternal generation and an incomprehensible birth most remote from our eyes, examples of inanimate things being born are more fitting; for as some are born from others, in most cases there is only one single thing that generates. There is, moreover, no more apt or clearer example than that of life and light. Contemplate, therefore, and look up at the highest mystery and the deepest sacrament, by which all things concerning eternal generation are declared, and which the heavenly Wisdom preaches in this newest Theology restored to the world from heaven: how the eternal Son is generated by the eternal Father, saying, *Light from light.* Moreover, Mercurius <note>Hermes Trismegistus</note> says that the Father, because He is light, begat and brought forth the Son. Therefore, they show us such a generation and origin of that eternal Mind as is the birth of immense light from immense light, of purest light from purest light—a procreation very different from our own and that of that eternal Mind. And as far as it is permitted to grasp eternal things by thinking through visible examples, the generation of light from light is almost the only one that fits the understanding of that eternity. The Divine Apostle <margin>B</margin> and he who so greatly celebrated Wisdom called the Son of God <note>original Greek: ἀπαύγασμα αἰωνίου φωτὸς</note> *the brightness or ray of eternal light.* Thus Mercurius, as you see, wishing to explain how the Father generates, says that He is light. You recognize that the ancient Theology was no different than this of our own ages: for the truth is always the same, shining forth in some way at every time. Mercurius also proposes this cause for why Mind begets mind: because the Father is life. In this lies a most profound mystery. For the Father is simultaneously light and life. For He is not that light which shines before our eyes, but a living light, the greatest light, a light shining and living, light with the greatest life, either a most brilliant life or a most vivid light. Our light is a silent light, and as it were, a dead light. For neither does the light live, nor the brightness flowing from the light. But in that light is life. Indeed, life itself is clothed in light, as if with a garment. Because, therefore, the Father is the highest light and the most lucid life, He begets a second Mind, like a second light. And since this generation is compared to light, it is also indicated that there is a connection between the generator and the generated, such as exists between light and brightness. For never is brightness separated from light, or a ray from the light: they are always accompanying one another. Everywhere one begets, the other is begotten. Neither can the ray exist without the light as its mother or father and its cause, nor the light without the ray: because light can never not generate brightness. For what light can you provide that does not always pour forth its total brightness? These things the ancient Theology preached. The words and examples are clear. Then Mercurius calls the generated thing by three names, just as he had named the Father by His own names. He had called the Father Intelligence or Mind—although this is proper to the Second <note>The Son/Word</note>, as we have seen. Likewise, he calls Him "God" with the article and emphasis, because the name of divinity belongs principally to the Father, who is the author of the entire divinity. Then he describes the power of begetting with a figurative name, and...

It emphasizes that the truth of the eternal generation of the Son is a perennial concept found across different eras and cultures.</summary>

# AUGUSTINUS EUGUBINUS, BOOK I.

He expressed this according to human custom: first he called him Light, then Life. Although these terms also apply to the Son, they are nevertheless proper to the Begetter himself. For the Son is also God. But since this divinity is generated from the Father, the Father is God in a more primary sense. The Son is also Life and Light, but the Father is so in the proper sense, so that he is revealed as the cause, the life, and the fountain of divinity. However, the name of Mind properly suits the Son, as he is Wisdom. But the name that embraces the entire mystery and expresses all the force and power of the Son is the name of the Word <note>Latin: *Verbum*</note> and the Craftsman <note>Latin: *Opifex*</note>. In the writings of Hermes <note>The author refers to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Egyptian sage believed to have anticipated Christian truths.</note> there are paternal properties and the eternal generation of offspring. Finally, there is the creation of the world through this same Son. For when he calls him the *creative Word* <note>original Greek: *λόγον δημιoυργὸν* (logon demiourgon)</note>, that is, the Word as Craftsman, you should know that these names signify the authority of creating and the origin, which all theology, both new and old, decrees to the Son of God with the greatest consensus. Therefore, if you look at his eternal generation, he is called the Son, he is called the Offspring; but if you look at the authority and the principle of creating which he received from the Father, he is called the Word and the Craftsman. He is called the Word or Speech, as if he were the speaker and interpreter of God, like a messenger of God to all things, or the reason, the form, and the origin of created things. This is both the power of God, and his authority, his mission, and his wisdom: and all these things regard the creation and the administration of all things handed down from the Father to the Son. This is that Word through which the heavens were made firm, and through which the heaven stands forever. For so the Prophet sings: *Forever, O Lord, your Word stands in the heaven.* <note>A reference to Psalm 119:89.</note> Truly wonderful is the harmony of all theology of all times. Since, therefore, the Hebrew Prophet affirms that the heavens were made through the Word of God, and Hermes proclaims that the Word of God was born and generated from God, it is clear that the Hebrew preached that the heaven was created through the one generated by God. And when the ancient Hebrews once sang this Psalm, and now sing it daily in their synagogues, they certainly sing that the heavens were created through the Son of God. So it is indeed; whether they wish it or not, they come to this point. For great is the force of truth, like a certain rapid tide, sweeping even those who resist toward itself.

<margin>D</margin> According to the authority of Aristotle, the Word (in Greek, *Logos*) can also be interpreted as the reason, the form, and the essence of each thing. Moreover, the Son of God is the reason and principle of all things: the common Form, the reason, and the principle. He is the Form of forms, the discovery of all essences and forms, the "Kingdom of Ideas" as Plotinus said earlier: a most vast Form holding within itself the reasons and forms of all things in the most perfect way. Thus, everything is found by it, just as a horse can be born from a horse, a lion from a lion, fire from fire, and as all things come from particular reasons. Every thing has a particular *logos* <note>reason or defining principle</note> by which each thing is what it is, and can beget another like itself. But there is one greatest and most omnipotent Reason, alone as powerful as all the particular ones combined, able to produce every thing just as if it were that thing's particular form. By this Reason, a human can be created and a fire kindled no less than by a human or a fire; therefore he is the *Logos* and the Form of forms. The first reason—by which the Son of God is called the Word in the Scriptures, from which this expression was born—is more fitting. For he is the Word of God as if he were His speech, His discourse, His mission, His interpreter, and His legate. And it is the habit of Hebrew expression to use the abstract name for the person designated, just as they call the Savior "Salvation" and the Redeemer "Redemption." By this reasoning, they call him who speaks for God the "Word of God," and they say "Speech" as if meaning the Speaker and Interpreter. The second interpretation is more suited to those practicing philosophy and investigating the truth of things, who do not accept human-like speech in God.

### CHAPTER XXIV.

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<margin>A</margin> Many are the renowned and august names of that divine offspring which has been known and adored throughout all ages. Among these, two are foremost, of which there is always sacrosanct mention in all secret Philosophy: these are **Mind** and the **Word**. In Theology—which later opened all hidden places and revealed unknown mysteries to all men—the "Word" is more frequent. Among the Philosophers, both are common, but "Mind" is more customary. Many excellent and admirable things have been handed down by them concerning the Mind, as well as the Word. However, the reasoning behind both names is the same. "Mind" designates that divine and eternal Wisdom. The "Word" (in Greek <term>logos</term> <note>original: "λόγος". In Greek, this term implies both "speech" and "reason" or "proportion."</note>) is reason, notion, and the power of perceiving. The *Logos* could also have been called, as I said, a form or an "Idea."

The first, therefore, from whom these ideas could reach our understanding was Hermes Trismegistus <note>A legendary Hellenistic figure identified with the Egyptian god Thoth, regarded as the author of the Hermetic Corpus.</note>, a most rich witness and a great herald of that which is called the Word. He was followed by the whole class of Greek Philosophers; it is likely they read what Hermes wrote (and other things unknown to us) as interpreters of ancient mysteries—for we gladly report grand things known to few. They frequently looked up to that very Word and extolled it with extraordinary praise. Therefore, from among many, so that you may know the prince of these ideas and from where those things we admire in later writers flowed, Trismegistus said this of the Word:

> For his Word, proceeding forth, being all-perfect, and fertile, and the creator, falling upon a fertile nature and fertile water, made the water pregnant. <note>original Greek: "ὁ γὰρ λόγος αὐτοῦ προελθὼν παντέλειος ὤν, καὶ γόνιμος, καὶ δημιουργός, ἐν γονίμῳ φύσει, πεσὼν ἐπὶ γονίμῳ ὕδατι, ἔγκυον τὸ ὕδωρ ἐποίησε."</note>

Which is to say: His Word, proceeding as everywhere perfect, fertile, and the craftsman, having fallen into a fecund nature and fecund waters, made them prolific. This is the admirable Theology of Hermes. First, he said the Word "proceeds," namely from its own parent; then "perfect," "fertile," and "craftsman," because it is the most fruitful creator, the richest source of Wisdom, the most copious treasury of things, and infinite riches. For without it, nothing was made that has been made. It is the origin, the invention, and the generation. When you ask from where all things were made, assign it to this Word and stop your step; you have discovered the true origin of things. The name "Craftsman" <note>Latin: *Opifex*, often used to translate the Platonic *Demiurge*.</note> is also common in Platonic Theology. This Craftsman creates the world for Plato. In Moses, as in Trismegistus, there is a Spirit over the waters, making them fertile, from which all things are brought forth by the power of the divine Word. Hermes continues:

> Therefore the pyramid, lying beneath nature and the intelligible world, has as its presiding ruler the creator Word, the Lord of all, who after Him is the first power, unbegotten, infinite, leaning out from Him; he both presides over and rules the things created through him. He is the first-born of the All-Perfect, and is himself perfect, fertile, and a legitimate son. <note>original Greek: "ἡ οὖν πυραμὶς ὑποκειμένη φύσει, καὶ τῷ νοερῷ κόσμῳ, ἔχει γὰρ ἄρχοντα ἐπικείμενον τὸν δημιουργὸν λόγον τὸν πάντων δεσπότην, ὃς μετ' ἐκεῖνον πρώτη δύναμις, ἀγέννητος, ἀπέραντος, ἐξ ἐκείνου προκύψασα, καὶ ἐπίκειται, καὶ ἄρχει, τῶν δι' αὐτοῦ δημιουργηθέντων. ἔστι δὲ τοῦ παντέλειος πρόγονος, καὶ τέλειος, καὶ γόνιμος, γνήσιος υἱός."</note>

<margin>B</margin> Nothing could be clearer or more evident concerning the Son of God. In Latin, they are: The Pyramid lies beneath nature and the intelligible world. For it has a presiding prince, His craftsman Word, who is the lord of all. Who, after Him, is the first virtue, uncreated, infinite, emanating from Him. And he presides over and commands those things which were made by him. He is the first-born of the most perfect, and is himself perfect, fertile, and a legitimate son.

Do you see the wonderful Theology of this most ancient man? He calls the elements of things a "Pyramid," lying beneath nature indeed, but far more beneath the intelligent world. Moreover, the intelligent or mental world is primarily the craftsman Word, who is called Mind, the commander, and the one standing by the world. This Word belongs to Him by whom he is lord of all, who after the universal Lord is the first virtue, uncreated, infinite, proceeding from Him. This rules all created things. And he is the first-born of Him who is called <term>panteleios</term> <note>original: "παντέλειος", meaning "all-perfect" or "completely absolute."</note>, the most perfect and absolute. Of this most perfect one, he is himself perfect, and fertile on account of created things, and a legitimate son. O divine Theology!

Furthermore, the entire Theology of the Platonists proves that the ancient Hermes truly said these things, and that they were not—should anyone perhaps doubt it—concocted by one of our own [Christians], for it contains not only these names but the entire matter to a hair's breadth. You will see this especially in...

This serves to demonstrate a "perennial philosophy" where ancient pagan wisdom aligns with Christian doctrine.</summary>

# AUGUSTINUS EUGUBINUS, BOOK I.

Philo <note>Philo of Alexandria, a Hellenistic Jewish philosopher who used the concept of the "Logos."</note> has occupied nearly the same position, with whom it is written that the Word is the first-born, the legitimate son: that the Word governs the world and is the bond of the universe. And it is proved that this is the ancient Wisdom. Nothing more divine can be said than that, after the Father, the Word is the first power—uncreated, infinite, flowing from Him, holding the empire and the rudder of the world. Mercury <note>Referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Egyptian sage.</note> follows, speaking with another Egyptian priest who asked him what these things he had said signified: *“Do you not understand, O Sinacas? The nature of His intellectual Word is a generative and demiurgic nature. This is, as it were, His generation, His nature, His form, or whatever you wish to call it; for of old, thinking alone, He was perfect from the perfect, and He works simply perfect goods, and He creates and gives life. Since, therefore, He possesses such a nature, He is rightly addressed thus.”* <note>original Greek: οὐ δὲ ὦ σινᾶκας, φύσις τοῦ νοεροῦ αὐτῶ λόγου... This passage emphasizes the Word as the active creator or "Demiurge."</note> You, he says, did not understand. The mental nature of the Word itself is a generative and crafting nature. Call this His generation, or nature, or custom, or whatever you please; perceiving this one thing: that He is perfect within the perfect, and from the perfect He works, strives for, and gives life to perfect goods. Since, therefore, He is especially endowed with this nature, it is rightly so named.

Behold, now Trismegistus—just as above he had mentioned the eternal generation, the divinity, the substance, and the divine power of the Offspring and His Begetter—so now he explains why it is called the Word: prolific, generative, producing, and creating all things from nothing, because it is most fruitful in itself, full of forms and origins, and does not require pre-existing matter. For He Himself is the matter and form of all things, because such is His nature and such His power, that He contains within Himself the generations, the genital forms, and the principles of all things. Just as the forms of animals lie hidden invisibly in the seed, and having obtained heat and time, they are opened and become clear; so all forms and principles of things lie hidden invisibly in that Word. Nor is there need, as I said, for another preceding principle, or other matter and form. Such is His nature, <margin>D</margin> and He is called prolific, fertile, most opulent, and most wealthy by God. Just as in a royal palace there are various riches, and in the earth there are various forms, and as fertile water brings forth wonders: so (if it is permitted to compare small things with great, and to ascend from the corporeal to the invisible) He is the city and the palace of all forms that are or can be in the heavens, the lands, the sea, and the ether. By this He creates and strives, the inventor of all good things. By this He is perfect: nothing escapes His invention and power.

Nothing is more ineffable than this Theology, nothing that can more greatly quiet our minds when they cannot be led to grasp how "all things were made from nothing." It would indeed be a marvel if God were sterile and unfruitful; but now, since He is the art of all forms, such a thought <note>i.e., that God is sterile</note> will only fall into an unskilled mind accustomed to corporeal things. Finding these things in that ancient Philosophy, I cannot be filled with enough admiration; I am struck senseless with astonishment, and joyfully I give thanks to our highest Redeemer, who—being Himself the declarer of His divinity, which was once revealed to a few—has most recently come and filled all lands with the knowledge of Himself.

Mercury adds to his son Tat, whom he called by his own name. For Mercury is called by the Egyptians, according to Plato, *Tohyth* or *Theuth*; I believe it is *Tat*. *“The Word of the Creator, O son, is eternal, self-moving, incapable of increase or decrease, immutable, incorruptible, unique, always like unto itself, equal and even, stable, well-ordered, being One, existing after the God who is known beforehand.”* <note>original Greek: ὁ τοῦ δημιουργοῦ λόγος ὦ τέκνον, ἀϊδιος... This describes the Word using the attributes of Platonic perfection.</note> In these words also, as much Theology as can be expressed by human voices concerning the divine Word, as much as the strengths of the mind are capable of thinking—or rather, not the strengths of minds, but as much irradiation of truth as could glide from heaven into human breasts—has been poured out in these few words.

He asserts that the Word contains the blueprints for all creation, making the concept of creation from nothing easier for the human mind to grasp.</summary>

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.
<note>The title refers to the concept that a single, true theology has existed through all ages, shared by both ancient philosophers and Christians.</note>

<margin>A</margin> Where then (to seize upon this first point, by the gift of truth), where, I ask, did the Arians <note>Followers of Arius who denied the full divinity of Christ.</note> and Valentinians <note>A Gnostic sect.</note> drink that madness of theirs, which they seem to have drawn from the books of profane theology? For they fought for the idea that the Word of God was merely made or created, and that the Father alone was the eternal God. Clearly, they were as blind in Philosophy—in which they used to boast and vaunt themselves as "profane wisdom"—as they were in the sacred scriptures. Do you not see Trismegistus <note>Hermes Trismegistus, a legendary Hellenistic figure associated with ancient wisdom.</note> preaching the eternal Word, giving him those conditions, those endowments, that life, that substance, and that place which only divinity admits? He is eternal, namely co-eternal with God. He has from himself all motion, all life, and every impulse, whether toward creating or toward contemplating and speculating upon his own divinity. He is the principle of his own motion. He is not moved from the outside by objective things, as our senses are, nor does he have anything in heaven or earth to impel him; rather, he is his own motion, his own origin, the fountain of his own life. And since he is eternal, since nothing is better, since he is infinite, he also necessarily possesses this: that he cannot be increased, and no addition can be made to him. For what could be added that he does not already possess? However much you wander through the vast field of all goods, however curiously you explore whether there is anything anywhere that you do not find there, certainly nothing will occur that does not overflow in those kingdoms. And this is the marvelous, solitary, and singular thing in him: that these goods cannot be diminished or undermined, nor can such great happiness be interrupted, just as it cannot be increased. For the logic of diminishing and increasing is the same. That which can be taken from can also be added to. Where there is such perfection that the arrival of external things finds no lodging—why do I say external, since he himself is all things?—he cannot even move from his own seat. For he is immutable, incorruptible, singular, endowed with a nature not only rare but unique, always remaining the same and always like himself; not like <margin>B</margin> us, whose minds and bodies are carried here and there as if by constant waves, and change their faces like a stage set. Therefore, he is equal and level, not possessing now this mind and now that, or varying senses. For he himself said, *I am God, and I am not changed.* <note>original: "Ego Deus, & non mutor." A reference to Malachi 3:6.</note> And he is next in rank after God: for the Father is properly called God. But since he himself also obtains all the endowments of divinity, he is also himself God, but God from Him. Moreover, in the discourse which is called the *Logos* <note>original: "λόγος" - The Word or Reason.</note>, addressing the Son of God, he says: *Son of the blessed one, and of good will, whose name cannot be named by a human mouth.* Augustine cites this passage from the earlier writings, as does Cyril—both most learned and holy men. We see in these things that Christian wisdom and theology are indeed eternal with God, but have always radiated with some splendor in the world. This was fully declared in these most recent ages so that all might recognize what our Creator felt, saw, knew, and tasted in the beginning; so that it might be known by all, through God manifesting his own divinity, what once existed among a few through oracles and successions. But let us set forth certain testimonies concerning Mercury <note>Hermes</note> himself, the Sibyls, Zoroaster, and the Magi, so that their theology may be more fully proven.

### CHAPTER XXV.

<image-desc>A decorative drop cap 'P' featuring classical architectural elements and foliate scrolls.</image-desc>
It may be doubted whether these things were not indeed spoken by Hermes Trismegistus, but were forged by those who came later. But there were no ancient theologians or philosophers to whom the doctrine of this divine man was not most clearly seen, so that they called him "Thrice-Greatest" <note>original: "Ter maximum"</note> on account of his unheard-of genius and his wonderful, manifold knowledge of all things. It is the custom of the Greeks, when they wish to describe something supreme and perfect, to say that very thing is "Thrice." Homer calls the "thrice-blessed" <note>original: "τρισμάκαρας"</note> those who are in every way most happy. In the same way, they call the most wicked man "thrice-accursed" <note>original: "τρισκατάρατον"</note>, thrice-ex...

<summary>This page argues that ancient "profane" philosophers like Hermes Trismegistus actually recognized the eternal divinity of the Word (Logos), contradicting heretical views like Arianism. It asserts that Christian truths have always been present in the world, even before the coming of Christ, through a lineage of ancient sages.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the origins and authority of Hermes Trismegistus, establishing him as a primary source of wisdom for the Greeks and a figure comparable to Moses.</meta>

->AUGUSTINUS EUGUBINUS, BOOK 1.<-

...venerated. Thus, they called him "Thrice Great" <note>Latin: *Ter Maximum*; the title Hermes Trismegistus</note> because they perceived him to be in every respect the greatest and most wonderful. This was the title used by the ancient Greeks who, having traveled to Egypt and spent a long time conversing with the priests, recognized the abundance and greatness of the genius of such a man. Therefore, those who think he is called Thrice Great because of three offices—that he was a priest, a king, and a prophet, and all of these in the highest degree—do not, in my opinion, guess correctly; nor do they know or notice the force of the Greek language. Plato had a full view of this man, drank from him entirely, and drew his own sources into his books from him, so that whatever more divine or celestial quality we admire in Plato proceeded not from Moses or the prophets, but rather from Mercury <note>The author uses the Roman name Mercury interchangeably with the Greek Hermes</note>. In the *Phaedrus*, Plato writes that he was considered a God among the Egyptians, to whom the Ibis bird was sacred; he was the first inventor of numbers, the science of calculation, and likewise of Geometry, Astronomy, dice, and gaming pieces, as well as letters. In the *Philebus*, he describes the skill with which he distinguished the elements of speech and letters, and established the art of reading. Philo the Phoenician, according to Eusebius, writes that Sanchuniathon—who was the illustrator of Phoenician Theology—said that of all mortals whom the Sun beholds, this man was the wisest; and that he followed him as a prince and leader in tracing the most ancient origins of things and in describing all Theology, having translated into his native tongue whatever books that same man had written from the Egyptian language. These books, according to Clement, Josephus the Jew translated from the Phoenician language into Greek. He also calls Mercury "Toautus," saying he was called *Thoyth* (θωῦθ) by the Egyptians and *Thoth* by the Alexandrians. In Plato, he is always called *Theuth* (θεὺθ). Cicero, in *On the Nature of the Gods*, calls him *Thoyth*. The Platonists worship him with divine cult. Hence Iamblichus, beginning his work *On the Mysteries*, says: <note>original Greek: θεὸς ὤν λόγων ἡγεμὼν ἑρμῆς πάλαι δέδεικται καλῶς ἅπασι τοῖς ἱερεῦσι εἶναι κοινὸς</note> "Mercury, being the God who is the leader of words, has long been rightly shown to be common to all priests." The same author states that the ancients were accustomed to dedicate all their discoveries and all monuments of their genius to him. He mentions that there were certain very ancient columns of his in Egypt, which Pythagoras and Plato saw and read, from which they gave themselves over to Philosophy. Plutarch says: <note>original Greek: ἑρμῶν δὲ γραμματικῆς, καὶ μουσικῆς εὑρετὴν νομίζοντες</note> "The Egyptians hold Mercury to be the inventor of grammar and music." Lactantius and Augustine, most learned men, used his testimonies; they would not have done so unless they held his teaching in high esteem. Apuleius the Platonist also translated his divine dialogue into Latin. Certain of his books were translated into the Greek language by those who traveled to Egypt for the love of Philosophy, just as Horus Apollo was also translated. Cyril reports that a certain Athenian translated fifteen of his books, titled *Hermaica*. There he repeats first what Plato writes about Mercury in the *Phaedrus*. Regarding other matters, he writes of him: <note>original Greek: τελειὸς ὤν, καὶ τμήμασι θεῶν πλησιάσας καὶ πεφρονικὼς εὑρίσκεται τὰ μώσεως... (The Greek text describes Hermes as a perfected man who approached the divine and reflected the teachings of Moses, though not perfectly)</note> "Being perfected, and having approached the divisions of the gods, he is found to have contemplated the things of Moses, even if not entirely correctly and without fault. But at least in part, he benefited from the Mosaic writings." Mention of him is also made by the one who wrote the books called *Hermaica* (that is, Mercurial) separately at Athens. He introduces a certain priest speaking in this manner: "Have you not heard of our Hermes, how he distributed Egypt into private parts and lots, measuring the fields with a cord?" and so forth. By these things it is established that that Mercury was truly divine, full of learning, a man of extraordinary wisdom and incomparable genius, so that it is no wonder he wrote the aforementioned things. Also regarding his columns, which were in Egypt...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the ancient origins of philosophy, specifically linking the wisdom of Egypt and the Magi to the later systems of Pythagoras and Plato.</meta>

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<margin>A</margin> That Egypt was seen to be full of many mysteries is mentioned not only by Iamblichus <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 245–325 AD) who wrote on the Egyptian mysteries.</note> but also by Proclus: <note>original: "μαρτυρᾶσι δὲ καὶ οἱ προφῆται φασὶ τῶν αἰγυπτίων ἐν σήλαις ταῖς ἔτι σωζομέναις, ταῦτα γέγρα-φθαι."</note> "The prophets of the Egyptians also bear witness," he says, "stating that these things were written on columns which are preserved even until now." And again in the same book: <note>original: "ἡ δὲ μνήμη διὰ τῆς ἱσορίας, αὕτη δὲ ἀπὸ τῶν στηλῶν, ἐν αἷς ἀνεγράφοντο τὰ παράδοξα, καὶ τὰ τῶν θαυμαστῶν ἄξια, τῶν πραγμάτων, ἅτε ἐν πράξεσιν, ἅτε ἐν ἐννοίαισιν."</note> "Memory came from history, and history from the columns on which were inscribed wonderful and noteworthy things, whether they were discoveries or great deeds." That Pythagoras and Plato also followed the theology of the Magi and of Zoroaster (so that the true cause may become known as to how Plato was able to speak of the three kings, of the Cause, and of its Father) and that those "Magical Oracles" <note>A collection of esoteric verses attributed to the Chaldeans/Magi.</note> truly belong to them—since their meanings are found in Plato, who imitated the Magi—is attested by the eminent Platonist Plethon, surnamed Gemistos, in his work against Scholarios: <note>original: "ἐφιλοσόφησε, τὴν ἰδίαν ἑαυτοῦ φιλοσοφίαν τεμὼν..."</note> "He philosophized," he says, "not by cutting out a private philosophy of his own, but by following the philosophy of Zoroaster which came down to him through the Pythagoreans. For Pythagoras, having associated with the Magi in Asia who descended from Zoroaster, pursued this philosophy, or perhaps that of a later Zoroaster. Others say much more: that he lived five thousand years before the Trojan War. Thus, while this may not be certain, he would at least be the most ancient of all those named as wise men and lawgivers. The priests of the Egyptians especially adopted the doctrines of Zoroaster at that time, and those of the Jews, at least regarding the doctrines; and that Plato pursued this philosophy—the teachings from Zoroaster—is shown by the oracles still preserved among us, which are entirely and in every way in harmony with the opinions of Plato." And to Bessarion: <note>Cardinal Bessarion, a key figure in bringing Greek learning to the West.</note> <note>original: "οὐ γὰρ τἀκεῖνα μάλιστα λόγια τὴν ἀπὸ ζωροάστρου μάγων, οἷς καὶ πυθαγόρας καὶ πλάτων ἠκο-λούθησε."</note> "For those oracles belong especially to the Magi of Zoroaster, whom both Pythagoras and Plato followed." Plato philosophized, he says, not with a peculiar or private philosophy he discovered himself, but by following the philosophy of Zoroaster, which reached him through the Pythagoreans. For Pythagoras learned this philosophy in Asia from the Magi who were descended from Zoroaster. Plutarch is a witness that Zoroaster 
<margin>B</margin> preceded the Trojan War by five thousand years. Although this may perhaps seem incredible, he is certainly the most ancient of all who are famous either in philosophy or in the making of laws. The priests of the Egyptians took up the dogmas of this Zoroaster and sought great glory for themselves from them. Moreover, that Plato practiced this philosophy is indicated by the "Magical Oracles" which descend from Zoroaster and are preserved even to our times, being altogether consistent and congruent with the opinions of Plato. Nor indeed are these things found only in the oracles or magical sayings which Pythagoras and Plato followed. By this testimony of Plethon, a man great in the philosophy of Plato, it becomes clear that those oracles or sayings which have been applied everywhere above truly belonged to the Magi themselves, whose decrees may be discerned throughout the entire philosophy of the Pythagoreans and of Plato. For the philosopher Psellus <note>Michael Psellus, an 11th-century Byzantine scholar.</note> testifies to the same things as Plethon: that Pythagoras and Plato adopted those magical dogmas in part. This is the origin, this is that concord: that while the magical oracle speaks of "two minds," Plato also wrote of the "Sovereign Cause" and its "Begetter." He saw this in the secret dogmas of Mercury <note>Hermes Trismegistus.</note> and the Magi. Iamblichus also, in his book *On the Mysteries*, venerates and follows this as the ancient and truth-telling theology of the Assyrians. Regarding the Sibyls and Trismegistus, the Platonist Hermias, in his commentary on Plato’s *Phaedrus*, speaks as follows (and he was not a Christian) so that it may be clear that great and ancient men had faith in the songs of the Sibyls, the magical oracles, and Trismegistus: <note>original: "περὶ δὲ τῶν σιβύλλας, ὅπως ἔσει θαύμασα τὰ λεγόμενα..."</note> "Concerning the Sibyls, the things said are so wonderful as to seem like myths; for there have been many Sibyls, all choosing this life. They were all called Sibyls for a certain reason, just as Hermes Trismegistus is said to have remembered himself after traveling often to Egypt, and was called Hermes in a certain way. And many Orpheuses arose among the Thracians; perhaps, therefore, these women also have some commonality and memory and chose these titles. For the Erythraean Sibyl was called so from the beginning when she was very young in the temple at Erythraea; they say that as soon as she came forth, she addressed each person by name, spoke in verse, and [prophesied] in a short time..."

<summary>This page argues that the philosophies of Plato and Pythagoras were not original inventions but were derived from ancient Egyptian, Magian, and Zoroastrian sources. It cites various Neoplatonic and Byzantine scholars to prove a "perennial" chain of wisdom preserved on ancient columns and in sacred oracles.</summary>
<keywords>Zoroaster, Plato, Pythagoras, Egypt, Magi, Magical Oracles, Plethon, Iamblichus, Hermes Trismegistus, Sibyls, ancient theology, Neoplatonism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of ancient prophetic figures, specifically focusing on the Sibyls and their historical validation by classical authors like Plato, Hermias, and Plutarch.</meta>

# AUGUSTINUS EUGUBINUS, BOOK I.
->D<-

<note>original Greek: "taking the form of a man"</note> taking the form of a man. Regarding the Sibyl, he says, the things reported are so marvelous that they are almost considered fables. Yet there were many Sibyls, all following the same way of life, and perhaps all called "Sibyls" by themselves for a rational cause: just as it is said that Trismegistus <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the "Thrice-Great," a legendary Hellenistic figure associated with magic and philosophy</note>, who often lived in Egypt, made mention of himself and was called Hermes for the third time. It is also said that three Orpheuses existed among the Thracians. Perhaps, therefore, these women chose these titles because of some communication or shared memorial among them. Since this very Erythraean Sibyl, of whom Plato now speaks, was called Herophile from the beginning. They say that as soon as she was born, she called everyone by their proper name, spoke in rhythmic verses, and in a short time developed into the perfect likeness of a human being. Thus he writes. It is clear, then, that the most learned men both believed in the Sibyls and placed faith in their songs. Regarding Trismegistus and the Chaldeans, the same faith exists among the most erudite. With these words of Hermias and the testimonies concerning the Sibyl, those things written by Plutarch in the work he titled *Why the Pythia Ceased to Respond in Verse* agree in a wonderful manner. And first he reports of her: <note>original Greek: "The Sibyl, with frenzied mouth, according to Heraclitus, uttering things unsmiling, unadorned, and unperfumed, reaches over a thousand years with her voice through the god."</note> The Sibyl, he says, speaking with a frenzied mouth according to Heraclitus, unadorned, disordered, and unpleasing, has surpassed a thousand years with her voice, by the inspiration of God. Here Plutarch, when giving the reason why the oracle of Apollo ceased to respond in song as it once did, first says it was the custom of ancient times that not only oracles, but also history and philosophy were written in meter, citing the examples of Orpheus, Hesiod, Parmenides, Empedocles, Xenophanes, Pherecydes, and Thales, who wrote what they wrote in meter: thus the Sibyl uttered many oracles in verse, embracing thousands of future ages with her voice. Truly she surpassed many years with her voice, as one who sang not only of intermediate times, accidents, calamities, and many wars, but also of those things that would come on the final day. Concerning the predicted wars and other calamities, the same Plutarch is a witness. If, he says, you think the things told of the Sibyls' prophecies are fables: <note>original Greek: "But these things are attested by many destructions and migrations of Greek cities, many appearances of barbarian armies, and the overthrows of empires; and these recent and new sufferings around Cuma and Puteoli, not sung of old but chanted through the Sibylline verses, time has paid back like a debt: eruptions of mountain fire and boilings of the sea."</note> but many destructions or migrations of Greek cities attest to the prophecies: many arrivals of barbarian armies, and the slaughters of famous men. For have not these new and recent calamities around Cuma and Puteoli <note>Modern Pozzuoli, near Naples, an area of high volcanic activity</note>, predicted and chanted in the songs of the Sibyls, been rendered by time like a debtor? The breakings-forth of mountain fire, the boiling of the sea among the rocks, the fires kindled by the winds, the destruction of so many and such great cities, so that those who arrived by day, the whole region being confused, could not know in what place these cities had been inhabited. Thus Plutarch, adding that these things could not have been predicted without divinity. He testifies that the same Sibyls predicted the wars of the Romans against the Carthaginians, and that they would carry arms against all nations, five hundred years before: <note>original Greek: "But the order reveals the foreknowledge, and the fact that they foretold to the Romans, five hundred years before or after, the time in which all nations would go to war at once."</note> But, he says, the order of the ages declares that they foresaw these things, and they predicted a time of five hundred years in which the Romans would battle against all nations. The same Plutarch, just as Hermias, testifies that the Erythraean Sibyl was called Herophile: <note>original Greek: "They called Herophile the Erythraean prophetess who became a Sibyl."</note> They called Herophile the Erythraean seer, who was a Sibyl. Regarding the first Sibyl he also reports: <note>original Greek: "For a sign was given near the rock."</note> and what follows. After we sat on the rock, which is in the forum, upon which it is said the first Sibyl sat, descending from Helicon, from the Muses...

<summary>This page discusses the historical authenticity of the Sibyls, citing classical authorities like Plutarch and Hermias to argue that their prophetic verses accurately predicted major historical events and natural disasters.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "Perennial Philosophy," moving from the prophetic authority of the Sibyls to the theological concept of the Divine Word (Logos) as found in Philo of Alexandria and Hermes Trismegistus.</meta>

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<margin>A</margin> ...educated by the Muses. Some indeed say that she came to Meleona, the daughter of Lamia, who was born of Neptune. Serapion mentions her poems, in which she praised herself, claiming that even in death she would not lose the power of prophecy, or rather, that even when dead she would not cease to prophesy. Thus says Plutarch. Furthermore, Justin Martyr <note>A 2nd-century Christian apologist and philosopher.</note> and Philosopher invites the nations to believe the Sibyl: *Believe, as I have said, the most ancient and very old Sibyl, whose books happen to be preserved in the whole inhabited world.* <note>original Greek: πείσθητε ὡς ἔφην τῇ ἀρχαιοτάτῃ @ σφόδρα παλαιᾷ σιβύλλῃ ἧς τὰς βίβλους ἐν πάσῃ τῇ οἰκουμένῃ σώζεσθαι συμβαίνῃ.</note> He says: Believe the most ancient and by far the oldest Sibyl, whose books it has happened are preserved in the whole world. Lactantius also, in his book *On the Wrath of God*, says that many of the greatest Greek authors wrote about the Sibyls. He also says: "The volumes of the Cumaean Sibyl, in which the fates of the Romans are written, are kept in secret; however, the books of almost all the others are not forbidden from being in common use." Thus says Lactantius. You see how great is the agreement of all the most learned men concerning the Sibyls and their poems.

---

->*Concerning the Divine Word, from Philo the Jew, by whose sayings it may be proved that the truly ancient Mercury <note>Referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Egyptian sage.</note> wrote the aforementioned things concerning the Word itself. And the manifold Theology concerning it in Philo: that it is the creator of the World and the origin, that it is the Son of God, and the archetype of man.*<-

### CHAPTER XXVI.

<margin>B</margin> BUT THAT the most ancient Mercury perceived and handed down the aforementioned things concerning the Divine Word, all the admirable Theology of the Platonists and the Hebrews proclaims. Nor is it possible to find any other source for those mysteries contained in the books of the Platonists than the ancient contemplation of the Egyptians and the Hebrews. Among the Hebrew theologians is Philo <note>Philo of Alexandria, a Hellenistic Jewish philosopher who sought to harmonize the Torah with Greek philosophy.</note>, whom a Greek proverb equals with Plato, and who tempered the Egyptian and Greek—especially the Platonic—philosophy with the Mosaic. He sings wonderful things, like an oracle, concerning the Divine Word, and exactly the same things as Trismegistus and our John <note>The Apostle John, author of the Fourth Gospel.</note>. Indeed, I have often thought that Philo spoke so many and such clear things concerning the Divine Word from the same secret Theology of the Hebrews as did John, who was also a Hebrew. For beyond the fact that John was able to write his Theology from the breast of Christ, he also wrote things which were previously held among the Hebrew writers of secret matters; many such sublime mysteries, later better explained by the Apostles, can be detected among the ancient Hebrews. Therefore, what the Apostle John, a Hebrew, said—*In the beginning was the Word <note>original Greek: λόγος (Logos)</note>, and the Word was with God, and all things were made through him*—he said in order to teach that the Word was the creator of things, and that he existed before the world, as the one through whom the world was created. Philo, not a Christian but a Jew, speaks many things of the same kind throughout all his books. In the book *On the Allegory of the Laws*: *He called the Word of God a book, in which it happens that the compositions of all things are written and engraved.* <note>original Greek: βιβλίον δὲ εἴρηκεν ἐν τῷ θεῷ λόγῳ, ᾧ συμβέβηκεν ἐγγράφεσθαι, καὶ ἐγχαράττεσθαι τὰς τῶν ὅλων συστάσεις.</note> Moses, he says, allegorically calls the Word of God a "book," in which the existences of all things are described and impressed. By this testimony, Philo declares the same Theology as John and the Platonists mentioned above concerning the Mind. For as the former said, "All things were made through him," so the latter claims that in the Word of God (which he always calls the creator), as if in a book, there exist the reasons and essences of all things—like a supreme form, most capable of all things to be created, a vast origin, an infinite Mind, a most broad majesty, a most copious power for creating things, a reason pregnant with all things. Also in the book concerning those things which followed the six days <note>Referring to Philo's commentary on Genesis.</note>, he writes this: *That which is most generative is God, and second is the Word of God.* <note>original Greek: ὁ δὲ γενικώτατόν ἐστιν ὁ θεὸς, καὶ δεύτερος ὁ θεοῦ λόγος.</note> The most powerful and especially generative thing is God, and second, the Word of God. This also is the Theology of John: "All things through him..."

<summary>This page argues for a universal theological truth (the Perennial Philosophy) by linking the prophecies of the Sibyls and the "Divine Word" (Logos) of Philo Judaeus to the Gospel of John and Hermetic traditions. It emphasizes that the concept of the Word as the creator and archetype of the world was shared by ancient Hebrews, Egyptians, and Platonists.</summary>
<keywords>Sibyls, Justin Martyr, Lactantius, Philo Judaeus, Hermes Trismegistus, Logos, Divine Word, Platonism, Hebrew Theology, Creation, Gospel of John</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Word of God" (Logos) as the intermediary of creation, drawing parallels between the Gospel of John, Platonic philosophy, and the writings of Philo of Alexandria.</meta>

-> AUGUSTINUS STEUCHUS OF GUBBIO, BOOK I. <-

<margin>C</margin> All things were made through Him. Because the Word of God is most especially generative and fruitful, he is the second, as the Platonists used to say above, after God. Our own Theology also speaks of the Father Almighty, creator of heaven and earth, concerning the Son through whom all things were made. Philo, calling the Son something of this sort, calls him the "Second." Because all things are from the Father through the Son, by which the union of the divinity is demonstrated. For both create, and both are most generative. And he calls the Word "Second," that is, in rank, not in nature or divinity after the Father. Both are God, because both create the world. However, there cannot be two creators of the world, and there cannot be two Gods, because God is the Supreme Nature. Moreover, there cannot be a Supreme except for one. In the book, however, *That Dreams are Sent from God*: <note>original Greek: κατά δευτερον δὲ τρόπον ὁ θεῖος λόγος, τόπος ἐστὶ, ὃν ἐκπεπλήρωκεν ὅλον, δι' ὅλων ἀσωμάτοις δυνάμεσιν αὐτὸς ὁ Θεός</note> "According to another mode, the divine Word is the place which God himself has filled entirely throughout with incorporeal powers." Philo explains this Theology, which is found in John concerning the divine Word, with this same testimony: which name <note>The term "Word" or "Logos"</note> was bestowed by the ancient Philosophers because of the creation of the world, not because of spiritual birth, as it seemed to some later. And he says the same thing about this Word as John, "All things were made through him": Philo says that God filled him with incorporeal virtues, that is, with eternal Ideas, prolific virtues, and everlasting reasons, the abundance of which he held from God his Father, as Plotinus <note>A major Neoplatonic philosopher (c. 204–270 AD)</note> also testifies concerning the Mind. And just as John, saying "He was with God," calls the Father specifically "God": so Philo says that God filled the Word with incorporeal virtues, namely that the Word received the power of creating the world from the Father, being begotten and sent by him to create the world. I say "sent," speaking in a human manner and declaring a divine matter by a corporeal name. He calls this the "place of incorporeal Ideas," as if it were a vast Mind, a most ample kingdom of Ideas, origins, reasons, and forms; a most fertile and productive world of things to be created, an infinite origin. <margin>D</margin> Moreover, he says the whole was filled by God, as if begotten prolific from every side. And all this Theology looks toward the creation of the world, whose principle all confess to be the Word of God, the greatest shaper, the most widespread wisdom, possessing all virtues, by which, as if by his own origins, he might produce all things. In the same book also, concerning the two Firsts: <note>original Greek: ὧν τὸ μὲν ἕτερον, θεὸς ἐστὶ λόγος: τὸ δὲ ἕτερον ὁ πρὸ τοῦ λόγου θεός. ὁ δὲ ξεναγηθεὶς ὑπὸ σοφίας εἰς τὸν πρότερον ἀφικνᾶται τόπον εὑράμενος τῇ ἀρεσκίας κεφαλὴν, ᾧ τέλος τὸν θεῖον λόγον, ἐν ᾧ γενόμενος οὐ φθάνῃ πρὸς τὸ εἶναι θεὸν, ἐλθεῖν, ἀλλ' αὐτὸν ὁρᾷ μακρόθεν</note> "Of which one indeed is the divine Word: but the other is God, who is before the Word. But he—that is, the Intellect and man himself—led by Wisdom, arrives at the first place, having found the source and end of pleasure in the divine Word. Placed in which, he does not reach the point of becoming God, but gazes upon Him from afar." In this place also, Philo commemorates both the divine Word and God, who is before the word, calling the Father himself "God," just as John says, "the Word was with God." Then he says that the divine Word is the principle and the end of pleasure, happiness, and contemplation—love, desire, and joy, as our own scriptures sing. All this is called in Greek <term>areskeia</term> <note>original: ἀρέσκεια; meaning "pleasingness" or "satisfaction"</note>. Through this very word, he says men gaze upon God from afar; that His Word is the Guide for reaching God: just as He <note>Christ</note> called himself the "door" in the world, by which one reaches the father. And he says that first, through wisdom, one reaches the Son: from there, God is seen from afar. And it is a most profound Theology, which I think Philo found in the monuments of his ancestors; likewise celebrated among the Chaldeans and Egyptians. In the same book he writes: <note>original Greek: τὸ μὲν παράδειγμα ὁ πληρέστατος ἦν αὐτοῦ λόγος</note> "The exemplar," he says, "was his most full Word." Thus in this place also Philo denotes that the Son of God is the origin of the world. "Exemplar" on account of created things; "most full" on account of fertility and the infinite power of creating things, the form of things...

<summary>Steuchus argues that the concept of the "Word" (Logos) as a secondary divine principle and creator is shared by the Gospel of John, Platonists, and Philo of Alexandria. He emphasizes that the Word acts as the "place of Ideas" and the intermediary through which humanity contemplates the Father.</summary>

--- Page 81 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "Perennial Philosophy," specifically examining how the Jewish philosopher Philo of Alexandria used terms that align with Christian theology regarding the Word of God (Logos).</meta>

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<note>The author is citing Philo Judaeus, a Hellenistic Jewish philosopher who sought to harmonize Torah with Greek philosophy.</note>

...infinite Wisdom, the mother of all things. In the same book, regarding the meanings of the "Sun," he says: **"He calls the divine Word the seventh meaning of the Sun, which traverses the heavens."** <note>original Greek: φησὶ τὸ ζ' ἤτοι σημαινόμενον ἥλιον καλεῖ τὸν θεῖον λόγον, τὸν τὰ κατ᾽ οὐρανὸν περιπολοῦντα</note> According to this third <note>The text notes "third" here despite the Greek "seventh" above, likely a citation discrepancy</note> meaning, he calls the divine Word the Sun of Him who walks through the heavens. What more wonderful thing could Philo say about the Word of God? That He is called the Sun, He who said of Himself, "I am the light of the world." And the Church sings, "For from You has arisen the Sun of Justice, Christ our God." This naming refers to the great divinity of the Word, His universal and incorporeal brilliance, and a clarity invisible to human eyes—a clarity that cannot be more truly designated than by the name of the Sun. In this same sense, John said of the heavenly city, "And the city has no need of the Sun, nor of the Moon, to shine in it. For the glory of God illuminates it, and its lamp is the Lamb, and they need no light of a lamp, nor light of the Sun, because God illuminates them." So also the same John says, "He was the true light, which illuminates the whole world." Moreover, when he speaks of "walking through the heaven," he refers either to the Father Himself, whose majesty is in heaven, filling everything within and without the world, or certainly to the Sun of Him who walks through the entire heaven. This is what Plato said of Jupiter, and the Psalm also: "Who ascends the heaven of heavens to the East." Which in Hebrew is: "Who rides upon the *Araboth*" <note>Hebrew: עֲרָבוֹת, often translated as the highest heavens or clouds</note>, the celestial orbs, or the supreme heaven. Also in the same book: **"For there are, as it seems, two temples of God: one is this world, in which the High Priest is His first-born divine Word; the other is the rational soul, whose priest is the true man."** <note>original Greek: δύο γὰρ ὡς ἔοικεν ἱερὰ θεοῦ, ἓν μὲν ὁ δὲ ὁ κόσμος ἐν ᾧ καὶ ἀρχιερεύς ὁ πρωτόγονος αὐτοῦ θεῖος λόγος. ἕτερον δὲ λογικὴ ψυχὴ, ἧς ἱερεύς, ὁ πρὸς ἀλήθειαν ἄνθρωπος</note> What could be clearer, more divine, or more wonderful? That the High Priest of the temple, which is the whole world, is the divine Word, the first-born. The world is the temple of God. Of this temple, the divine and supreme priest is the first-born of God, His Word. Because, of course, He is King and Lord of the world, ruling and sustaining all things, the creator and nourisher. 

<margin>B</margin> Paul also calls Him the High Priest, who offered a sacrifice for sins, a mediator and negotiator between men and God. For this reason, Dionysius <note>Dionysius the Areopagite, a Christian theologian and philosopher</note> also calls Him the **"Leader of the Rites"** and **"Guide of the Mysteries,"** <note>original Greek: τελετάρχην, μυσταγωγὸν</note> the prince of initiations and mysteries. He is the first initiator and sanctifier, who initiated us into His mysteries, especially the knowledge and worship of His Father. This the greatest Priest in the whole world has done and continues to do. And here are three divine names for the Son of God found in Philo: Divine Word, High Priest, and First-born. This is the highest theology of the Apostles. How divine it is also that he calls the world the temple of God, in which the invisible God is cherished, adored, known, and praised! So too, that the soul is the temple of God is our own theology: "You are the temple of God," says Paul, "God's building." Philo also, in the same book, speaking of this very thing: **"The image of God, His messenger Word."** <note>original Greek: τὴν τοῦ θεοῦ εἰκόνα, τὸν ἄγγελον αὐτοῦ λόγον</note> Here also are three most divine names of the Son of God, frequent in true theology: The Image of God—for Paul also calls Him that, and the "Character" <note>or "exact imprint"</note>. He is likewise the Messenger <note>Angelos</note>, as in Isaiah, the "Messenger of Great Counsel." For this reason He is also called **Logos**, the speech of God, the utterance of God, the messenger of God, speaking and doing all things on behalf of God, as a mediator between us and Him. In these names is all theology. Also in the book *On Agriculture*: "All these things," he says, **"God, as Shepherd and King, leads according to justice and law, having set over them His upright Word, His first-born Son, who shall take up the care of this sacred flock like a lieutenant of a great King."** <note>original Greek: ποιμὴν καὶ βασιλεὺς θεὸς ἄγει κατὰ δίκην, καὶ νόμον, ἐφιστάμενος, τὸν ὀρθὸν αὐτοῦ λόγον πρωτόγονον υἱὸν, ὃς τὴν ἐπιμέλειαν τῆς ἱερᾶς ταύτης ἀγέλης, οἷά τις μεγάλου βασιλέως ὕπαρχος διαδέξεται</note> In these words, Philo openly names the first-born Son as the Word, and says that He looks after His flock, the whole world, and especially the human race. The Son Himself also said this, calling men His sheepfold, and the men entrusted to Him by the Father His sheep. Finally, He said that the entire administration, redemption, and care of the human race was handed over to Him by the Father, and that He was sent by Him.

<summary>This page explores the writings of Philo Judaeus to demonstrate that ancient Jewish philosophy anticipated Christian concepts of the Logos (the Word) as the "Sun," "High Priest," and "First-born Son." The author aligns Philo's descriptions of the Word's role in governing the world and the soul with the teachings of St. Paul and St. John.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Philo Judaeus’s theology, specifically focusing on the nature of the Logos (the Word) as the intermediary agent of creation, following the previous page's exploration of the Son's relationship to the Father.</meta>

# AUGUSTINUS STEUCO, BOOK I.

...sent into this world, acting upon what his Father had commanded. And for that reason, Philo
<margin>C</margin> calls him a *hyparchos* <note>original: ὕπαρχον; a subordinate governor or lieutenant</note>, a magistrate, a minister, a "sub-commander," if I may speak thus, obeying the command of another: commanding indeed, but under another commander, namely the Father, who sends him to all things. And that is the reason for the title *Logos* <note>original: λόγου</note>, which is more correctly translated as *Speech* or *Discourse* than as *Word*. For that name implies a speaker, a messenger <note>original: internuntium</note>, and not, as I believe, a spiritual birth. Also, in the book *That Divinity is Unchangeable*: *For the divine Word dances in a circle, whom the men of old called Fortune; then, always flowing through the cities, nations, and lands of others, he distributes the affairs of all to all, changing the possessions of each in endless cycles of time.* <note>original: χορεύει γὰρ ἐν κύκλῳ λόγος ὁ θεῖος... Steuco provides the Greek text followed by his Latin translation, here rendered into English.</note> For the Word walks around, which most people call Fortune. Then, always traveling through cities, and nations, and regions that belong to some, he distributes the affairs of all to others and to everyone, in free times, changing what individuals possess. Here also Philo shows the Son of God, the Word, to be the ruler, governor, and moderator of the Universe, just as he said above that the care of the flock was given by the Father to the Son. Indeed, all these things tirelessly harmonize with the Sacred Scriptures. He says that He walks invisibly through the world. For the same reason, he called him above a *hyparchos*, a magistrate. He says that He distributes all things, turns and overturns kingdoms, and changes riches and wealth, all of which belongs to the one moderating the world. *Upholding all things,* he says, *by the word of his power.* <note>Hebrews 1:3</note> And *in whom are all things, from whom are all things, through whom are all things.* Likewise, in the book *On the Flaming Sword*: *Departing therefore from particular constructions, behold the greatest house, or city, this world; for you will find its cause to be God, by whom it was made; its matter the four elements, from which it was compounded; the instrument the Word of God, through whom it was constructed; and the cause of the construction the goodness of the Creator.* <note>original: μετάλθων οὖν ἀπὸ τῶν κατὰ μέρος... Steuco quotes Philo's Greek and then translates it.</note> Having departed, therefore, from individual works, look upon the greatest house, or city: this world. For you will find its cause to be God, by whom it was made; its matter, the four elements, of which it consists; the Instrument, moreover, the Word of God, through whom it was built; and the cause of the building,
<margin>D</margin> the goodness of the maker. How much Theology is in this place also! He calls the whole world a city, or a house; its maker and cause, God; the matter, the four elements; the Instrument—that is, the mediating cause—the Word of God. And he names four causes: the efficient, God himself; the material, the four elements; the Organic <note>or instrumental</note>, or the moving cause, serving the first; and the final cause, goodness, or even the efficient cause. These are the mysteries of our Theology, proclaiming God the creator of heaven and earth as the principal cause. But the Son, through whom all things were made, is expressed in Greek, just as in Philo, as *di' hou* <note>original: δι' οὗ</note>; for the Greek preposition *dia* <note>original: διά</note> designates a medium. And what Philo expressed as the "Instrument" <note>original: Organum</note>, that is, the mover who was moved by another: the Son was moved by the Father toward creation, and He used him as a craftsman; He commanded him to create, having given him the power of creating. Therefore, the world was created by the Father and the Son: by the Father as the first cause, willing, dictating, and commanding; by the Son, obeying, having been ordered and moved by the Father. And so there was one creator, and because both created, both are God. But since none is good except one, there is necessarily one divinity: which reason itself declares and Theology demonstrates. For the world was not created as if by two different authors, but by two as if one: by the principal cause, God, and by the cause moved by God himself, the Son. For the Son would not have created if the Father had not willed it, and if He had not given the power. He obeyed the Father, therefore, having received eternal power from Him. This matter is clear from human examples. Therefore, Philo says now what John says, using a similar preposition: *All things were made through him.* <note>John 1:3</note> Philo, however, says: *through whom it was constructed.* And that is the reason why he called him the "Instrument." You see, therefore, even here a most profound mystery: which you will also find in Plato, Orpheus, and Hermes Trismegistus; and they all agree with one another most divinely, preaching the theology that has been most radiantly revealed in these last ages. Also in the book in which he discusses the Lord appearing to Abraham, he says: *the Word is his interpreter* <note>original: ὁ ὑποφήτης αὐτοῦ λόγος</note>...

<summary>Agostino Steuco analyzes Philo Judaeus's description of the Logos as the "Instrument" and "sub-commander" of creation, arguing that Philo's Greek terminology aligns perfectly with Christian doctrine regarding the Son's role as the mediating cause of the universe.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Perennial Philosophy," specifically focusing on the nature of the Divine Word (Logos) as the intermediary between God and the created world, drawing heavily on the works of Philo of Alexandria.</meta>

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<margin>A</margin> The messenger, the announcer, and the interpreter of God is His Word <note>original: "Verbum," referring to the Logos</note>. For the same reason, he is called an *interpreter* <note>original: ὐποφήτης (hypophetēs)</note>, a *magistrate* <note>original: hyparchus</note>, and *Logos*—which means speaker, speech, or discourse—and above that, an *Angel*. In the second book of his *Allegories of the Laws*, Philo also writes the following: *The shadow of God is His Word, which He used as an instrument when He was making the world. This shadow, being like a likeness, is the archetype of other things; just as God is the pattern for the image (which he has just called a shadow), so the image itself becomes a pattern for others. As Moses showed when beginning the Law, saying: "And God made man according to the image of God," implying that while the image was modeled after God, man was modeled after the image, which took on the power of a pattern.* <note>original Greek: σκιά δὲ θεοῦ... παραδείγματος. This passage argues that the Word is the "blueprint" for creation.</note> These words mean: The Shadow of God is His Word, which He used as an instrument to create the world. This shadow, or likeness, is the pattern for other things. Just as God is the pattern for the image (which we just called a shadow), so the image becomes a pattern for another. As Moses expressed when beginning the books of the Law, saying: *And God created man in the image of God.* It is as if the image itself was made to resemble God, but man was made according to that image, which had received the force of a pattern. Here too, Philo speaks of the greatest and highest mysteries: that the Word is like the shadow of God (for he could not use a more suitable name). But he also explains himself, saying this shadow is like a likeness. Our own scriptures, as we have said, also call the Son the "image of God." For Paul says to the Colossians: *Who is the image of the invisible God, the firstborn of every creature.* <note>original Greek: ὅς ἐστιν εἰκὼν... κτίσεως.</note> And Solomon calls him the *Image of His goodness*. Hence Christ himself said: *He who sees me, sees also my Father.* Again, Philo names him the *Instrument* <note>original: Organum</note> which God used for creation, implying he is the mediator, the agent, and the author; this is why Paul calls him the firstborn of every condition and creation—that is, preceding and going before all created things. Because (as Paul confesses in the same place) in him all things were made, celestial and terrestrial, visible and invisible: Angels, powers, dominions, thrones; all were made through him and for him, and he is before all things, and in him all things consist. These are the words of Paul.

<margin>B</margin> Philo, though a Jew, says nothing different. Regarding the Word, he sings a divine and most eminent Theology: just as the Word is the image of the Father (so that the Father is a pattern for him), so the Word is the pattern for man. He says this was declared by Moses when he wrote of the creation of man: *In the image of God He made him.* By this, Philo thinks it is signified that man was made according to Him who is the image of God. That image is the Son. Thus, in that saying of Moses, there are two patterns and two images: first, when he says *In the image of God*, that is the Son; therefore the Father is the pattern for the Son. But since man was created according to the likeness of that image, the Son will be the pattern for man himself. Thus the Son, in respect to his Father, is an image; in respect to man, he is a pattern. Philo heard from the highest Wisdom that the Son created the world as the instrument of God, and created man according to his own pattern. Although he is called both the pattern and the *Idea* of the world by the Platonists, whenever Moses speaks of the world or man being created by God, he implies that God who is the Instrument and the Image of his Father: an instrument in respect to creation, an image in respect to his nature, which he holds as equal to the Father. Therefore, it is necessary that he too is God; and if God, then also eternal. But just as in the sacred scriptures the Father is properly called "God" (as we have often shown, because he is primarily the begetter), so in Philo, the Father himself is properly God. And since he seeks the testimony of Moses on this—and Moses said, *And God created man, in the image of God he created him*—Philo wishes this creator to be the Son, who created the world as if commanded by the Father and acting as His instrument. It is clear even by Philo’s assertion that the Word is God. Therefore, when it says, *In the beginning God created the heaven and the earth*, if you listen to Philo the Jew, he says the Father and the Son created them: the Father as the primary Author, and the Son as the Wisdom and Instrument of the Father. Therefore the ancient Hebrews interpreted what is in Moses as *In the beginning* to mean *In Wisdom*. If you consult those ancients and Philo himself, Wisdom is that very Instrument; it is the firstborn son, the Word, full...

<summary>This page explores the concept of the Logos (the Word) as an intermediary "instrument" and "image" used by God in creation, harmonizing the Jewish philosophy of Philo with the Christian theology of St. Paul. It argues that the Son acts as the archetype or "pattern" for humanity while remaining the perfect image of the Father.</summary>
