# AUGUSTINUS EUGUBINUS, BOOK I.

God of Ideas, the archetype of man. The Apostles preached this same Theology throughout the whole world; of which, as I believe, the secret and pure traditions of the Hebrews were full. Hence He Himself said, *Search the scriptures, and you will find there concerning me.* <note>John 5:39</note> And, *Beginning from Moses and all the Prophets, he interpreted to them in all the scriptures the things that were concerning himself.* <note>Luke 24:27</note> Nor could it have happened otherwise than that Philo drew this Wisdom concerning the Father and the Son from some profound Theology revealed by oracles. In the same book: <note>original Greek: ὁ θεολόγος ὀξυδερκέστατός ἐστιν, ὡς ἐφορᾶν πᾶν ἱκανὸς</note> *The Word of God is of the sharpest eye, so that it is able to behold all things.* By this testimony he perceives, as above, that there is a Providence of the Son of God, whom he said walks through the whole world. Also in the book, *Who is the Heir of Divine Things*: <note>original Greek: ἀόρατος καὶ σπερματικὸς τεχνικὸς θεῖος λόγος</note> *The invisible, seminal, craftsman-like, divine Word.* By these names also he declares the power of creating with which the Son is endowed, calling him full of art, of seeds, of reasons, and of forms. In the same book: <note>original Greek: ἔτι τοίνυν τὴν οὐράνιον τροφὴν ψυχῆς, ἣν καλεῖ μάννα, διανέμει πᾶσι τοῖς χρησομένοις λόγος θεῖος ἐξ ἴσου περιφρονήσας ἰσότητος</note> Which is, *Furthermore, the divine Word distributes the heavenly food of the soul, which he calls Manna, to all who will use it, looking out for equality from an equal standpoint.* Philo perceives that Wisdom, which is called Manna, is given by the Word, whom he had previously called the overseer and the provident one.

When all things are collected, you will find in his writings that the Son, or the Word, is the creator of the world, is the greatest Wisdom of the world's creation, the fountain and kingdom of Ideas—that is, of origins, seeds, and forms—the creator of man, the governor of the world, the moderator of the human race, changing and transferring kingdoms, and giving wisdom. Although this Manna, which he now mentions, can be understood as heavenly blessedness, which is granted by the Son of God. Also in the same book: You will find, he says, <note>original Greek: δύο λόγους, ἕνα μὲν ἀρχέτυπον ὑπὲρ ἡμᾶς, ἕτερον δὲ μίμημα, τὸν καθ’ ἡμᾶς ὑπάρχοντα... [etc]</note> *That there are two Words, or Reasons: one indeed exemplary, above us; the other an imitation, which is ours. Moreover, Moses calls that one which is above us the Image of God; but that which is ours, the likeness of the image. For God made man, he says, not as the image, but according to the image. Whereby it happens that the mind of each one of us—which is indeed truly and properly the man—is a third expression from him who made it. The middle one, however, is the exemplar of this of ours, and an assimilation of that one.* Thus says Philo.

You have heard, therefore, of two either Minds or Reasons: one above us, the archetype and exemplar for ours, according to which ours was created, just as Moses declared; whom Philo the Jew also perceives to have been speaking about the Son, about the Word. And that this was he who created man according to his own likeness, because he was the exemplar for him, who, looking upon himself, created man. So that just as the Son himself is the image of the Father, so we are the image of him. And so if you listen to Philo, Moses preached and wrote divinely concerning the Son and the Word of God: so that the Greeks rightly and deservedly call the Son of God the <term>prototypon</term> <note>original: πρωτότυπον; the first or primary model</note>, the first example for man himself created by him: so that he was the true man, and from him the other man, our mind, according to his likeness. This is the most holy and eminent mystery of our Theology. Hence we sing to the Son himself: *Who in the beginning of the world formed the man Adam, to whom you gave a countenance similar to your image.* And so, with Philo the great Theologian as witness, he who created the world, who breathed in the human spirit and mind, and did the other things which are so often ascribed to God in the Old Testament, was the Word of God—the mediator, interpreter, messenger, and legate of the highest Father to man. Thus he carries out the roles of providence just as he carried out those of creation. In the same book: <note>original Greek: ὁ γὰρ θεῖος λόγος τὰ ἐν τῇ φύσει διεῖλε, καὶ διένειμε τὰ πάντα</note> *For the divine Word divided and distributed all things which are contained in nature.*

<summary>Steuco uses the writings of Philo of Alexandria to argue that the "Word" (Logos) is the divine architect and archetype of humanity, bridging the gap between Old Testament Hebrew tradition and Christian theology. He identifies the Word as the creator of man and the provider of spiritual "Manna."</summary>

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<margin>A</margin> Here also Philo repeats that the Word <note>original: "Verbum," referring to the Greek "Logos"</note> was the creator and the governor who arranged, distinguished, and distributed all things. Thus, it is that "Mind" <note>original: "Mens," referring to the "Nous" of Anaxagoras</note> of Anaxagoras, which arranged Chaos and reduced it into order. This is likewise referred to as Providence. Also in the same book: *The two natures are indeed indivisible: the one within us, which is reason, and that one above us, which is the divine Word.* Shortly after: *The mind within us, and the one above us.* That is to say, the two natures are therefore indivisible: that of reason which is in us, and that of the divine Word which is above us; the Mind which is in us, and that which is above us. Behold, he always recites the same things and renders them clearer, commemorating and celebrating the divine Word, calling it the Mind which is above us, and further stating that our own mind was formed according to it. He says both are of an indivisible nature, just as they are incorporeal. For with our mind is also reckoned the Mind of the Angels, which He himself also created, as will be made manifest later. In the same book he also writes: *Every human being, according to their intellect, is related to the divine Word, having become an impression, or a fragment, or a radiance of that blessed nature.* Every man, he says, according to the Mind, is made like to and communicates with the divine Word, having been made an expression, or a cutting, or a ray of that blessed nature. What could be deeper or more wonderful than these things? Behold again the creation of our soul, the divine Word, and that we have a society, affinity, and communication with it; that our soul is either a likeness of that blessed nature, or a certain cutting from it, or a radiation. For it is not possible to explain better what is handed down by Moses, when God breathed into man. This breath, which he intends to be our soul flowing from God, he calls either an "expression"—as when we produce the image of a seal in wax, so that such a man was formed according to the divine Word, the Word being the seal and man the wax—or a "ray," or a "cutting," by which arguments the immortality of the soul is declared.

<margin>B</margin> And since Aristotle himself confesses our soul to be *most akin* <note>original: "συγγενέστατον" (syngenestaton)</note>, most similar, neighboring, and of the same kind with God, behold how this ought to be understood: namely, as being similar to the divine Word. And when he says the soul comes into the body *from without* <note>original: "θύραθεν" (thyrathen)</note>, from the outside, from the Son of God, from the divine Word, from our Archetype, it is to be understood through the declaration of Philo himself—not as a Christian, but as a Jew and a most eminent Platonist. Likewise in the book, *That it is not fitting for women to act unbecomingly*: *For the soul of man is a seal; when it departs and moves away, all things that are left behind are defiled, being deprived of the divine image. Since every human mind is god-like, being formed according to the archetypal Idea, the highest Word.* What likewise could be more divine than these things, more excellent, or deeper in all Theology concerning the creation of our soul? And that it is necessary to retain the image, for when it departs, all things are fouled and polluted. Therefore, this entire Theology is found in Philo, nor would you find another in the sacred writers; so that it is a marvelous thing for such clear and least ambiguous testimonies to be brought forth by a Jewish man, a Philosopher—unless we saw that the Theologians of all nations had known and preached the same things, and that what was once celebrated among the wise of all nations has become famous in these most recent ages. The Word, therefore, according to Philo, is the Firstborn, is the Son, is the Instrument of God, is most fruitful, and the Idea of Ideas; it is the archetypal world, and likewise the exemplar and archetype of man. It is the image of God, the distributor, the governor; it is he through whom one comes to God; it is the Sun, the High Priest in this temple of God which is the world; it is the ruler and moderator of all things, the magistrate of God, the dispenser of human affairs and kingdoms; it is most provident and most sharp-sighted; it is the Mind above us, according to which ours was created, and man is like unto it. Likewise, that this "Logos" is God, and eternal according to Philo—not as many most insane people, ignorant of nature, have suspected: that it is not God, but a certain Mind like a greatest Angel, as the Valentinians <note>A Gnostic sect</note> claim—all these things declare...

<meta>This page continues the author's argument that ancient philosophers—specifically Philo, Plato, and Hermes Trismegistus—recognized the Divine Word (Logos) as the creator of the world, aligning with Christian theology.</meta>

# AUGUSTINUS STEUCHUS OF GUBBIO, BOOK I.

<margin>C</margin> What we have just brought forward is also found in the book *On the World*, when he speaks of the creation of man. For while in those cited passages he said the divine Word was the archetype and pattern of our soul, in the book *On the World* he writes that others have said man has an affinity with the ether. But the great Moses taught that the form of the rational soul is similar to no created thing: <note>original Greek: ἄλλα αὐτῶ ἔφη τὸν θεῖον λόγον... Steuchus provides a Greek excerpt from Philo Judaeus</note> "but he said it was the divine Word, and the invisible divine image—consider it a power—of which he [man] partook; and being formed by the seal of God, whose distinct character is the eternal Word. For God, he says, breathed into his face the breath of life; so it is necessary for the receiver to be modeled after the sender, which is why they say man was made according to the image of God, and certainly not according to the image of any created thing." But he said it was the image of the divine Word and of that invisible God, deeming it probable that even that which was formed was the seal of God, whose character is the eternal Word. For "God breathed," he says, "into his face the breath of life." Wherefore it is necessary that he who received it be assimilated to Him who sent it. Therefore they also say man was made in the image of God, not in the image of any created thing. Behold, Philo confesses the eternal Word itself, he confesses God, he confesses that we are in His image, and he confesses that Moses spoke of Him.

->*From Plato: That the Son has a Father; that the Word is most divine, the Prince of things present and future.*<-

### CHAPTER XXVII.

Plato also—whose entire Theology flowed from Trismegistus <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Egyptian sage</note>, and who, having spent thirteen years in Egypt, looked into all their wisdom and <margin>D</margin> enriched his own greatness with it—confessed in the *Epinomis*, in clear words wrapped in no ambiguity, that the most divine Word created the world. Mercury <note>Hermes</note>, therefore, and Philo called the *Logos*—that is, the Word—not only the Son of God, but also the Craftsman <term>Opifex: a creator or master-builder</term>. And the Word is the Craftsman because of the worldly workmanship, in which the workmanship of heaven is chief. Moreover, Plato says this in the *Epinomis*: "Each star completes its own course, <note>original Greek: συνάπτεται δὲ κόσμῳ...</note> and is joined to the world-order which the Word, the most divine of all, has ordained for the visible world." Completing and perfecting, he says, that order and beauty which the most divine Word made so that it might be seen. Plato had heard, therefore, from Mercury that the Mind, the Craftsman Word, had established the world. Wherefore in the *Phaedo* he cries out and affirms that Mind is the creator of all things; in the *Timaeus* he calls the creator the Craftsman of the world; in the *Epinomis* he says the most divine Word devised the celestial orders. From this it necessarily follows that Aristotle and Plato feel the same way: Aristotle calls the Mind "most divine" <note>Greek: θειότατον</note> in his books of divine Philosophy; Plato says the Word is "most divine." Surely there cannot be two "most divines." They are, therefore, all names of the Son of God, by which the same reality is signified among these two renowned philosophers and the whole theology of the ancients. Since, therefore, both the names and the realities are found in Plato which were previously held in the theology of Mercury, there is a clear reason that those things brought forth from him are most evidently his, and were not invented by Christians or anyone else. But hear another bright testimony concerning both the theology of Mercury and the truth itself, regarding the Father and the Son in Plato; and see how much of Christian Theology was known to the ancients. Then understand from this that what has been brought forth concerning the Magi, Sibyls, and Egyptians is, without controversy, not adulterated or fabricated. The Son is called the Prince of the whole orb, of heaven and earth, not only by Christians but also by all those whose testimonies we have used, as the Sibyl used to sing. "The Prince," says our [Scripture], "of the kings of the earth, the Lord of lords." He is also called the Craftsman and the Cause. For we know it is now celebrated and commemorated everywhere that the cause of all things is the
<meta>The text cuts off at "is the," with the catchword "Son" indicating the start of the next page.</meta>

<summary>Steuchus argues that Plato and Philo Judaeus derived their understanding of the "Divine Word" (Logos) as the creator of the universe from the ancient Egyptian teachings of Hermes Trismegistus, aligning ancient philosophy with Christian doctrine.</summary>

<meta>The previous page likely discussed the ancient origins of theological concepts in pagan philosophy, specifically focusing on the "Perennial Philosophy" that links classical thought to Christian revelation.</meta>

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<margin>A</margin>The Son of God. This term is also equivalent to the Craftsman <note>original: "Opifex"</note> and the Cause: for whoever speaks of a work of craftsmanship necessarily designates that which comes from a cause. Plato used these names most clearly to refer to the Son himself. Then, in the same passage where he had said this, he mentions the Father of that Cause and Prince, and he asserts that this mention is mystical, secret, and most sacred. In his letter to three friends, Hermias, Erastus, and Coriscus, having spoken of friendship and charity, he added these words at the close of the letter: "It is necessary for you three to read this letter together; if not, then certainly two together, and as frequently as possible, with a firm and holy covenant and law, which is justice, aiding with all mental effort that discipline which is the sister and companion of your studies: <note>original Greek: καὶ τὸν τῶν πάντων θεὸν ἡγεμόνα... εὐδαιμόνων</note> *swearing by the God who is the leader of all things, both present and future, and by the Lord and Father of this leader and cause; if we truly philosophize about Him, we shall all clearly know Him, as far as is granted to blessed men.*" That is to say: Adjuring also God, the Prince of all things—of things, I say, present and future—and then the Father of this Prince and Cause. If we have truly philosophized concerning Him, we shall recognize Him as lucidly and clearly as can be granted to blessed men. These are Plato's words concerning the fountains of Hermes <note>likely referring to Hermes Trismegistus</note>, as I believe. Reflecting on these things, I almost shed tears for joy. For I see that the Savior of the human race was always known, and never withdrew himself so far from human sight that, upon his coming, his Divinity and that most august thing called the Trinity should seem an entirely new matter. Indeed, it seems wonderful to the ignorant that there is a Father and a Son in God, and this especially displeases the blind Hebrews, whose minds are always full of the most foolish thoughts, nor does their lack of skill ever allow them to see the truth. You have here in Plato—the high priest of secular philosophy, a man born many years before Christ—a naked and open mention of the Father and the Son.

<margin>B</margin>He says the Son is the Prince of all things present and future. But what are future things, if not the world to come? Indeed, Paul also calls him the King of ages, and the Prince of the present and future age. Plato calls him the Prince of both, and shortly after names his Father. Then Plato calls the same one the Cause—namely, that which created all things, the prince and mistress of all causes. Therefore, he is called the "Cause" by way of excellence, just as the Father is called "the Good" itself by way of excellence. Plato implies that Cause which, in the *Epinomis*, he calls the most divine Word <note>original: "Verbum"</note>, the discoverer of celestial order; in the *Phaedo*, the Mind <note>original: "Mentem"</note>; and in the *Timaeus*, the Craftsman. This, I say, is called the Cause by him in his letter. Diogenes Laertius also testifies to this, asserting that Plato established two principles of the created world: God and Matter, <note>original Greek: ὅν καὶ νοῦν προσαγορεύει, καὶ αἴτιον</note> *whom he calls both Mind and Cause.* All these, therefore, are names of the Son of God: Cause, Mind, Craftsman, Word. O most wonderful thing! See also the generation of that Cause. For he names his Father: you see the creation of the world through the Son, while he names the Cause, he names the King or Prince of things present and future, just as the Sibyl calls him the King or <term>Leader <gloss>original Greek: hegētera</gloss></term>, the Prince of the Gods. Moreover, the "Gods" are future things in respect to us. For Paul says, "Every name that is named in this age and in the future." The Sibyl said the Son was appointed Prince of these by the Father. Similarly also Plato. The names too are similar: the Sibyl uses *hegētera*, Plato uses *hegemona*. Then Plato calls this very Cause "God." Therefore, Plato spoke all these things most evidently in his Letter: that there is a Son and a Father; and that the Son is the Cause, the King of things visible and invisible, and is God. What remains in Theology? Finally, what is in every way to be wondered at, looked up to, and kissed—and in which I myself cannot moderate my tears and joys, nor be satisfied in wondering—it is said by Plato that one must truly philosophize concerning the Father. For it will be that this knowledge makes us

<summary>The author argues that Plato and other ancient figures like the Sibyl recognized the Christian Trinity, specifically the relationship between the Father and the Son (the "Cause" or "Word"), long before the birth of Christ. He views these philosophical texts as evidence of a "perennial" divine truth accessible to the wise of all ages.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's argument that ancient Greek philosophers, specifically Plato and Orpheus, possessed a hidden knowledge of Christian mysteries, such as the nature of God the Father and the Son.</meta>

# AUGUSTINE EUGUBINUS, BOOK I.

...blessed. Certainly, divine Plato, you were hinting at something great; and therefore, you spoke cautiously and in a hidden manner, after the custom 
<margin>C</margin>
of the Prophets. For what does it mean to "philosophize truly concerning the Father"? Why specifically about the Father? Surely for this reason: because, as we were saying above and as we shall show in what follows, according to the opinion of Philo, Numenius, and Plotinus, the Father was not known to men, but only the Son—that is, the creator of the world. All men knew and proclaimed that the world was created by God. Therefore, they knew only the Creator; they did not know the Father. Thus, he rightly warns that it is necessary to philosophize truly concerning the Father. What does "truly" mean? In Greek, it is <term>ontōs</term> <note>original: ὄντως; meaning "really" or "essentially"</note>. That is, to philosophize, think, and speak worthily of Him regarding those things which are His; and to do what His Son did, who revealed this Philosophy concerning His Father, ascribing all things to Him—His divinity, essence, and power—saying that He was sent by Him, appointed by Him as judge of the living and the dead, and that all things were delivered to Him by Him. He called Him His Father and His God, affirming that the Father was greater than He, and that the Father could never be recognized, nor His most joyful sight reached, unless the Son revealed Him and led the way. This, I say, is the Philosophy concerning the Father revealed by the Son, making men blessed, indeed most blessed. It was revealed by the Son, I say, not only in these recent ages, but also, as it appears, from the very origin of the world. The Son revealed it to make us blessed through this knowledge and to lead us to the paternal delights and the paternal kingdom. 

Since Plato proclaims that blessedness is found here, who will be so slow or stone-like as not to be moved to admiration that this "modern" wisdom, which is now most common everywhere, was known and preached in prior ages, albeit to a few? Moreover, that Plato spoke of the Son of God in this letter, and that I am not the first to whom this truth has shone, the most learned Greek Theologians—who were also the greatest Philosophers—are witnesses: Clement of Alexandria, Origen, Eusebius, and Cyril. They confess that in this Letter, Plato wrote 
<margin>D</margin>
concerning the Son of God, and they did so while disputing against profane philosophers, refuting them with the writings of their own men. Among these, Origen writes against Celsus in the sixth book: <note>The following is a translation of the Greek and Latin text provided in the source</note> "But Celsus, who professes to know all things and brings forward many testimonies from Plato, voluntarily—as I myself judge—keeps silent regarding what Plato says about the Son of God in the Letter to Hermias and Coriscus. For thus Plato writes there." These are the words of Origen. Eusebius, Cyril, and Clement in the *Stromata* say the same. But Origen alone suffices. For we shall see the Platonists themselves in the course of this work confessing similar things, using this Letter as testimony for the two <term>hypostases</term> <note>original: hypostaseon; referring to the distinct persons or realities of the Father and the Son</note>. The elder Hugo, a very good Theologian, also saw and brought forth this same point.

---

### *From the Theology of Orpheus, concerning the divine Word, and that it is the divine voice, and the shaper of the world.*

#### CHAPTER XXVIII.

We have called Orpheus the prince of Greek Theology. For almost no one before him is mentioned among the Greeks. Indeed, he flourished before the Poets whom they call Theologians, and before the Philosophers. It is well established that he traveled for a long time in Egypt and learned Theology from the Egyptian Priests. John the Grammarian, in his *Life of Homer*, calls him "most wise" <note>original: σοφώτατον</note>. Justin Martyr and Cyril mention him excellently. In his Theology, many traces of ancient truth are seen, which, although they were later defiled by certain fables of the superstitious, will regain their beauty once set aside, separated, and purged. For since...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Perennial Philosophy," specifically tracing the lineage of divine truth through ancient pagan figures who predate or parallel Christian revelation.</meta>

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<margin>A</margin> Although he had previously been devoted to false superstitions, the worship of demons, and every kind of idolatry, and had sung hymns and odes to fictitious gods (if indeed those hymns are his, and not rather the work of another Orpheus much later in time), he was afterwards aided by the knowledge of true things. He sang a <term>palinode</term> <note>A poem in which the author retracts something said in a former poem.</note> of his former life, and with his gaze fixed upon the one true God, he celebrated with wondrous praise His majesty and divinity, which he had learned among the Egyptians. Therefore, since all confess him to be of great antiquity, and some assert that he was a disciple of Moses—that is, of Musaeus—among whom is Polyhistor (which I do not affirm myself), he wrote many true things from that Egyptian wisdom and other theology scattered throughout the world, which are to be admired in these ages. Following the famous report of the Most High Word of God, he calls it the "Voice," through which he asserts the Father created all things. Being about to write these most high mysteries, he began his song thus:

->I shall speak to those for whom it is lawful; let the doors be closed to the profane.<-
<note>original Greek: φθέγξομαι οἷς θέμις ἐστὶ, θύρας δ' ἐπίθεσθε βέβηλοι. Latin: Dicam queis fas est, claudatur porta profanis.</note>

Therefore, only certain songs will be set forth for the sake of faith, so that you may recognize the consensus of all ages. For the same things appear in his work concerning the Son as are found in Plato, Hermes Trismegistus, the Chaldeans, and all the others. Regarding the Son:

->And looking toward the divine Word, attend to it,<-
->directing the intellectual vessel of the heart; and walk well<-
->the path, and look only upon the shaper of the world,<-
->the immortal one; for an ancient word shines forth concerning him,<-
->that he is one, self-perfected, and by him all things are perfected,<-
->and he himself moves among them, nor does any<-
->of mortal souls see him, but he is seen by the mind alone.<-

<margin>B</margin> And looking toward the Word of God, be ever present to it,
Fixing the high lights of the soul; proceed on the straight
Path, and look upon the one creator of the world, O son,
The immortal one, whom the fame of the earlier fathers brings
As one, and perfect in himself; then that all other things
Are perfected by him, and that he dwells among them, yet is not
To be discerned by any souls of men, but seen by the mind alone.

By these verses, not only is the dignity of our Theology of these most recent ages declared—which has shone upon the whole world—but the sayings of Hermes Trismegistus, the Chaldeans, the Sibyls, Anaxagoras, and Plato are confirmed. With these Orpheus, and all of them among themselves, consenting by a certain divine instinct, decree the highest divinity to the Son of God, preaching the creation of things and that he is to be adored and looked up to. The Chaldean said the Father of all things gave authority and creation to the Son. He is called "Mind" by Anaxagoras and the Platonists. This is confirmed by Plato and Aristotle. He is called by Hermes not only "Mind," but also the "Word," begotten of the Father. Behold, therefore, all these things are also found in Orpheus. For he names him the "Divine Word": "Word" declares the authority and the reason for creating; "Divine" shows that he is the Word of another, namely of Him who is properly and principally God. Whom the Son himself also called his God. Yet the divinity of the Son is not taken away by any of them, but is preached and promoted; they exhort all that we should acknowledge and venerate him. Then Orpheus calls him the <term>shaper of the world</term> <note>original Greek: κόσμοιο τυπωτήν (kosmoio typōtēn)</note>. O, wonderful consensus of all! For this is the sense of the voice of those earlier men who called the Son "Mind": they said it, namely, on account of the forms of the world, on account of its grace and beauty, and on account of the discovered orders. Reason alone can give ornament; Mind alone, intelligence alone—and that the greatest, wisest, and Omnipotent Mind—can give the highest and universal orders. Therefore, he was called "Intelligence" by them because of creation, because of Chaos, from which the Omnipotent Mind, as if from a source and a rough beginning, expressed all orders most wisely...

It highlights the "wonderful consensus" between Orpheus, Plato, Hermes Trismegistus, and other ancient philosophers regarding the divine creation of the world.</summary>

# AUGUSTINUS EUGUBINUS, BOOK I.

This occurs. Orpheus, expressing this same sense with another more familiar sound, calls him the *Formator* <note>original: "τυπωτῶ" (typotō) - the Molder or Shaper</note>, the Shaper. Therefore, the Mind and the Shaper are the same. For to whom does it belong to give form, if not to Intelligence and Mind? Our Poet <note>likely referring to Ovid or a similar classical authority</note>, hearing and looking back upon and celebrating all these things, having commemorated Chaos, called this very one whom Orpheus called the Shaper—with exactly the same knowledge—the Origin of a better world. That Shaper, I say, was the Origin of a better world. The "better world" is the form of heaven and earth, and of all things celestial and terrestrial. And in Plato, it is the most divine Word; in Orpheus, the divine Word; in Hermes <note>original: "Mercurium" - Hermes Trismegistus</note>, the first Mind generates a Mind, the Word. In Anaxagoras, it is the Mind arranging Chaos and creating; Plato confirms and rejoices in this. In the same author, it is the Cause, and excellently the Cause. In Orpheus, the Shaper of the world. Therefore, there is no disagreement. Later, Orpheus thundered this song even more expressly:

> I adjure you, O Heaven, the wise work of the great God;
> I adjure you, O Voice of the Father, which he first uttered.
> <note>Translated from the provided Greek: "ουρανὸν ὁρκίζω σε θεοῦ μεγάλου σοφὸν ἔργον, Αυδὴν ὁρκίζω σε πατρὸς, τὴν ἐφθέγξατο πρώτην."</note>

> I call heaven to witness, the wise work of the Almighty,
> I adjure you by the Voice of the Father, which he himself brought forth
> First, when the world stood firm by his counsels.
> <note>The author's Latin verse translation of the Greek above.</note>

"Word" and "Voice" are names of the Son, and the reasoning for both is the same. He was commanded, and spoken, and sent to create. Here also lies open the theology of the most recent ages, which confesses the Father as the creator of all, but through the Son. Beneath these words lies that sense which we have declared many times: that the Father is as one commanding, as one dictating and sending the Son to the creation, to whom he gave not only authority, but also his own nature and divinity—that is, he begat him. Moreover, as we know and preach that the Father did not come to redeem the human race, nor perform human deeds, nor fulfill the duties of mortals, but the Son did; so likewise the same Son was sent to create the one whom he would redeem, and for whose sake he would create heaven and earth. Just as he himself said, and we know well, this redemption was commanded by the Father and performed by the Son. 

<margin>D</margin>
However, things are said to belong principally to those who first move them, from whom the counsel for carrying out affairs is born; those who actually do them are secondary causes. Thus the creation of the world belongs to the Father as the principal cause. Likewise, redemption is said to be done by him, by sending his only-begotten Son for this very purpose. It is therefore a marvelous thing that this was also known to those ancients—namely, a most obscure matter which mortals cannot recognize by themselves. For he truly said: *No one knows the Father except the Son, and he to whom the Son wishes to reveal him.* Indeed, we are able to know and think that there was some creator of the world; but that this creator has a Father, and that the divinity is Father and Son, we not only do not know, but almost deny, not grasping or believing it could ever be possible that with God there is one who begets and one who is begotten. The Hebrews, mocking this most of all, were the ones who especially did not know the Father. For they know and celebrate the creator of the world, and worship him in Synagogues; but he is the Son of God, whom they later—when he was hiding the greatness of his divinity in a body—affixed to a cross, having been made like us then, so that he might make us like himself afterward. Therefore, unless the Son had revealed it, we too would be wandering in a similar error. And certainly, it must be believed that he himself manifested his Father to the ancient ages, from whence the Chaldean, the Sibyl, Hermes, Plato, and Orpheus drew this knowledge, which he later revealed most abundantly, showing that this is true happiness: that men should understand the Father with the Son. The ancestors knew the Father; for all remembered the Father, I believe, as much from ancient tradition as from heavenly revelation. For what Plato wrote concerning the Father, it must be believed he received from his elders, or from Hermes or Orpheus; and it became known to Hermes, the Sibyls, and the Chaldeans, both from a heavenly oracle and from most ancient successions. Indeed, that same Orpheus remembers an ancient report, which carries that this creator of the world is most perfect, needing nothing, surrounding all things, ruling and sustaining them, who is seen by the mind alone and by the purest thought. Therefore, Orpheus reported these things descending from ancient fame and affirmed them. And we see here not only the theology itself, but also the clear testimony of Orpheus concerning it descending from the ancestors. Fame, therefore...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Perennial Philosophy," specifically focusing on the shared theological insights between ancient Greek poets like Orpheus, the Hebrew prophet Moses, and early Christian thinkers regarding the nature of the Son of God as the Creator.</meta>

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.
<note>The "Perennial Philosophy" refers to the idea that a single, universal truth runs through all the world's great religious and philosophical traditions.</note>

->i 2<-

<margin>A</margin>
Therefore, there was an ancient tradition concerning Him who, being Himself most perfect, caused all things to be made perfect by Him. For that was the reason why He Himself finished the rest and created them most perfectly: because He is most perfect Himself, and most highly equipped with all power and strength. There are three names for the Son in the works of Orpheus <note>Orpheus was a legendary Greek poet and musician; here, the author treats his poems as containing hidden divine revelations.</note> which contain his entire Theology: *the Word of the gods*, *the Molder*, and *the Self-Perfected* <note>original: "θεῶν λόγος, τυποτὴς, αἰσσηλὸς" (the third term is likely a variant of "self-complete").</note>, or the Divine Word, the Shaper of the world, perfected through Himself or by Himself. By these names, both His supreme divinity and the creation of the world are most clearly designated. Furthermore, Orpheus says three things about Him pertaining to deep Philosophy. First, that He moves through the things established by Him, namely ruling and governing them, as Philo <note>Philo of Alexandria, a Jewish philosopher who blended Greek philosophy with Hebrew scripture.</note> used to declare; then, that His turning through and presence in all things is invisible, such that it cannot be seen by our eyes; and lastly, that He is understood by the mind and reason alone. For now, even if bodily eyes do not perceive Him, the mind ought to reach that point. Indeed, we perceive more things with our souls than with our bodily eyes. To see through the soul is called partly reason and partly faith. These things Orpheus said, along with many others, concerning the divinity of the Son.

It should also be known that the name *Self-Perfected* <note>original: "αὐτοτελῆ"</note> is the same as that found in Moses, *Self-Existent* <note>original: "αὐτοὼν"</note>, existing through Himself, existing first, and existing from no one else. For that is what He indicated to Moses: *I am who I am*, which in Greek is *The One Who Is* <note>original: "ὁ ὤν"</note>, He who exists preeminently. The Greek article <note>The word "the" (ho) in Greek.</note> indicates excellence, and the word *self* <note>original: "αὐτὸς"</note> has the same power as the article. The pronoun indicates the beginning or origin; the article indicates excellence. Truly, He is the same who exists through Himself and who possesses this very existence most absolutely, especially, and primarily. Furthermore, *Self-Perfected* and *Self-Existent* are the same. For perfection and being itself are the same thing. Therefore, He who exists from Himself is perfect from Himself. On this account, Orpheus says the same thing as Moses. He also follows him most evidently. Orpheus calls the Son of God by this name *Self-Perfected*, as is clear. Since, however, *Self-Perfected* and *Self-Existent* are the same, 
<margin>B</margin>
it follows that He whom Moses called *Self-Existent* (*The One Who Is*) is the same as He whom Orpheus called *Self-Perfected*. But that is the Son of God in Orpheus; therefore, He is the same in Moses when he says, *I am who I am*. Dionysius the Areopagite <note>A Christian theologian and philosopher of the late 5th century.</note> also calls the Son of God *The Being* <note>original: "τὸ ὄν"</note>. It should also be known that the four-letter name among the Hebrews, *Jehovah*, sounds like this very one whom Orpheus called in Greek *Self-Perfected*. For that name designates Him who is; and that is the whole of the ancient Philosophy, as will be seen; and it is exactly what the Greek philosophers themselves called *The Being* (that which is). Since there is a "substantial verb" among the Hebrews, *hayah* <note>The Hebrew root for "to be."</note>, from which the four-letter name is formed, it signifies "the existing one." Orpheus most beautifully signified in Greek what sense that name holds. Therefore, the Son of God is celebrated among all people. Justin Martyr, who was both a philosopher and a witness, testifies in his *Apology* that Orpheus knew the Word of God as the creator of heaven and earth, just as, he says, the prophecy of the saints taught us: *And having arrived in Egypt, he knew that through the Word of God, all nature was brought into existence* <note>original Greek: "καὶ ἐκ μὲν γὰρ αὐτὸς ἐν τῆ αἰγύπτω προσχθὼν, ἔγνω, ὅτι διὰ τὸν λόγον τοῦ θεοῦ, πᾶσα ἐγίνετο φύσις"</note>. Having handed down the same things in part while in Egypt, he recognized that through the Word of God, the entire creation came forth.

## *Certain other matters from Philo the Jew, concerning the Father and the first-born Son, who is His Word, the creator of the world, the archetype of man: and that the Father is most eminent, the Son is the law and guidance of the world, the prince of Angels, co-eternal with the Father.*

### CHAPTER XXIX.

THE things we have seen the ancestors speak of until now so reinforce one another, and provide such a torch and light, that whoever would wish to move any of them from their place would necessarily have to tear apart the entire sequence. For the writings of Plato and Aristotle are certain, confirming both their own sayings and those of their ancestors. But I believe there will be no one to whom these things seem less faithful or credible, since they offer themselves in the clearest light and the indissoluble harmony of all writers. I shall therefore relate certain other things from the same...

<meta>This page continues the author's argument regarding the divine "Word" (Logos) as the intermediary of creation, drawing heavily on the Jewish philosopher Philo of Alexandria to bridge the gap between Hebrew scripture and Platonic philosophy.</meta>

-> AUGUSTINUS STEUCHUS OF GUBBIO, BOOK I. <-

<margin>C</margin> ...with that distinguished philosopher Philo the Jew, so that the order of the narrative may be entirely consistent within itself. For these matters have always been a source of wonder to great men. He, therefore, in the work titled *Questions and Solutions*, inquires in this manner regarding that passage of Scripture in Genesis chapter 2: *He made him in the image of God*: <note>Steuchus provides the Greek text of Philo followed by his own Latin translation, both of which are rendered here into English.</note> "Why does he say, 'In the image of God he made man,' as if speaking of another God, and not rather in his own image? This was uttered as an oracle most beautifully and wisely. For nothing mortal could be formed after the likeness of the Highest One and Father of all things, but rather after the second God, who is His Word <note>Greek: *Logos*</note>. For it was necessary that the rational form in the human soul be imprinted by the Divine Word, since the God who is prior to the Word is superior to every rational nature. It was not permitted for anything created to be made like unto Him who is established above all things in the most excellent and unique Idea." These are the words of Philo. Nothing could be clearer or more fitting regarding the divinity of the Father and the Son; nothing more profoundly theological: so much so that one could hardly expect better from Christians.

Listen, O iron-hearted Hebrew people, and finally lift up your eyes heavy with darkness; listen, I say, to the most learned and eloquent man of your own race, gathering and demonstrating from Genesis—from your own literature—the Father and the Son. The latter he calls the Word <note>Latin: *Verbum*</note>, in accordance with Christian Theology and superior to all those we have previously cited. He asserts that man was created in His image, just as the Chaldean <note>likely referring to Zoroaster or the Oracles</note> and Hermes <note>Mercurius Trismegistus</note> had said; and that the Word itself is the image of the Highest Father. What else does Paul say? <margin>D</margin> He who says that He is the image, the radiance, and the character of the substance of God. Let the Hebrews, I say, hear this greatest of men—second to none among them, not even to those Rabbis whom they adore—witnessing that man was created by the Son and Word of God, so that they may understand the truest reason why man was redeemed by the same.

For Philo would teach them that it would have been a violation of divine order for the Father to come into the world, just as he proves it a violation for man to be created directly toward the Father. Instead, the Son performed this duty as the lesser <note>Steuchus uses "minor" here in a functional/hierarchical sense common in Renaissance Neoplatonism</note>, whom the Father uses as His minister and messenger, as the same Philo claims elsewhere. O immortal God, who revealed these things to this man? Who admitted him into these inner sanctuaries of Theology? By what ship did he penetrate into this sea? Was it because he was a Platonist, and had seen those most abundant riches in Hermes and the other most ancient Theologians, from which the doctrines of Plato had been plucked? Furthermore, he flourished at the time when the Gospel was already shining throughout the whole world.

There are in these words the highest and deepest mysteries. A human mouth cannot speak a higher Theology. Consider how intimately he looked into the cause of why all things were created through the Son and the Divine Word. I am amazed by this: by what guide did he perceive that the Son was sent for the creation and administration of things? He even agrees with the Gospel, where the same Son says of Himself to the Father: *As you have given him power over all flesh.* All have understood that the Father is the first cause, the parent of the second Wisdom, the highest cause most remote from created things. The Son stands between the Father and created things; it would be improper for the Father to act by entering into things directly or involving Himself, having a mediator through whom He works. For what was said by Philo regarding creation is also true of the whole world: just as man ought not to have been created [directly] toward the Father, and is the creation of the Son, so the creation of the whole world belongs to the same. Wherefore, most deeply and from the most profound Theology, the same Philo, in his books *On Agriculture*, spoke these divine words (which should be inscribed in letters of gold, as seen above): "God, as Shepherd and King, leads all these things according to justice and law, having set over them His right Word and first-born Son, who, like the lieutenant of a great king, shall receive the care of this sacred flock."

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<margin>A</margin> All things that exist are led and governed most fittingly by God, the Shepherd and King, who has appointed His Word <note>original: *Verbum*</note> and first-born Son as the presiding officer, acting as a righteous law. This Son sustains the administration of this sacred order as the magistrate of the Supreme King. These are likewise the greatest mysteries of the Christian religion. One can almost hear Paul himself speaking here. Indeed, the power of truth is great, and the opinions of great men are always in harmony with one another. You have heard of the first-born Son of God; you have heard of the Word; you have heard of the ruler and law of the world established by the Father. To Him, the province of governing all things has been entrusted, as He is, so to speak, the magistrate of God, the messenger, and the executor of the paternal will. You have also heard that the world is ruled by the Father, who employs His First-born at this helm. Therefore, the world is ruled by the Father through His Son, just as a kingdom is ruled by a king through his magistrates. Moreover, the Father, who loves His Son uniquely, singularly, and indeed omnipotently—just as He is omnipotent—does not envy the Son His honors. Instead, He has given Him every kingdom, visible and invisible, and set Him over all things. I speak of the Son as His delight, in whose loving, and in the Son’s reciprocal love for the Father, they enjoy eternal pleasure and incomprehensible joys. Let the most desperate Hebrews <note>The author is addressing contemporary Jewish scholars who rejected the divinity of Christ, arguing that their own ancient traditions support it.</note> listen and recognize from their own literature, and from their most weighty author, that He who proclaimed Himself the only Son of God, the Word, the One Sent, and the Messenger, is now adored and worshiped throughout the whole world. He always ascribed all things to the Father as His parent and superior, His lord, His source, His origin, and the cause of His divinity. For although the Son possesses everything the Father has, the Father holds them as granted by no one and received from no one; the Son holds the same, but from the Father. The Father is that fountain, giver, light, and first cause; the Son is the radiance from that light, eternal light from eternal light. These things are said by **Philo**, a Jew by race but a Platonic by profession—that is, similar to **Plato and the Platonists**. He was born under **Tiberius** and practiced philosophy under **Claudius**, during the time of the **Apostles**,
<margin>B</margin> before **Plotinus, Amelius, Porphyry, Proclus, and Damascius** <note>These are famous Neoplatonist philosophers who lived centuries after Philo.</note>, and was almost a contemporary of **Numenius**, among whom similar ideas are found. If Philo were to see the blindness and persistence of his own people now, I believe he would—like many of the most esteemed men of that nation—lament and rebuke it. Among the earlier theologians, by whom all Theology is comprised, these names for the Son exist: Word, Mind, Craftsman, and Cause. To these, **Philo** added: First-born, Son, Light of the World, and Governor. In Greek, these are: *logos* <note>Word/Reason</note>, *nous* <note>Mind</note>, *demiourgos* <note>Craftsman/Creator</note>, *aition* <note>Cause</note>, *protogonos* <note>First-born</note>, *huios* <note>Son</note>, and *hyparchos* <note>Lieutenant/Governor</note>. Since these terms are found among all philosophers, both ancient and new—**Plato, Aristotle, Anaxagoras, the Chaldeans, the Sibyls, Hermes, Orpheus**, and the entire Platonic school—it is apparent that the whole Theology concerning the Son, which is now worshiped and celebrated, was known, confessed, adored, and preached before He Himself became man. **Philo** also asserts that there is nothing that sustains the world to prevent it from dissolving except the eternal Word of God. This is the firmest and most stable bond of all things, which, reaching from the center to the extremities and from the extremities back to the center, rules the eternal and indefatigable course of nature. For he says the Parent established the indissoluble bond of the Universe. Rightly, therefore, he says that the earth will never be washed away by all the water contained in its bosom, nor will fire be extinguished by the air, nor the air be inflamed by fire, for the divine Word separates all things and rules over the elements like a soul. Thus, from diverse parts, the universe creates a sweet harmony. These things are in his second book *On Agriculture*. The same is also in his book *On the World*. In that passage, it should be noted that in the printed volumes, "law" <note>Greek: *nomos*</note> is wrongly read instead of "Word" <note>Greek: *logos*</note>. For that book is faulty in many places. Furthermore, he repeats the same sentiment in the book *On Agriculture*, where the word is *logos*. There is rich evidence that the passage is corrupted because he writes in the same place: "For the Father who begat it made the Word the indissoluble bond of the universe" <note>original Greek: *desmon gar arrēkton tou pantos ho gennēsas epoiei patēr*</note>. This does not fit the description of a "law," but rather the "Word," which is begotten by the Father. Again, in the work...

# AUGUSTINUS EUGUBINUS, BOOK I.

<margin>C</margin>
In the book he entitles, *That Evil is Wont to Lie in Wait for Good*, he says these things: It is fitting, he says, for those who have entered into a covenant with knowledge to be affected by a desire to see <note>original: "τὸ ὂν"</note> **That Which Is**. But if that is not granted, then at least they should desire His most sacred image, the **Word** <note>original: "Verbum," translating the Greek "Logos"</note>. Then, if anyone has not yet been deemed worthy to be called a Son of God, let him strive at least to be made like His first-born Word, who is before all Angels, and holds the sovereignty over the Angels, and is called by many names. For indeed he is called the Beginning, and **That Which Is** (namely the four-letter Name <note>The Tetragrammaton, YHWH, which Philo associates with the Word</note>, as I believe Philo intends) and the Word, and the Man according to the image, and the Seer, and Israel. And I proceeded a little earlier to praise the virtues of those who say that we are all sons of one man. For even if we have not yet reached the point of being sons of God, let us at least be sons of His eternal Image. For the Image of God is the eternal Word. Moreover, I heard one of the friends of Moses uttering such an oracle: *Behold the man, whose name is the East* <note>A reference to Zechariah 6:12. The Latin "Oriens" can mean the East, the Rising Sun, or the Daybreak</note>. A new naming indeed, if you think it is said of one who consists of soul and body. But if you speak of that incorporeal one, bearing the divine Idea, then you will confess the truth, that the most upright and significant name bestowed upon him is the **East**, or the **Rising** <note>original: "ἀνατολὴ"</note>. For the eternal Father of all begat him, whom elsewhere he called the First-born. But he who was begotten, imitating the paternal ways and looking toward His original patterns, formed the species. But if anyone wishes to use names more plainly and readily, let him call the **intelligible world** <note>The world of pure thought/ideas before physical creation</note> nothing other than the Word of God, already creating the world. For if man, who is a part of the world, was made according to His image, how much more so the whole world. Therefore, God is the Word, the Idea, the Archetypal of Ideas—that is, the original. And when Moses said, *In the beginning God created*, he did not, as most people think, look to a beginning of time; for there was no time before the world, but it was either with it or after it. But if Moses did not look to the beginning of time there, it is necessary to say it was a beginning according to number: so that *In the beginning he created* is just as

<margin>D</margin>
if he had said, *first* he created the heaven. First therefore, says Philo, the creator himself created the incorporeal heaven, and the invisible earth, and the idea of air, and the void. Of which he called one part Darkness, since air is great by nature; but this other, that is the idea of air, he called the Abyss: for the void is deep and almost infinite. Then there is the substance of incorporeal water, and the spirit. Then, seventh above all, the light. Which was itself incorporeal and the intellectual pattern of the Sun, and was destined to make all the luminous stars in heaven. Moreover, the spirit and the light obtained a special privilege. For he said the spirit was of God, because the spirit is most full of life, and God is the author of life. Furthermore, light was chosen because of its excellent beauty, being as much clearer and more radiant in its intellectual self than the visible light, as the sun is than darkness, day than night, or the judgment of the mind (which is the sovereignty of the whole soul) than the bodily eyes. That invisible and intelligible light was therefore the image of the divine Word, interpreting its generation. And it is the super-celestial star, the fountain of the visible stars; which you might not without reason call <note>original: "παναυγίαν"</note> **all-shining**, that is, the "all-light" or "luminosity," from which the Sun, and Moon, and the other wanderers <note>The planets</note>, and the fixed stars are kindled, insofar as he was able to bestow from that most pure and sincere light upon each one, when it began to turn itself according to the intelligible light toward the change of the visible—that is, to become visible light from the invisible. For nothing of those things which are in the senses is pure. Therefore the incorporeal world now had its end, established and firmed in the divine Word. These are the words of Philo, which we have most faithfully translated from the Greek. The same things are also brought forward by Eusebius, but they ought not to have been passed over by us, nor did we think we should blush to recall into one body things that were scattered in many places. In these things, therefore, he preaches most wonderfully; he recognizes the divine Word and knows what power it has, such that you would find scarcely better things among Christian Theologians. All are heralds of the divine Word, both the old and the new: but here in Philo, they are stupendous: and for that reason...

It explores the metaphysical origins of light, the "intelligible world," and the non-temporal meaning of the "beginning" in Genesis.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Perennial Philosophy," specifically tracing the concept of the Divine Word (Logos) through ancient thinkers to show how pre-Christian philosophy aligns with Christian theology.</meta>

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<margin>A</margin> We wonder all the more because we cannot easily grasp where these ideas originate: whether from the ancient Theology, or because he flourished alongside the new [Christianity], he of whom they write spoke with the blessed Peter in Rome <note>The author refers to Philo of Alexandria, whom some early traditions claimed met St. Peter.</note>. He recognizes, therefore, the divine Word; he calls him the first-born, the creator, and the rival or imitator of the Father’s Wisdom. Indeed, the Son says the same: *My teaching is not my own, but his who sent me.* He calls him the "Rising Sun," and he gathers or illuminates this entire Theology from Moses and the Prophets. He shows that all things were created through the Son, who is the image of the Father, the model and the <term>Idea</term> <note>In Platonic thought, the perfect blueprint or form of a thing.</note> of created things, such that the Father is the original model and Idea of the Son, while the Son is the Idea and <term>Logos</term> <note>original: "λόγος" — meaning Word, Reason, or Plan.</note>, that is, the governing principle of created things. Let the great Word mediate between the Father and created things. He says the same is the source of all things and of all light, imitating the paternal ways. For the same is said in the Gospel: *The Son does nothing except what he sees the Father doing.* Nothing more sublime can be recorded; thought cannot penetrate further. Whatever could have been said among mortals concerning the divinity of the Father and the Son was said by Philo. There remains only the giving of thanks, that we may praise Jesus Christ, our God, the eternal Word of God, Wisdom, and Creator, who revealed his truth—unknown to human eyes—and made it so manifest that it could be known and adored. Recognizing him, we simultaneously recognize the Father as the source of his divinity, and we adore the Father together with his Wisdom.

->*Testimony concerning the Father and the Son from Numenius the Pythagorean: and that the Father is first, the Son second: and that the Son performs all things while the Father commands: and that the Father is Goodness itself.*<-

### CHAPTER XXX.

<margin>B</margin> NUMENIUS, as I have said, was distinguished among the Pythagorean philosophers. According to Porphyry, Plotinus used to study his books diligently. It was Numenius who believed the philosophy of Plato was derived from Moses, famously saying that Plato was nothing other than "Moses speaking in the Attic [Greek] tongue." This man—whether because he held the views of the ancients (chiefly of all those we have brought forward), or because he had seen something of this sort in Moses, or Plato, or certainly Philo, or perhaps because he flourished after the birth of Christ—said much concerning the Father and the Son, calling them two Causes and two Gods: the First and the Second. Like Philo and Plotinus, he asserted that God the Father is the cause of the Son, and the Son is the cause of the world, following the same order as all those mentioned above. A few Greek words shall be brought into the midst; the rest shall be most faithfully rendered in English: <note>original Greek: "ὁ θεὸς ὁ πρῶτος... πατέρα τὸν πρῶτον θεὸν."</note> *The First God,* he says, *existing in himself, is simple, because he is always with himself and is never divided. The Second and Third are one. For it is not fitting for the First to perform labor; and regarding the craftsman Father and the Son, one must consider the First God to be the Father.* Numenius indicates what he thinks in many other places. For he adds: *If, therefore, we inquire concerning the Craftsman, saying that he must exist prior in order to act thus, it is well said. This is the approach of reason. But if the discussion is not about the Craftsman, but the inquiry is about the First, I consecrate those things which have been said and establish them as holy. And let them be as secrets, not to be spread abroad. I pass on, therefore, to grasp the reasoning, hunting it from elsewhere. Before we enter the discourse, however, we must confess a matter that is not ambiguous: namely, that the First God and King is free from all labor. But the Craftsman God commands as he travels through the heavens. These are the lives: one of the first God, the other of the second God. For the first God shall be thus, but the second is moved conversely. The First, I say, is concerned with intelligible things <note>Things understood by the mind/spirit rather than the senses.</note>; the Second is concerned with both intelligible and sensible things.*

<summary>This page explores the parallels between the Christian Trinity and ancient philosophy, specifically focusing on Philo of Alexandria and the Pythagorean Numenius. It argues that these thinkers recognized a "First God" (the Father) and a "Second God" or "Word" (the Son/Craftsman) who creates and governs the world.</summary>
<keywords>Numenius, Philo of Alexandria, Logos, Trinity, Pythagoreanism, Plato, Moses, Creation, Divine Wisdom, Neoplatonism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of ancient philosophical witnesses to Christian doctrine, specifically focusing on the Neopythagorean philosopher Numenius and his alignment with the Gospel of John regarding the Father and the Son.</meta>

# AUGUSTINUS STEUCHUS OF GUBBIO, BOOK I.

<margin>C</margin> Do not be surprised by what I have said. For you will hear much more wonderful things. For instead of the motion that exists in the Second <note>The "Second Mind" or the Son/Logos</note>, I say that rest exists in the First; a motion that is kindred and close, from which the order of the world, eternal perseverance, and salvation are poured back into all things. In his sixth book, Numenius writes as follows: "Since Plato knew that among men, only the Maker <note>original: *Opificem*; the Demiurge or Creator</note> was known, while the First Mind, who is called the Father, was entirely unknown, he spoke in such a way as if one were to say: 'O men, the one whom you think is the Mind is not the first; rather, there is another prior to this one, more ancient and more divine.'" And again: "A pilot traveling in the middle of the sea, standing above the rudders high in the stern and holding the tillers, turns and directs the ship: but his eyes and mind are turned toward the ether, looking to the heights." The same Numenius says in his book *On the Good*: "For if the Creator God is the principle of generation, it is necessary that the Good be, by proportion, the principle of essence. The Creator God is the imitator of this Good by proportion. Indeed, generation corresponds to substance as its image and imitation. If, therefore, the Second Mind is the creator of generation, the Good will be the creator of essence itself, the good innate in essence by itself. For the Second, being double, by himself makes both his own Idea and the world, since he is the Creator. Therefore, the First God is the Good by himself; the Creator, the imitator of this one, is the good." Numenius says all these things, somewhat obscurely indeed because of the obscure subject matter and the manner of speaking used by philosophers. We have seen many excellent things: the Father and the Son, the first and second Mind, by which the sayings of the Chaldeans <note>The Chaldean Oracles, often cited by Steuchus as ancient wisdom</note> are confirmed and become clear: the first Mind as the procreator and parent of the second; the second as the maker and creator of the world, imitating the first. We have seen the Father called the Good by himself, and the Son called the Mind, the creator of the world and the imitator of the Father; the Father being free from all labor, while the Creator and King extends through all things. In the Gospel of John, the same Mind says: *The Father judges no one, but <margin>D</margin> has given all judgment to the Son.* <note>John 5:22</note> Plato says that men believe in this Creator, but are ignorant of the Father. Men evidently believed and perceived, as Plato witnesses, the Son of God—that is, the Creator of the world—but the Father was unknown. It appears, therefore, that our Theology has flourished in all ages. Indeed, through the testimony of Numenius, it is perceived to be most true that the ancients wrote and handed down the things we marveled at above. For these things and those are the same. From these sayings of Numenius, a certain mystery <note>original: *sacramentum*; a sacred secret or doctrine</note> is opened which is also very frequently mentioned in our Theology: that he who is recorded in Moses and the Prophets as the creator of heaven and earth, the fashioner of man, the lawgiver, the governor and ruler of the human race, and who of old spoke with the fathers, appeared to them, and sent Angels to them, was the Son. For the Father, say Plato and Numenius, was more obscure. And mortals were deceived, believing that the first and most eminent principle was the author of the world and the human race. It was, they say, the Son and the Second Mind, upon whom the Father conferred all divinity. And for that reason, he calls him his Word <note>original: *Verbum*</note>, that is, his speech, authority, mission, and magistracy. The Father was more obscure, and unless the Son had revealed him—whether through oracles and prophets, or through succession, or most recently by his own presence—mortals could never have brought him to mind. The Father, as the supreme, is at rest; the Son is at work. From this, everyone will notice that the Theology of Philo, Numenius, and Plotinus is the same, as if one had learned from the other.

->*lius brought forth the testimony of John: and that Heraclitus also*<-
->*had spoken concerning the Word.*<-

->Amelius<-

# ON THE PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY.

<sig>A</sig> <image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'A' featuring a seated figure, likely a scholar or shepherd with a staff, in a landscape with architectural ruins or classical buildings in the background.</image-desc> AMELIUS <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher of the 3rd century AD, a student of Plotinus.</note> was himself not undistinguished among the Platonists, and is a most eminent and noble witness of all those things which have preceded. The truth of these matters, which we discuss in this work, is all the more irrefragable because it has been strengthened through a succession of men and centuries, proclaimed and confirmed by every age. For besides what everyone had said above, there follows, even with the testimony of Amelius, the testimony of Heraclitus <note>A pre-Socratic Greek philosopher known for his doctrines on change and the Logos.</note> concerning the Word of God. For he speaks thus:

-> "And this then was the Word <note>original Greek: *Logos*</note>, according to which, being eternal, all things that are made were made, as Heraclitus would also maintain; and by Zeus, whom that Barbarian <note>Amelius refers to the Apostle John as "the Barbarian" because he was Hebrew, not Greek.</note> maintains, standing in the order and the disorder of the beginning, to be with God, and to be God, through whom absolutely all things have been made; in whom that which was made, lived, and was life and Being; and having fallen into bodies and put on flesh, he appeared as a man, while at the same time showing the majesty of his nature; and indeed, having been released, he was again deified and was God, such as he was before he was brought down into the body and the flesh and the man." <-

And this certainly, says Amelius, was the Word through which, existing forever, those things which are were made, just as Heraclitus thinks; and by Jove, which that Barbarian considers the Word standing in the order and confusion of the beginning, to have been with God, and to have been God, through whom all things in the universe were made: in whom that which was made was living, and life, and Being: then having fallen into bodies and put on flesh, he appeared as a man, showing even then the greatness of his nature. And being entirely released, he was again deified. And he stood forth as God, just as he had been before he had descended into the body, and the flesh, and the man. These things says Amelius, a man indeed foreign to the Christian religion, but who both held the principles of Christian wisdom as known and approved of them. In this, he followed not only his ancestors and the Greek philosophers, but also our own, especially Saint John, whom he calls a "Barbarian" because he was a Hebrew. He asserts that John's entire Gospel—in which he philosophizes concerning the eternity of the Word, the creation of things through Him, and His descent into the flesh—is true.

<sig>B</sig> He says that in the flesh He demonstrated the greatness of His divinity, and lost nothing of His majesty: He created all things in order and confusion. By "order" he means the created world itself; by "confusion" <note>original Greek: *ataxia*</note>, that is, disorder, he means Chaos itself, which preceded order, as is found in Plato. This Word was present, says Amelius, when order was made out of disorder. For He Himself was the cause, He Himself the governor. He was in that beginning: and He was the mediator between order and confusion: and even then He was with God, and was also Himself God. Amelius marvels at all these things found in the "Barbarian," he praises and approves them, exclaiming and swearing by Jove that this is that very Word through which all things were made. 

Listen, I pray you all: for this discourse is not about a trivial matter. With Amelius as author and witness, that Word of which Plato, and before him Heraclitus, had spoken, is that Word through which all things were created; and concerning which John wrote in the beginning of his Gospel—whom Amelius calls a Barbarian, that is, not a Greek, but as if an Egyptian, Chaldean, or Hebrew. Just as Plato says that philosophy came from the Barbarians to the Greeks. For they were accustomed to call all who were not Greeks "Barbarians." See the harmony of all ancient and new philosophy! Not I, but the philosophers themselves acting as mediators and judges, assert that the Word celebrated by the Chaldeans, Hermes Trismegistus <note>referred to here as "Mercurio"</note>, Orpheus, Heraclitus, and Plato, is that through which all things are created, and which afterwards, having assumed flesh, was seen as a man. Nor was His divinity diminished in Him, but it appeared wonderful in Him, and of such a kind and greatness as it once was. Therefore, the old and the new Theology join right hands and kiss one another; though separated by intervals of centuries, they return, by the authority of the philosophers themselves, to an embrace and are mutually joined. And by the hands of the philosophers, Truth is led into her sanctuary and home: whose certain and true home is this most recent arrival of His, to which all things correspond. O blessed times, in which this truth, this most manifest Theology, has shone forth from heaven—

It argues for a "Perennial Philosophy" where ancient pagan wisdom and Christian revelation are in perfect harmony regarding the creation and the incarnation.</summary>

It met their eyes, but because it was not yet as luminous as it would later become, nor bursting forth entirely with the full flood of its own light, it left their deceived eyes once more in a certain darkness. Therefore, the ancient and the new Theology are one and the same; nor do we worship any other God as the author and prince of the human race than they did. Indeed, even Plotinus <note>A major Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 204–270 AD) whom Steuco views as a bridge between Greek thought and Christian truth.</note> affirms that these discourses now held concerning these great principles—concerning the Word <note>original: "Verbo"; referring to the Logos</note>, the Image of the Highest Good, the First Cause, and the Creator <note>original: "Opifice"; the Craftsman or Demiurge</note>—are not new, but are of the greatest antiquity. He suggests that what is said by us now are, as it were, interpretations of those ancient things. He spoke most truly indeed. For this newest Theology has torn back the veil, revealed all things that lay hidden, and dispelled every darkness.

Therefore, since these matters are manifest not only by our own preaching and profession, but are also proven by the testimony of the Philosophers themselves, I do not see what separates Philosophy from Theology. For we cannot—if we wish to be fair judges—separate even Aristotle from the company of these men, whom you will often find admiring the Theology of his ancestors. I shall leave that judgment to the most learned men.

I myself shall declare this in part, wherever the subject and necessity demand.
