# THE WRITINGS OF AGOSTINO STEUCO OF GUBBIO
<margin>A</margin>

## ON PERENNIAL PHILOSOPHY, BOOK TWO.

### On the Theology of the ancients: the testimony of Aristotle, and examples from others showing how much it is to be venerated and respected.

#### CHAPTER I.

I am about to write in this second book concerning the three Names, or hypostases <note>The Greek term *hypostasis* refers to the distinct persons of the Trinity—Father, Son, and Holy Spirit—within one divine substance.</note>, and the entire Divinity, following the method of my previous demonstrations. Before I do so, it seemed best first to hear Aristotle speaking on the Philosophy of the ancestors, so that what precedes and what follows may be confirmed and brought to light. For even if he saw—or pretended not to have seen—those books which Plato knew (namely those of Hermes Trismegistus <note>original: "Mercurij"</note>, the Sibyls—for Plato mentions the Sibyl in the *Phaedrus*—and also Orpheus, and the others whom Plato knew and whose doctrine he so admired that he followed them: I mean Anaxagoras, Heraclitus, Pythagoras, Empedocles, and Parmenides, and the rest), nevertheless, by his testimony it can appear that those ancients said many noble and wonderful things. Whether they are categorized as Theologians or Philosophers, many things flowed from them which testify to a certain great wisdom of the earlier ages. 

Aristotle, therefore, when discussing the principles of things in his books on First Philosophy <note>Now commonly known as the *Metaphysics*.</note>, wrote these things: that it was handed down to posterity by those most ancient and primitive men, under the cloak of fables, that these very ones whom we have mentioned are Gods, and that the divine nature rules and contains the entire nature of things. He wrote that the rest was wrapped in fables for the sake of being believable, for the sake of observing laws, and for utility, by those who fashioned Gods similar to men and other living creatures, and added many things of that kind to them. 

Regarding these matters, if one separates the better part—namely, that which they handed down: that the Gods are the first and principal substances—one should judge that they spoke divinely. And it is likely that often every art and philosophy, having been perfectly discovered, was again extinguished and destroyed; and that these opinions and decrees of theirs have reached down to these times like remnants. Therefore, the ancestral opinion concerning the first substances has been clear to us until now. 

These are the words of Aristotle in his books on First Philosophy concerning the wisdom of the ancestors descending down to his own times, but mangled, and of which only remnants, as it were, flowed down to his age. Likewise, he says that every art and discipline flourished in ancient ages, but having been extinguished by the injuries of time, did not reach posterity except in a mangled state; yet a great and divine body of it existed among the ancestors. Those ancients believed and asserted that God—that is, the *theion* <note>original: "θεῖον", meaning the divine or the divinity</note>, the divinity itself and the power—contains the whole of nature within its embrace, and that they said this by a certain divine inspiration. Aristotle affirms these things; and if I were able, I would pray him to weigh his own words and diligently consider what the ancestors handed down concerning God. If he praises them because they believed that God, embracing all nature, sustains it, he must also necessarily admire the wonderful, sublime, and profound things they preached concerning Him. And if Aristotle thinks that Philosophy once flourished whole and perfect, of which only rem-

<sig>k 2</sig> <meta>continuity: because</meta>

<summary>Steuco introduces Book Two by citing Aristotle's "Metaphysics" to argue that ancient wisdom was once a complete, divine truth that has survived only in fragments and fables. He asserts that the earliest thinkers understood the divine nature through inspiration before their knowledge was "mangled" by time.</summary>
<keywords>Perennial Philosophy, Aristotle, Metaphysics, Ancient Theology, Trinity, Hypostasis, Hermes Trismegistus, Plato, Divine Inspiration, Lost Wisdom</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>The author continues his defense of "Perennial Philosophy," arguing that even Aristotle, despite his reputation for innovation, relied upon and respected the ancient wisdom of the "theologians" who preceded him.</meta>

# AUGUSTINUS EUGUBINUS, BOOK II.

<margin>C</margin>
I would ask again and again of those who come after us, that they do not despise or look down upon me as I collect these remains and assert from them—just as he did—that a certain greater and divine Wisdom flourished among the ancients. Certainly, one should not do this unless they are at odds with themselves. For I admire and affirm, right along with him <note>referring to Aristotle</note>, a certain divine and perfect Philosophy held by the ancients; I proceed just as he did, through certain conjectures and remains, as it were, carried down to us. For if Aristotle praises and admires that part of the ancestors' Philosophy which confessed God as the ruler of things, he will also admire certain other things which he had either seen or suspected. Indeed, no adversary remains if Aristotle also admires and commends the ancestors, whose wisdom he affirms was wrapped in fables. Let him see, therefore, and consider what the truth compelled him to confess. For when he sought God and the principles of things, and his reasoning had reached the highest point, he struck—as if against a reef which perhaps all Philosophy had resolved to avoid—the Theology of the ancients, nor did he have anything further to say about God than what they had established. For he saw that nothing else could be said. Therefore, if he established the same first principles as the ancients, he must necessarily also accept those things which they discussed regarding principles and the whole of Philosophy: the immortality of souls, the creation of things, and providence. Furthermore, many things must be asserted by him who understands and confesses that the philosophy of his predecessors is worthy of approval and admiration. Moreover, these lesser matters are contained within those greater ones. Behold, by his own voice, Aristotle is compelled to assert both the creation of the world and other things which were sung in the Theology of the ancestors. For everyone previously confessed the creation of things; and that is the ancient Theology so greatly commended by Aristotle. However, John Philoponus <note>A 6th-century Christian philosopher and commentator on Aristotle</note> gave the reason why, since there are three kinds of Philosophy—divine, mathematical, and physical—Aristotle followed the last most closely and left behind the things copiously treated by his predecessors, choosing instead the physical <margin>D</margin> in which few had labored up to that time, so that he might show the strength of his own genius. From this it becomes clear that Aristotle did not despise the Theology of the ancestors because they did not treat the same things as he, but, as far as one can guess, he even valued it highly. It has also been detected and revealed by most learned men that he either pretended not to understand many of the opinions of the ancients, or distorted them by interpreting them wrongly, not presenting them in the sense intended by their authors. For he twisted everything toward the first Philosophy he was laboring to build; since he wished to place its principles as unshaken and firm—chief among which is the eternity of the heavens—he adapted many things taken from the ancients to his own discoveries. Of these I shall propose only two examples. For when the ancestors had called the sky "ether" from <term>aithein</term> <note>original: "αιθειν"</note>, meaning "I burn," because they thought the sky was a fiery body, he distorted it to fit his own dogma (that is, eternity) so that he said the ancients gave this name to the sky because it is "always running," from <term>aei thein</term> <note>original: "αει θειν"</note>. Again, when those same ancients had separated "eternity" and "time" by a clear and manifest distinction, calling eternity the eternal life of God, and time a small part or image of eternity, Aristotle so contaminated time with eternity that he said eternity was the space of celestial motion, encompassing infinite time. And what the ancestors made the age of God, he made the age of the heavens, although they neither mentioned eternity in connection with the motion of the heavens, nor did they adopt time when mentioning eternity, as has been shown in its proper place. And he himself attributed everlasting eternity to God in his *Metaphysics*. But as we unroll his entire Philosophy, we detect many sparks of divine light from which it can be proved—and shown to men who are not entirely harsh, wild, and stubborn in his decrees—that his Philosophy is the same as that of his ancestors, I mean regarding divine matters. Let us now set him aside, putting off further conversation with him to another more suitable time.

<summary>The author argues that Aristotle's philosophy is fundamentally rooted in ancient "divine" theology, despite Aristotle's attempts to rebrand or distort ancient terms like 'ether' and 'eternity' to suit his own physical theories.</summary>
